> "The conclusions reached in this work are the result of considerable research into the significance of the Hermetic <note>Relating to Hermes Trismegistus and the ancient tradition of alchemy and magic.</note> and Mystical Symbolisms contained in alchemical literature. Regarding fundamental doctrine, they offer a reasonable harmony between the transcendental teachings of Eastern and Western Theosophy <note>Theosophy refers to systems of esoteric philosophy seeking direct knowledge of the divine.</note>."

> "The lessons these stories are intended to convey are unmistakable, but not so intrusive as to weary even those of tender years. <note>The diagram illustrates ten attributes or emanations (Sephirot) through which the Infinite reveals itself. They are organized into three pillars: the Left (feminine/restrictive), the Right (masculine/expansive), and the Center (balancing). Daath is often considered a "hidden" Sephirah, representing the bridge between the higher intellect and the lower emotions.</note>

# PREFACE

Few educated persons, and certainly none belonging to the class of students for which this work is more especially designed, will need to be told that the Kabalah is a form of esoteric philosophy, that it makes a high claim for itself, or that this claim has, from time to time, been admitted by persons who are entitled to our consideration. Nor will it be necessary to state that the literature called Kabalistic arose among the Jews during the Christian centuries that followed their dispersal and the destruction of their Holy City. It offers a strong contrast to the sacred scriptures of Israel, which are direct, beautiful, and simple, while Kabalism is complex, obscure, and in many ways repellent in its outward form. The Bible is in focus with humanity; the Kabalah is distorted out of all correspondence with the simple senses, and we must grind our intellectual lenses with exceeding care if we would bring it into perspective.

From whatever point of view it may be approached, however, the Kabalah is of importance: it connects with literatures that are greater than itself and with significant issues of history. It is part of the history of philosophy, and as such it once entered into the thought of Europe. Broadly speaking, it is responsible for all that strange web of symbolism and ceremony which made up the magic of the Middle Ages; at a later period it sought to transform alchemy; it influenced many of those conventional practices and beliefs which we term super-

<summary>This preface defines the Kabalah as an esoteric Jewish philosophy and notes its historical importance and influence on European thought, medieval magic, and alchemy.</summary>
<keywords>Kabalah, Kabalism, esoteric philosophy, Jewish history, Middle Ages, magic, alchemy</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the Preface, transitioning from the discussion of the Kabalah's influence on medieval superstitions to the author's specific goals and methods for this book.</meta>

# Preface

stition generically, and the form in which we know them is therefore chiefly a Kabalistic form. If we might dare to suppose for a moment that behind magic, behind alchemy, and behind astrology there is any mystery of secret and real knowledge, then it is entitled to special respect because, according to the hypothesis of some of its defenders, it is through this seemingly impenetrable literature that the road to the secret lies.

A comprehensive account of the Kabalah—primarily bibliographical and historical, but seeking to establish its connections with other forms of occult philosophy and to determine its influence and importance from more than one standpoint—is the design of the present work. In this work, special regard has also been paid to the limitations and requirements of English readers—in other words, those unacquainted with the languages, ancient and modern, in which Kabalistic literature has been, with few exceptions, available until now. The subject is exceedingly abstruse and has been presented by some of its interpreters in a highly technical fashion; in this case, no prior knowledge is assumed of the reader, and hence all technicalities have been avoided, while the scope of the project much exceeds the one or two attempts—mostly in foreign languages—which have been made to simplify the study of the Kabalah. It is the result of an inquiry undertaken, in the first instance, for my own personal requirements; and I must add, in fairness to my readers and in gratitude to early laborers in this obscure field of research, that the overwhelming difficulties of the Hebrew and Aramaic originals have been simplified by referring to the vast storehouses of Rabbinical lore buried for some centuries in such Latin collections as those of Buxtorf, Bartolocci,

<summary>The author explains that this work aims to provide a comprehensive, non-technical history of the Kabalah for English readers who lack access to the original Hebrew or Aramaic texts. He notes that his research was aided by massive Latin collections of Rabbinical lore compiled by scholars like Buxtorf and Bartolocci.</summary>
<keywords>occult philosophy, Kabalah research, English readers, Hebrew, Aramaic, Rabbinical lore, Johannes Buxtorf, Giulio Bartolocci, bibliographical history</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's discussion of sources, moving from Latin collections to modern English, French, and German scholars, while clarifying the book's purpose and intended audience.</meta>

<page-num>xi.</page-num>
<header>Preface</header>

Pistorius and Rosenroth; indeed, a very large proportion of the materials necessary for an intelligent familiarity with Jewish esoteric tradition are found in these. These and other resources have been subsequently strengthened by an extensive range of reading among modern writers in England, France, and Germany who, directly and indirectly, have concerned themselves with the subject. This work, therefore, has the learner's advantage of being without pretense and without ambition; however, it is also believed to contain all that the ordinary student is likely to require in order to appreciate at their proper worth the various claims put forward on behalf of the Kabalah by those who take it seriously today. It is necessary to add that it has been written by a transcendentalist and chiefly for the use of transcendentalists; while offering materials for their judgment, it also indicates the lines of the conclusions toward which the writer himself leans and seeks to support some of them. It has been preceded in England by only two books dealing directly with the subject; one is the brief but significant essay of Dr. Ginsburg,\* which is critical rather than descriptive and is, on the whole, hostile in its tendency. Moreover, it has long been out of print. The other is *The Kabbalah Unveiled* † by Mr. S. L. MacGregor Mathers, which is largely translation and commentary, and, in addition to other limitations, therefore covers only

---

<note>\* "The Kabbalah." By C. D. Ginsburg. London, 1865. Octavo.</note>
<note>† "The Kabbalah Unveiled, containing the following books of the Zohar: 1. The Book of Concealed Mystery; 2. The Greater Holy Assembly; 3. The Lesser Holy Assembly. Translated into English from the Latin version of Knorr von Rosenroth and compared with the original Chaldean and Hebrew text." By S. L. MacGregor Mathers, London, 1887. Octavo. The commentary is partly that of Rosenroth and partly the work of the translator.</note>

<summary>The author describes his sources, including Latin collections and modern European scholars, and states that his work is intended to help students and transcendentalists evaluate modern claims about the Kabalah. He notes that the only prior English works on the subject are a hostile essay by Dr. Ginsburg and a limited translation by S. L. MacGregor Mathers.</summary>

<keywords>Pistorius, Rosenroth, Jewish esoteric tradition, Kabalah, Ginsburg, MacGregor Mathers, Zohar, Knorr von Rosenroth, Kabbalah Unveiled, transcendentalism, occult bibliography</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's assessment of existing literature on the Kabbalah, specifically noting that previous works have only covered a fraction of the available material. It then begins to categorize the primary sources of the Jewish esoteric tradition.</meta>

<page-num>xii.</page-num>
<header>Preface</header>

a small portion of an extensive literature. The present comprehensive account fulfills a distinct purpose and may, it is hoped, be held to occupy—not altogether unworthily—a vacant place from which there is a wide prospect. This view is by no means lacking in importance for those who are not transcendentalists, for the student of philosophy and history, and for those curious about branches of literature which the elder D'Israeli, despite the bias of his birthright, declined to enter.

The works containing the esoteric tradition of Israel, as distinguished from the exoteric tradition embodied in the Talmud and its related texts, fall under two headings:

*a.* The Sepher Yetzirah, or "Book of Formation."
*b.* The Zohar, or "Splendor."

Connected with the "Book of Formation" are its commentaries, the most prominent among which are those of Saadya Gaon, Rabbi Azariel Ben Menahem, and Rabbi Abraham Ben Dior. The treatise itself consists of only a few pages.

The Zohar proper is a commentary on the five books of Moses,<note>This, at least, is its conventional description; Graetz, the German historian, seems uncertain about its correctness but does not offer an alternative. It is actually a theosophical medley connected with the Pentateuch and arising from it.</note> but a number of distinct treatises are connected with or embedded within it. There are also supplements and additions which must be considered, to some extent, separate from the original collection.

Furthermore, certain important developments and commentaries are connected with the Zohar, some of which are included in the *Kabbala Denudata* of Rosenroth. They fall under two headings:

1. Those which deal with the subject matter of

<column-break/>

<summary>The author identifies the Sepher Yetzirah and the Zohar as the two primary pillars of the Jewish esoteric tradition, distinguishing them from the exoteric Talmud. He also notes the various commentaries and supplementary treatises associated with these foundational texts.</summary>

<keywords>Sepher Yetzirah, Zohar, Talmud, Saadya Gaon, Azariel Ben Menahem, Abraham Ben Dior, Kabbala Denudata, Rosenroth, Graetz, Pentateuch, esoteric tradition, exoteric tradition, Book of Formation</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the classification of Zoharistic commentaries and other Kabbalistic texts, concluding the list of works that fall under the heading of commentaries and moving on to other secondary sources like the Sepher Sephiroth and Æsh Metzareph.</meta>

<page-num>xiii.</page-num>
<header>Preface</header>

the work and are designed to clarify certain of its most obscure treatises.

2. Those which explain and expand the doctrines of Pneumatology that are established in the Zohar.

These commentaries are, of course, the work of later Kabbalists whose dates can be determined with more or less certainty. Such Zoharistic writings have sometimes been confused with the Zohar.<note>Thus in *The Kabbalah Unveiled*, Mr. Mathers mentions the "House of God," or "The Elohim," and the "Book of the Revolutions of Souls" among the most important sections of the Zohar. (Introduction, p. 15.)</note>

Some classifications of the Kabbalah also include and give prominence to:

*c.* The *Sepher Sephiroth*—that is, the "Book of Numerations" or "Emanations."

*d.* A treatise titled *Æsh Metzareph*, or "Purifying Fire."

Whatever their authority and importance, there is no justification for placing these works among the great classics of the Kabbalah. The "Book of Emanations" is more accurately categorized among the dependencies of the *Sepher Yetzirah*, while the "Purifying Fire" occupies a unique position, as it has almost no history and is perhaps the only Kabbalistic treatise that deals directly with alchemy. Its date is uncertain and its authorship is completely unknown.

A brief but thorough account of all the works mentioned above will be found in Books IV, V, and VI; a formal categorization like this has not been attempted before in the English language. The first three books contain a historical and critical survey of the entire subject,

The author notes that a formal English-language tabulation of these works is provided later in the book.</summary>

# Preface

showing the philosophical system embodied in the Kabbalah, the method by which it was developed, its connections with previous theosophies—which were designed, like itself, to explain the fundamental mysteries of the universe—and the evidence for its antiquity.

The seventh book is devoted to Christian students of the Kabbalah and collects for the first time the opinions of the major Christian scholars whose names have been connected with Kabbalism. The purpose of this book is to show a consensus of opinion among all those authorities who are most popular with occultists regarding what is valuable in Jewish esoteric traditions and what is useless material. On this point, there has never been any real difference of opinion, except within the modern school of transcendentalism. This school, lacking a proper standard of judgment and viewing the entire subject from a new perspective, has been inclined to accept everything indiscriminately as part of the genuine tradition; it has thus valued the methods of *Gematria* and *Notaricon* as no less important and mysterious than the Zoharistic philosophy of the unconditioned, summarized in *Ain Soph*.

The eighth book deals briefly with other channels through which the esoteric tradition is believed to have been preserved in the West and their connection with the path of Kabbalism.

To those who may approach this work from a historical and bibliographical perspective, the presence of its main theme and its appeal to a specific class of students may require some explanation. It has, I believe, been suggested that since the appearance of Dr. Ginsburg’s destructive criticism, there has been no

# Preface

Among mere scholars—that is to say, within the realm of research which has only a scholarly horizon—there was no interest worthy of special mention in the period immediately preceding that work, and it is quite true that there has been just as little since its publication, though not primarily because of Dr. Ginsburg’s criticism. There has always been—or, more precisely, since the days of Thomas Vaughan, Cudworth, and the Cambridge Platonists—a certain class of thinkers for whom the claims made by and on behalf of the Kabbalah have held great interest and importance, and this group is now much larger than at any time before 1865. It also constitutes, intellectually, a more respectable and significant body than the academic reader might be inclined to imagine, especially if they are unfamiliar with the literature representing it. One is forced to refer to these thinkers by the general term "occult students," though the phrase is somewhat imprecise and has been used to describe people who deserve little serious consideration. A forbidden way of thinking is here, as in other cases, unfairly identified with the lesser minds that follow it, and an unpopular subject is judged by the intellectual debris that has accumulated around it. But the group I have mentioned does not deserve ridicule or contempt in itself; it consists of those who believe in the continuation of a secret religious—or, more accurately, theosophical—tradition from an early period of human history, and this is not clearly an unjustified conclusion to draw from a philosophical study of religions. Now,

xvi.

# Preface

The Kabbalah is not only, as I have said, the occult philosophy of Judaism; it is not only one of the primary sources from which occult science and philosophy have drawn in the Western world, but it has also been portrayed as the channel of the tradition I have just mentioned. It is, therefore, not merely reasonable to suppose, but it is a fact, that an inquiry like the present must appeal to occultists, and almost exclusively to them. Other interests are incidental; their interest is vital. To determine the claims of the Kabbalah as a department and inspiring center of occult philosophy is to determine what is of the greatest importance regarding it.

It is for this reason that I have been led to consider the Kabbalah—not, indeed, as an occultist in the accepted sense, but from the occult standpoint—and to return frequently to the belief in a secret religious doctrine, as well as to other related questions that need to be reconsidered by those who hold such beliefs. If, during this inquiry, I have had to relegate certain illusions to their proper place in the realm of the fantastic—and especially if I have reduced the sphere of what is called Mysticism to its proper dimensions—I trust that my intentions will be justified by those I have sought to set straight. By its nature, the foundation of Mysticism cannot lie in occult science or occult philosophy, while it is only on the historical side that it connects with any past traditions, whether popular or achromatic.*

---

<note>* The reason is indicated by Schopenhauer, when he remarks on the astonishing unanimity of mystics in all ages. They are unlike in everything except those principles that constitute mysticism, yet they do not hold those principles as a sect clings to its tenets, for they are not and can never be a sect.</note>

<summary>The author explains that this study of the Kabbalah is written from an occult standpoint because occultists are the only ones with a vital interest in the subject. He notes that while the work may challenge certain mystical illusions, it aims to define the proper historical and philosophical boundaries of Mysticism.</summary>
<keywords>Kabbalah, occult philosophy, Judaism, Mysticism, Schopenhauer, secret doctrine, esoteric tradition</keywords>

xvii.

<header>Preface</header>

In the course of the general inquiry, I have endeavored to clarify the most curious <term>Kabbalistic</term> teachings on the subject of the soul, its origin and destiny, the primal state of man, the life of the world to come, and the Messianic dream of Jewry.

I should add that this study is mainly concerned with the documents, and as such, it cannot deal exhaustively with all the issues. It is also bibliographical rather than philosophical. An exhaustive study of the <term>Kabbalah</term> would have to be made in connection with that of comparative religion, demanding qualifications to which I can make no claim and a space for its development which would have been, under any circumstances, impossible. I wish my inquiry to be regarded rather as the organized notebook of a student. Finally, the work has been performed in a spirit of impartiality, and this is perhaps the only way in which it leans toward perfection. There has been no interest at stake which I have felt myself compelled to defend, and it is therefore free from the animus of extreme hostile criticism, as well as from the unreasoned assumptions of those—mostly non-critical believers—who still have the cause of the <term>Kabbalah</term> at heart. I claim, furthermore, that I have considered honestly and sympathetically all the interests at stake, including those which the bias of modern scholarship is inclined to overlook, or to dismiss in an intolerant spirit.

I should add that the history of this book has been checkered and somewhat unfortunate. The manuscript was completed for the press at the end of 1898, and in the following year, the greater part had been typeset and a number of the sheets printed, which were completely destroyed in a fire at the printer’s.

I note with satisfaction that there are many debated points on which we have reached the same conclusion, despite working independently. <term>Dr. Karppe’s</term> study is designed for the scholar and the philosopher, while my own, as previously explained, is intended—at least primarily—for the <term>occult student</term>. He presents the Jewish mysticism that led up to and preceded the <term>Zohar</term> very thoroughly, but he says nothing about the influence that work exerted or its subsequent history. Conversely, the plan of my own treatise has necessarily led me to skim over <term>pre-Zoharic theosophy</term>—including <term>Saadyah</term>, <term>Ibn Gebirol</term>, <term>Judah Ha Levi</term>, <term>Aben Ezra</term>, <term>Maimonides</term>, and others—because they did not significantly affect European occult thought. To this exception may be added certain points of critical importance to which I have personally only had occasion to refer in passing. The student will also notice a tendency in certain instances to overlook questions of criticism as if there had never been a dispute regarding them: on the one hand, the commentary of <term>Hay Gaon</term> is used as if no scholar had challenged its authenticity; and, on the other, the late date of the *Bahir* is taken for granted. Criticism may not have said its last word on either subject, but Dr. Karppe ignores the criticism.

The limits of the present work—which, regarding the original intention, have already been exceeded—have necessitated the omission of a number of subsidiary yet not unimportant matters. I will mention only two: certain developments of <term>pneumatology</term> <note>the study of spirits or the soul</note> and <term>eschatology</term> <note>the study of final things, such as death and the afterlife</note> which are representative of the doctrinal or systematic part—let us say, the hells of <term>Kabbalism</term>; and certain historical consequences, such as the mission of <term>Sabbatai Zevi</term>, the <term>Neo-Pietists</term>, and the <term>Zoharists</term>. But in each case, these omissions are without real consequence for the occult student.

<sig>B</sig>

# BOOK I

## POST-CHRISTIAN LITERATURE
## OF THE JEWS

->*ARGUMENT*<-

The literature of <term>Kabbalism</term> has a philosophical, historical and, within certain limits, an interpretive interest for ordinary students, but it has a living interest only for those who believe: (*a*) that a secret religious tradition has been preserved from the early ages of history, and (*b*) that Kabbalistic literature has been one of its vehicles. Such persons are here called occult students. The purpose of the present book is to show that, whatever the value of the first view (*a*), imperfect investigation has placed the second (*b*) in a false position. The occult estimate of the <term>Talmud</term> is cited as a case in point, and the divisions of the <term>Kabbalah</term> are distinguished to make clear the issues and to correct some other misconceptions.

### I. INTRODUCTORY

THE interpretation of the exile placed by the "princes of the exile" upon the sacred oracles of ancient Israel cannot be dismissed as unimportant. From the period of the dispersal of the Jews after the destruction of Jerusalem by <term>Vespasian</term> down even to our own times, Hebrew literature has developed in many of the chief centers of Europe, but outside the scattered remnant of the children of the covenant it has remained largely unknown. Many persons, not otherwise ill-informed, would be astonished to discover that as far back as the

end of the seventeenth century, there were nearly four thousand works* written in the Hebrew language which were individually known and quoted by one authority on rabbinical bibliography, namely, Julius Bartolocci, of the Reformed Order of St. Bernard.† Almost every conceivable department of human learning and intellectual activity is represented in this literature,‡ which, in secular matters as in sacred ones, has the seal of Israel's sanctity upon all its pages. It is otherwise an extremely curious and, in some respects, profound literature, which translation has done little to make known, and which is represented quite incompletely even in the great and authoritative textbooks of Hebrew history. There is no need to add that its size and its difficulties make it a formidable subject to approach. It is, indeed, an undiscovered country, still awaiting its Columbus;§ a land full of wealth and mystery, of strange shrines and sanctuaries shining weirdly far away through the darkness of our ignorance with a light which might well be a reflection of the <term>Shekinah</term>, so foreign does it seem to that which enlightens most people born into the modern world.

<page-num>3</page-num>
# Post-Christian Literature of the Jews

Within this literature, there is, so to speak, another and stranger literature included. Reports of it have been among us for several centuries, and to a certain extent, it must be admitted that it is known to some of us, primarily because it has been made available by the pioneers of bibliographical scholarship—the Latin-writing scholars of the past. This storehouse of Hebrew theosophy, for that is what it is, has exerted a unique fascination on many great minds of Christendom, and its non-Jewish students, even today, may for all we know be as numerous as its Jewish followers. It is called the <term>Kabalah</term>, a term for which more than one explanation has been suggested by those who create imaginative etymologies. For example, the word has been derived from the name of the Hindu teacher Kapila,* to whom a philosophy of numbers is attributed, seemingly on the weak grounds that one branch of Kabalistic literature also deals extensively with this subject. Another equally fanciful suggestion makes the term an analogue of Cybele,† the mythological Queen of Heaven, who is thus connected with the Jewish personification of Wisdom in a female aspect. Regarding the true derivation, there is no room for uncertainty; it possesses that simplicity which is so often the seal of truth in matters of language, as it has been said to be in

---

<note>* Mr. C. W. Heckethorn has recently made himself responsible for this view in the new and enlarged edition of his very uneven work, "Secret Societies of all Ages." See vol. i. p. 83.</note>
<note>† Responsibility in this case rests with the late Edward Vaughan Kenealy, whose anonymous "Book of God" and its sequels are frequently quoted as an authority by occult writers. Its philology belongs to the period of Godfrey Higgins, the author of "Nimrod," and Bryant's "Ancient Mythology." See Kenealy's "Introduction to the Apocalypse of Adam-Oannes," p. 613.</note>

It is unnecessary to say that a literature of such considerable scope encompasses many other subjects, but these are the main points of the instruction as I find them set forth in an excerpt from a Latin letter in the collection of Baron von Rosenroth.<note>*Kabbala Denudata, or the Transcendental and Metaphysical Doctrine of the Hebrews*, vol. i. *Apparatus to the Book of Zohar, part two*, pp. 3-5.</note> The Kabalah, in a word, is the hidden thought of Israel regarding the doctrines of the Jewish religion—which are in most cases Christian doctrines—and regarding the proper understanding of that Written Word which is attributed to a divine origin in both Christendom and Jewry. It is therefore obvious that, in a general sense, it may be expected to shed some kind of light upon the problems of the Christian faith; but its Christian interpreters have argued that it does this also in a more specific way: that the New Testament and the writings of the early Church Fathers were derived not only from the inspired records of the first covenant, but from the interpretation placed on those records by this esoteric tradition.<note>† "It is apparent from the many similarities in this Kabbalistic philosophy to the doctrines in the New Testament and early Patristic literature that both of the latter most probably have had a common germ and origin in the esoteric teachings of the Israelites, as well as in the more open and exoteric teachings of the Hebrew Holy Writings." Isaac Myer, LL.B., "The Philosophical Writings of Solomon Ben Yehudah Ibn Gabirol," Philadelphia, 1888, 8vo., p. 7. The letter of St. Jerome to Paulinus, which dwells consecutively upon the mysteries contained in all the books of the Old and New Testaments, has been sometimes regarded as a case in point.</note>

through which it was communicated to chosen ministers in the angelic world; and it was imparted by divine revelation to Adam in his unfallen state. When he fell from that pure condition, he lost this precious legacy, but it was subsequently restored to him so that he might return toward perfection. It was handed down through Noah to Abraham, and from there it was passed to Moses. It is the hidden meaning of the Mosaic Pentateuch, the secret of which was entrusted by the lawgiver to the seventy elders; from these, in due course, it passed onward to David and was possessed in a special manner by Solomon. It was not written down until after the destruction of the second temple.<note>* The legend has many variations and has been vaguely repeated by many imprecise writers. Quite naturally, it occasionally takes the form of a book delivered to Adam. An old Jewish tradition tells us that this volume was brought by the Angel Razael. Mr. John Yarker, in his “Notes on the Scientific and Religious Mysteries of Antiquity” (London, 1872, p. 21), connects this myth with the pre-Zoharic “Book of Razael”—not the fraud of Ceremonial Magic sometimes referred to by this name, but presumably the ancient legendary Midrash, which was later developed by Eleazar of Worms and reproduced in various forms by degraded Kabbalism. Compare E. V. Kenealy’s mythical account of a Book of the Wisdom of Adam, received in a state of ecstasy and “full of mysteries and signs expressing the most profound knowledge.” See “Book of God,” p. 243. See also *the same source*, p. 273 and following, for a rabbinic account of a staff given to Adam, which is supposed to signify the support of a secret knowledge.</note> A genealogy of this kind will, of course, recall the legendary origins attributed to institutions like Freemasonry, the appeal made by the alchemists to the sages of antiquity, and many other fictions that deserve to be classified as outrageous. We should be careful, however, about labeling an esoteric literature as a fraud because its origin story is mythological; it should be remembered that we are dealing,

Moreover, an oral tradition is particularly prone to the exaggeration of its antiquity, and we must therefore distinguish between the possible fact of its existence in a remote period and the growth of legend surrounding it. We have the testimony of Christ Himself regarding the existence of a tradition in Israel, and we also have His judgment on its value. In one sense, the purpose of this inquiry is to determine whether the later literature of the "reception" <note>The word Kabalah literally means "reception."</note> is entitled to be included in the condemnation of the Divine Rabbi.

## II. THE OCCULT STANDPOINT

As the Kabalah claims to be a tradition long received in secret by one generation from another and at length committed to writing—as one of its classics informs us,<note>The *Sepher Yetzirah* or "Book of Formation," but the statement is probably the explanation of a commentator.</note> because of the poor state of the affairs of Israel, but yet written in a concealed manner<note>Obscurities, complexities, and confusions do not necessarily point to the existence of a double meaning.</note>—it is to be expected that its literary methods will offer difficulties to the ordinary student. Indeed, it has proved so unintelligible on its surface that, on the one hand, it has been considered merely meaningless jargon, while a few who claim to have penetrated to its real sense have, on the other, found pleasure in believing that it is sealed to uninitiated persons, for whom it must always remain a matter of curious and unrewarded research, though not perhaps wanting

some gleams of unexpected suggestion. The first view suggests that more patience and greater effort were needed; the second, that the capacity for painstaking effort is a kind of unique privilege possible only for the few, and this seems unwarranted.

Specialists in <term>cryptography</term> assure us—and we have even higher authority in the testimony of reason itself—that no cipher writing devised by human ingenuity is incapable of being solved by human ingenuity as well. However, this assumption, of course, presupposes good faith in the cipher; it must follow a certain method and conceal a definite meaning. There is also no system of <term>symbolism</term> and no form of philosophical speculation, however complex, that will not reveal its secrets to the searchlight of analysis, provided always that the symbolism is systematic and that the speculation is organized, no matter how intricate its complications. There are cryptic philosophies and concealed metaphysics, just as there is cryptic writing; but if they possess a meaning, it cannot ultimately escape the penetration of the patient and skilled critic. This is subject, however, to the necessary distinction between the meaning of a cipher—which is unmistakable from the moment that it is decoded—and the construction of a speculative hypothesis which, in its minor details, may always be open to debate.<note>The best example of a truly cryptic literature is that concerning <term>Alchemy</term>, and yet it is not cryptic in the sense of cipher-writing. It has a perfectly simple surface meaning; the concealment lies in the significance of certain conventional words and recipes. This is also its greatest difficulty; while cryptography must reveal its secret to skill and patience, it is nearly impossible to say what the word <term>Vitriol</term>, for example, might represent to any specific writer if it is not the ordinary substance known by that name.</note> In regard to <term>occult philosophies</term>, it is usual

But just as cipher writing will surrender its secret to analysis, which is the master-key to all <term>cryptography</term>, so will <term>occult philosophy</term> also disclose its mystery,<note>In most cases this mystery is merely the difficulty of a single meaning.</note> without conventional initiation. Even if we suppose the existence of a "royal road" of this kind, it might be regarded as a labor-saving apparatus which, if accessible, it would not be prudent to set aside.

There are, however, two considerations on the surface of a question like the present which will at once arrest attention. The first is whether <term>occult philosophies</term> are not inherently meaningless, and therefore unable to disclose what, in fact, they do not possess. Alternatively, if they are organized, one must consider whether the mystery which they cover is not out of all proportion to the enormous intellectual cost of unraveling it.<note>I refer here to the unraveling of the literal meaning; the existence of any other is a matter of conjecture.</note> Of these two points, at least one must be determined according to individual preference. For my own part, after spending some years among the strange pathways of human ingenuity, I know certainly that the <term>occult sciences</term> do constitute an organized system which is singularly complex,<note>In the sense that, for example, <term>numerical mysticism</term> runs through all departments of occultism.</note> and since *la science est une noblesse qui oblige* [knowledge is a nobility that carries obligations], I must bear testimony to this fact. I do so even though an imaginative reader may transform the statement and interpret too liberally the narrow concession which I have here made to sincerity. About the second point, it is

extremely difficult to express even a personal opinion. As far as knowledge is its own reward, I suppose that it may be worth its cost; but if any field of research should be excluded from the scope of this truth, it is occult science and philosophy—at least as far as the majority of people are concerned. The labor involved in their exhaustive study rewards those who undertake it in only a few cases. In the pursuit of occult knowledge, Campaspe is never finished. <note>This likely refers to the legendary painting of Campaspe by Apelles, representing a task that can never be truly completed.</note> But it is exactly for this reason that an inquiry like the present one may be welcomed, because it offers an understandable explanation of the issues to everyone interested in this important branch of occult philosophy, saving most of them the trouble of doing their own research.

The importance of the written tradition of <term>Kabalism</term> can be viewed from only two perspectives. First, there is the importance it may have for the sacred scriptures of the Jewish and Christian religions and for the public doctrines that more or less derive from them.<note>From the occult perspective, this is of more consequence than from that of ordinary interpretation. Adolphe Bertet, in his *Apocalypse du bienheureux Jean . . . devoilée* (Paris, 1861, p. 51), explains the position very clearly: "We find on every page of the five books of Moses <term>Kabalistic</term> expressions which declare that everything must be taken in a figurative sense; yet, in none of these books do we possess a complete treatise on initiation. From this, it follows that before Moses, oral tradition alone was responsible for transmitting the secret of initiation." Bertet drew his inspiration and often his phrasing from Éliphas Lévi.</note> Under this first category, we can also include its significance, if any, for the science of comparative theology and for the history of human thought. Besides these obvious and undisputed reasons why it deserves consideration,

There is another justification in the interest it holds for the seeker of occult knowledge. And here it is necessary to determine what is meant and involved by occultism. The study of the vast literature of the secret sciences is pursued by many people for many reasons, but few of these can be regarded as occult students in the proper sense of the term. Nor, indeed, does the attempted practice of any of the secret sciences in itself justify a claim to that title. In many cases, such practices suggest titles that are not flattering. As I understand it, the true student of occultism believes in the existence of a knowledge—which is effectively occult science—handed down from remote ages,<note>The best aspects of this belief are, as might be expected, quite modern; it can scarcely be said to have existed before the end of the eighteenth century, and even then, it had taken no definite shape. One of its aspects was developed in a remarkable way by M. de Brière, who, in his *Essay on Ancient Oriental Symbolism, Chiefly Egyptian Symbolism* (*Essai sur le symbolisme antique de l’ Orient, principalement sur le symbolisme Egyptien*, Paris, 1847), maintained: (a) A common origin for all religions; (b) The existence of priestly sciences as the exclusive inheritance of the priesthood; (c) The existence among all eastern priests of a common idiom of high antiquity, which served as a <term>theurgic</term>, magical, and effective language; (d) The reproduction of this language by <term>hieroglyphics</term> which were also <term>theurgic</term> and magical; (e) A dual priestly method of expressing the principles of priestly sciences, and chiefly of theology: (1) Imitation of words = hieroglyphs of the texts; (2) Imitation of thoughts = images, idols, emblematic figures of gods; (f) The existence of the sacred language and <term>hieroglyphic</term> writing among all peoples possessing priestly sciences, the Phoenicians and Chaldeans for example.</note> and that it concerns, broadly speaking, the way of union between humanity and God. According to its legend, it has assumed the disguise of many veils for various reasons; it is not confined to one country or people, nor is it the inner meaning of any single religion or any single literary cycle to the...

exclusion of all others; there are traces of its existence in all times, among all nations, and through all religions;* it is behind the conventional occultism of Magic and the transcendental physics of Alchemy; among occult philosophies, Kabalistic literature is one of its most important vehicles.

From this standpoint, the true message of the Kabalah is not exegetical or historical; it does not consist of systems, schools, or interpretations; it is of a living and spiritual kind. This is, indeed, the only vital point of view from which the subject can be regarded, and it redeems the entire circle of occult science from the charge of vanity.

Given this standpoint, Kabalistic literature is indescribably momentous, and yet to grant this position is impossible for ordinary criticism, and should not, indeed, be expected by those who hold it intelligently. It is, in truth, very difficult to defend, because until now it has been based either on the authority of a knowledge which cannot be made public (and is therefore pointless to proclaim) or on evidence which has little claim to serious consideration. If we take, for example, the explanatory literature of Kabalism which has been written from the occult standpoint in any modern language, there is not a single work which does not break down at once in the hands of even the most moderate criticism. Mr. Mathers, in England, has translated a small portion of the Zohar and has added an introduction which

I propose only to determine whether there is ground for believing that the Kabalah has been a channel for such a tradition. If this view must be abandoned, I intend to provide those who are willing to follow me with a way of regarding it which will make its existence at least intelligible, without taking anything for granted and without appealing to any source of knowledge which is not fully evidenced.

### III. THE KABALAH AND THE TALMUD

The post-Christian literature that holds authority in Israel must, of course, be distinguished from the varied works of its scholars and *literati*. It was the goal of rabbinical bibliographies, such as those by Bartolocci and Wolf, to summarize these works briefly. <note>*The Hebrew Library: or, a notice of both Hebrew authors of every age and of writings which were either first composed in Hebrew or translated by others, brought down to our own time.* 4 volumes, Leipzig and Hamburg, 1715, quarto.</note> In order to understand the place occupied by the Kabalah, it is necessary to say something of that great and authoritative collection known to everyone as the Talmud. The latter is a larger as well as an older development. Its starting point has been placed by moderate critical scholarship shortly before the birth of Christ; <note>There are critics outside occult circles who attribute a similar antiquity to the Kabalah, such as, for example, the author of the article under the heading *Cabale* in the *Grand Dictionnaire Universel du XIXe Siècle* (Pierre Larousse), vol. iii. Paris, 1867. “In reality, the Kabalah originated among the Jews five centuries before our era. Formed from a mixture of oriental ideas and Mosaism at the time of the captivity, it was developed silently, and mainly among the sect of the Karaites, but did not reach its final development until the period of Philo and the schools of Alexandria.” This view is inspired by Franck.</note> and, to use a somewhat conventional phrase, its two canons were established in the fourth and sixth centuries A.D. At those periods, although there is abundant evidence of a more esoteric doctrine, it cannot be shown conclusively that Kabalistic literature—in the limited sense that the term is used here—had yet come into existence. To put it briefly, the sources of the Talmud are said to be “the customs and regulations practiced by the authorities—

These materials were certain <term>Mishnayoth</term>, a term signifying repetitions—namely, notes of academic teachings, which subsequently received many additions.† About the year 220 A.D., a considerable proportion of these was gathered by Rabbi Judah the Prince,‡ by whom they were carefully systematized, with short comments of his own also being occasionally added.§

In this way the collection received the impression of his peculiar views, from which other authorities differed. He endeavored to destroy all rival <term>Mishnayoth</term>, but some of them were preserved in secret and came to light after his death. In this way we have—

(c) The <term>Gemara</term>—that is, conclusions or completion.*

The union of the <term>Gemara</term> and the <term>Mishna</term> forms the <term>Talmud</term>,† or instruction (from a word meaning "to teach"). There are two versions of it, with the <term>Mishna</term> being the same in each. The <term>Gemara</term> collected by Jerusalem rabbis—representing the school of Tiberias and Rabbi Johanon Ben Eliezer—together with the <term>Mishna</term>, forms the Jerusalem Talmud, which dates to the end of the fourth century. The <term>Gemara</term> collected by Babylonian rabbis, especially by Rabhina, Rabbi Ashi, and Rabbi José, along with the <term>Mishna</term>, forms the Talmud of Babylon, which is four times larger than that of Jerusalem.‡ It was begun in the fifth and completed in the sixth century, though even after this period, much additional material was gathered into it.

The first result of this confusion is to place a wrong interpretation on Talmudic literature by claiming that—just as is believed of the Kabalah itself—it possesses a double meaning, and that we must look beneath its literal sense.<note>Edersheim divides Talmudic traditionalism into two parts: Halakha, the legislative enactments of the Fathers; and Haggada, free interpretation.—*History of the Jewish Nation*, p. 136. Some of the Haggadic legends may possess an inner meaning; that is, they are allegorical stories. The story of the salting of the Leviathan is so absurd in its literal sense that one is moved, out of sheer generosity, to suppose that it meant something not apparent on the surface. Compare *Israel among the Nations* by Anatole Leroy-Beaulieu, p. 24. Just as Halakha is a rule or norm, Haggada is legend or saga, “a collection of miscellaneous sayings touching on every possible subject.” Only the Halakha is law.</note> It has been well noted that it would be just as reasonable to allow a metaphysical interpretation of the Common Law of England, to seek the deliberations of a Holy Synod within collections of State Trials, or to find a theory of alchemy in legal property transfers. Yet this is exactly what has been done to the Talmud by the one Kabalistic commentator whose influence over students of the occult in France and England is so great that he is considered almost beyond challenge. To Éliphas Lévi—who, in fact, misstated a great deal—we owe a grand presentation of the Talmudic system that is a serious offense to common sense.<note>*The Key to the Great Mysteries* (*La Clef des Grands Mystères*), Paris, 1861, 8vo, p. 351, etc. See also Waite: *Mysteries of Magic*, Second edition, London, 1897, 8vo, pp. 112-120.</note> He asserts that the first Talmud—the only truly Kabalistic one—was compiled during the second century of the Christian era by “Rabbi Judah the Holy—that is, Judah

<meta>This page continues the critique of Éliphas Lévi’s interpretation of the Talmudic system, specifically addressing his claims regarding the mystical structure of the Mishna and its alleged correspondence with the Sephiroth.</meta>

"the most holy and the prince"—who "composed his book according to all the rules of supreme initiation." He "wrote it within and without, as Ezekiel and St. John have it, and he indicated its transcendental sense by the sacred letters and numbers corresponding to the *Bereshith* of the first six *Sephiroth*."* This asserted Sephirotic correspondence has no basis in fact. The <term>Mishna</term> comprises six sections, of which the first concerns tithes, the beasts which it is unlawful to pair, the seeds which must not be sown together in the earth, the threads which must not be interwoven, the fruits which must not be gathered until the trees have passed their third year, and so forth. It is by no means chiefly, let alone exclusively, agricultural, as Lévi, who had obviously not read it, represents. Nor has it any special correspondence with *Kether*, except on the thin basis that "in the notion of the Supreme Crown is contained that of the fructifying principle and of universal production." Any attribution could be justified in this manner.

The second book concerns the festivals of Israel, the foods which are prohibited on these, the days of fasting, and so forth. There is no attempt to justify the attribution which connects it with *Chokmah*. The third book concerns marriage and divorce, or, in the

---

<note>I should note that, long before Éliphas Lévi, Adrianus Relandus (*Analecta Rabbinica*, 1702) and Galatinus (*De Arcanis Catholicæ Veritatis*, 1656) supposed a second meaning in the <term>Talmud</term>. There are, of course, many stories in the <term>Talmud</term> which must not be construed literally, but, as in the case previously cited, they belong merely to the realm of allegory.</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the distinctions between the Talmud and the Kabalah, moving from a critique of Éliphas Lévi’s inaccuracies to a more scholarly comparison of their differing purposes and methods.</meta>

<page-num>22</page-num>
<header>The Doctrine and Literature of the Kabalah</header>

The Talmud has its similarities to the Kabalah, but they are similarities of method rather than material. It is highly desirable to study it in connection with the Zohar, but it is a profound act of ignorance to confuse them and to regard them as being written upon the same principles and with the same goals.

Another writer, also an occultist but guided by very different scholarly standards, Mr. Isaac Myer, makes a very appropriate distinction when he states that the Kabalah and the Zohar "allow a great deal of room for speculative thought." He means that they are purely speculative philosophy, while the Talmud "deals with everyday life and humanity under the Law." He explains that the one "begins from a spiritual point of view, aiming for a spiritual purpose regarding the Law and its explanation," but that the other is "highly practical in both its starting point and end; having, in the face of the ignorance, lack of perception, and natural waywardness of the masses, nothing but the strict observance of the Law in all its details in view." *

It follows from the above distinctions that there are, broadly speaking, four separate groups or species of tradition in Israel which, by virtue of the meaning of the words, are entitled to rank as <term>Kabalah</term>: *

1. The administrative tradition of the <term>Talmud</term>, the authoritative regulations regarding the laws, customs, ceremonies, and civil life of the Jewish nation. The literature of this tradition is of great historical value, but it has little place in philosophy.

2. The magical tradition of the Hebrews, which is very important to the history of occult science, very obscure in its history, and very much exaggerated by those who write about it. It possesses little literature prior to the fourteenth century of the Christian era, by which time it had lost most of its ancient elements.†

3. Certain exegetical and other traditional

---

<note>...divides into theoretical and practical; the one is concerned with the emanations and worlds of <term>Kabalism</term>, the nature and names of God, the celestial hierarchy and its influence on the lower world, the mysteries of creation and so forth; the other deals with the mystical properties of divine and angelic names and the wonders performed with these. — "Talmudic Miscellany," London, 1880.</note>

<note>* Dr. Wynn Westcott, in his work on "*Numbers, their Occult Power and Mystic Virtue*," observes (p. 11) that the word <term>Kabalah</term> "includes the Hebrew Doctrines of <term>Cosmogony</term> and <term>Theology</term> as well as the <term>Science of Numbers</term>." The first he terms the <term>Dogmatic</term> and the second the <term>Literal Kabalah</term>. The <term>Kabalistic Science of numbers</term> is included in <term>Gematria</term>.</note>

<note>† The undeniable claim of magic to a place in Jewish <term>Kabalah</term> is asserted by all modern occultists, who have helped significantly to confuse the issues in question. <meta>This page continues the discussion of traditional Jewish groups that claim the title of Kabbalah, specifically focusing on the third group: exegetical methods used to find hidden meanings in Scripture.</meta>

methods by which a secret sense was extracted from the letter of Holy Scripture. Very curious results were sometimes obtained by these solemn follies, which appear so childish and ridiculous today.* They comprise:

a. *<term>Gematria</term>*, by which the letters of a word were converted into numbers, and the arithmetical value was used to explain its internal meaning.

b. *<term>Notaricon</term>*, by which each letter of a word was taken as the initial of another word, or, conversely, the initial letters of an entire sentence were combined to form a word, which was believed to shed light on the sentence.

c. *<term>Themurah</term>*, that is, the transposition of letters in a given word or sentence.

It is obvious that the scope of these methods is not limited to one language or one literature; their application to the plays of Shakespeare might produce results that would exceed even the claims of the "<term>Great Cryptogram</term>." It is somewhat humiliating to find an important subject and a fascinating literature connected with such diversions, but we shall see later that the peculiar views of the Hebrews regarding the divine character of their language gave them a certain plausibility, while, for the rest, our inquiry is fortunately not concerned with them. These methods are sometimes termed the artificial or prac-

4. The philosophical tradition, contained in the "*Sepher Yetzirah*" and the "*Zohar*" cycles. These divisions deal respectively with the natural and the transcendental world, and are sometimes collectively called the theoretical Kabbalah.

<page-num>29</page-num>

It is this which gave to Israel the intellectual horizon that was impossible for the Talmudic Jew, and it is this also which gave the Children of the Exile a place in Western philosophy. When we hear that the Kabbalah once fascinated some of the great minds of Christendom, it is to this alone that the statement can be applied.<note>Drach distinguishes three uses of the term Kabbalah for which authority can be cited: (1) It is frequently applied by the Talmud to the books of the Old Testament outside the Pentateuch; (2) The rabbis apply it to the legal or Talmudic tradition; (3) It signifies especially the "mystic, esoteric, acroamatic portion of the oral tradition."—*On the Harmony between the Church and the Synagogue. By the Chevalier P. L. B. Drach, 2 vols., Paris, 1844.*</note> It is this, finally, which the present inquiry aims to clarify.<note>I must not overlook the division of the Kabbalah proposed by Dr. Papus in one of his latest publications, though I regard it, critically speaking, as fantastic. The Kabbalah is, in his opinion, attributable to Moses, and the written word of Scripture is therefore naturally a part of the tradition. We have thus: (a) The written word; (b) The oral word; (c) An intermediate portion, consisting of rules ensuring the preservation of the text, i.e., the Massorah. This last is the body of the oral tradition; the Mishna and Gemara are its life; the Sepher Yetzirah and the Zohar are its spirit. Unfortunately, Dr. Papus has not made his thesis as clear as he usually does, and he seems to assume some of the most important points at issue. See *Elementary Treatise on Occult Science, 5th edition, Paris, 1898.*</note> It should be added that outside the cycle of the *Zohar* there is a large body of Jewish theosophical and mystical literature, of which the *Sepher Yetzirah* is an example. It was this which led up to the *Zohar* and was incorporated by it. But whether it was Kabbalistic in the same sense as the *Zohar* is a subject of scholarly debate.

# BOOK II

## THE DOCTRINAL CONTENT OF THE KABBALAH

### *ARGUMENT*

The fundamental doctrines of the Kabbalah are shown to be: (*a*) The Philosophy of the Absolute; (*b*) The evolution of the universe by way of emanation; (*c*) The division of the emanation into Four Worlds originating one from another. The secondary doctrines connected with these are: (1) The contrast between God in Himself and God as revealed to His people; (2) The sacramental nature of the conventional symbols of the human *Logos*; (3) Certain ways and methods by which knowledge and wisdom are attainable; (4) A complex system of pneumatology. These subjects are viewed from an occult perspective, but there is not enough evidence to support the claim that they represent an occult doctrine of Absolute Religion.

### I. THE UNMANIFEST GOD

THE conventional division of Kabbalistic doctrines into transcendental and physical, though useful for the purposes of classification, must not be taken to mean that there is a clear line of separation that causes the literature to branch off into different directions. Much less should it be thought that the Kabbalah offers a natural history of the universe. Its physics, so far as it can be said to have any, are transcendental physics. Because it admits no separation between God, Man, and Nature,<note>That is to say, the mystic communication is permanent, but the pantheistic doctrine of identity is quite foreign to the position of Kabbalism.</note> the

<summary>This page introduces the core doctrines of the Kabbalah, including the Philosophy of the Absolute, the process of emanation through Four Worlds, and the nature of the unmanifest God. It clarifies that Kabbalistic physics are transcendental and distinct from pantheism.</summary>
<keywords>Kabbalah, Absolute, emanation, Four Worlds, Logos, pneumatology, transcendental physics, God, pantheism</keywords>

--- Page 57 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to the doctrinal content of the Kabbalah, specifically exploring the bridge between the Absolute and the finite world, and the Kabbalah's status as a system of theosophy.</meta>

science that explains them is likewise one, and the best way to study it is to follow its perspective on the eternal order. It begins in that Absolute which all fundamental wisdom aims to make known or communicate to man; it attempts to show the transition from the Absolute to the relative, from the noumenal to the phenomenal, and to establish a chain of correspondence between the infinite and the finite. It is, however, more than a philosophical attempt to bridge the gulf that separates the timeless from the temporal; that is the aspect where it connects with philosophy as it is commonly understood.

The intermediaries of this transition are the ladder of ascent by which man returns to the Divine; therefore, it is also more than an explanation of the universe. Correctly speaking, it is a summation of religion, and since it is founded—regardless of the method—on those Scriptures which Jew and Christian alike have recognized as the specific revelation of God and the textbook of true religion, we can readily see what depth and mystery the Kabbalah seeks to infuse into the Bible. We shall also agree with those discerning critics who describe it, strictly speaking, as a system of theosophy; it is the application of the wisdom of Israel to the unsearchable mystery of God, and it begins, as we might have expected, by confessing that He is unsearchable—that beyond our best conceptions of all that is most divine, which are like so many truthful illusions, there is the unknown and unknowable God.<note>According to the Zohar, it is impossible to know what exists in this principle, for it never descends to our ignorance and is above even wisdom. See "*The Lesser Holy Synod*" when treating, for example, the *Head which is not a head . . . which is not comprehended by Wisdom or understanding. Kabbalæ Denudatæ Volume Two, p. 528.*</note>

<summary>This page describes the Kabbalah as a "sum of religion" and a system of theosophy that bridges the gap between the Absolute and the finite world. It emphasizes that while the Kabbalah seeks to explain the Divine through Scripture, it ultimately confesses that God remains an unsearchable and unknowable mystery.</summary>

--- Page 58 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Kabbalah as a system of theosophy, focusing on the distinction between the unknowable essence of God and His manifestations through nature and grace.</meta>

# The Doctrine and Literature of the Kabbalah

Even in the mystic communication possible between the divine and man—which is an old doctrine of Jewish mysticism existing long before the Zohar, at least in its present form—the essence escapes our understanding. We can, indeed, know God, but not as He is in Himself; our knowledge is made possible through the manifestation of the Deity, and this takes place in two ways—by the mediation of the Law of Nature, that is to say, in the physical universe; and by the Law of Grace, which is the manifestation of God in His relation with the souls of His elect. It will be seen that both these methods are sacramental, and the sacramental system is the form of all mysticism. For the Kabbalistic Jew, the Law and the Covenant were signs or mysteries capable of a plurality of interpretations, while the whole outward world was omen and metaphor. It is therefore to be expected that in the written word we must look for a meaning other than what is conveyed by the outward sense. It was also a part of the Jewish mental bias to look for an inward significance which was opposed to the external, and this unfailingly strikes the modern observer as strained and unnatural.

In the eternity which preceded either of the manifestations I have mentioned, the Deity was withdrawn into Himself and existed in a manner which entirely transcends the conception of human faculties.<note>The tract entitled the “Faithful Shepherd,” which forms part of the Zohar, says, on the authority of R. Simeon ben Jochai, that before God created the archetypal idea which underlies the form of the world, He was alone, without form or similitude, and hence there could be no knowledge of Him (*Raÿah Mehemnah*, in the Cremona edition of the Zohar, pt. ii. col. 73). There was, of course, no intelligence to</note> The names which are ascribed to the

# The Doctrinal Content of the Kabbalah

Titles for the Deity in this abyssal state are not names that represent either the condition or the Divine Nature; they are the conventions of a philosophical hypothesis. They are terms that serve to indicate that God, prior to manifestation, is nameless, just as He is beyond reach.<note>The interrogative pronoun "Who?" is assigned by the Zohar to this state of the Supreme. Earlier mysticism speaks of God being alone with His Name—that is, the Divine Tetragram—which, according to Maimonides, preceded the entire creation.</note> He is the Ancient One, and the most Ancient of all the Ancients, but this describes only the eternity of His existence. He is the Hidden of all the Hidden Ones, but this concerns only His concealment. He is *Ain Soph*, a phrase which Rosenroth translates as *fine carens*<note>*Apparatus in Librum Sohar, Part One, Kabbala Denudata*, vol. 1, p. 81.</note>—meaning the unlimited or infinite—but it also includes, through the separate meaning of the word *Ain*, the abstract conception of nothingness. This signifies only the inconceivable nature of His infinite mode.<note>Zoharic teaching specifically confirms this point. It states, for example, that prior to the creation of the world and prior to the production of any image, God was alone, formless, and resembled nothing. In this state, it is forbidden to represent Him by any image or under any form whatsoever—even by His Holy Name, or by any letter or point. — *Zohar*, ii., 42 b., Mantua.</note> According to the "Book of Concealment," His dwelling is the place which is not a place, or more literally, *locus qui non est*.<note>*Liber Occultationis, seu Mysterii*, c. i. § 5. *Kabbala Denudata*, vol. 2, p. 348.</note> At first sight, there is a touch of atheistical impiety in this attempt to describe God as one whom we cannot

---

comprehend, but the idea underlying the confusion is that the supposed period of God’s eternal rest is now beyond the comprehension of the human mind. We are unable to conceive of a state or period in which the world did not exist and God was alone.

It will be obvious that all ordinary notions of a personal God are destroyed or transcended by this *Non-Ens* or *Non-Ego* of the Kabbalists; it is absolutely simple—a unity without any multiplicity, above all number, and above Wisdom, which, as we shall see, is nevertheless one of its first emanations. ## II. THE DOCTRINE OF THE TEN EMANATIONS

Having thus postulated the existence of the Absolute and the Unconditioned, the next concern of the Kabbalah is the manner in which that withdrawn and inconceivable nature manifests. Having reached its ultimate and fundamental conception of the Deity through the process of elimination already mentioned, it was inevitable that attributing absolute reality to that which had been stripped of all realism would result in something outside intellectual comprehension. It is perhaps open to question whether this fact justified the transcendence with which *Ain Soph* was invested, just as it is also doubtful whether the methodical and elaborate antithesis of anthropomorphism created here was not as much a convention of the human mind as the one it sought to replace.

<page-num>37</page-num>
# The Doctrinal Content of the Kabalah

The intellectual difficulty was, however, the reason for the exaltation of the conception at the expense of the human mind by which it had been so laboriously devised. Now, the Jew was confronted by at least two problems which called for the exercise of his further ingenuity regarding the *latens Deitas* (hidden Godhead) of *Ain Soph*. He had to account for the bond of connection between this abyss of the Godhead and the visible universe, which has man as its mouthpiece; but so far, this is only the common problem of all philosophy that begins and ends in the unconditioned. He had, further, a problem peculiar to his own inheritance and election: this was to establish another bond of connection between the absolute transcendence of *Ain Soph*—apart from all limitation, outside all human measurement, and isolated from all relationship—and the anthropomorphic Lord of Israel, whose stature and measurements were not beyond the ingenuity of rabbinical calculations. Most of the Lord's members are mentioned with sufficient fullness and frequency in the sacred writings for any devout student to possess a clear notion of the “body of God,” and to describe it, if he pleased—and we shall see later on that he did, with considerable precision, in a book dedicated to the question. For the moment, however, we are concerned only with the first problem: namely, the difficulty of conceiving why the abyssal state, in which God unmanifest had been sufficient to Himself from eternity, should at any period have had another mode superadded to it. I say superadded by convention.

<note>The Zohar says that it is called *Ayin*, not on the ground of non-existence, but—it may be inferred—because that which is wholly outside our knowledge is to us as nothingness.</note>

The <term>non ens</term> (non-being) dwelling in the <term>non est</term> (non-is) is like the zero of the decimal system;<note>The circle is, in fact, a Kabalistic symbol of <term>Ain Soph</term>.</note> on its own it is nothing, and its expansion produces nothing; so too it is impossible to add to it, yet it gives value to all numbers. Thus a treatise entitled "The Royal Valley," by Rabbi Naphthali Hirtz, says: "Blessed be His Holy Name! Before anything existed, He, by His simple will, proposed to Himself to fashion the worlds. And it is the nature of the Supreme Goodness to dispense good. Now, if the world did not exist, on whom could He bestow it?"† The exegetical literature—treatises like the "Gates of Light"—indicate that the exercise of the Divine Will in the production of the emanations is a path so secret that no creature, not even Moses himself, can understand it.‡ At the same time, that will is *beneplacitum*, or "good pleasure," and *beneplacitum termine carens*, "good pleasure without end or limit." Hence the motive that accounts for the universe is the same motive that communicates the mercy of God to those who fear Him; after which it will be unnecessary to say that optimism is the fundamental characteristic of Kabalism, or that, according to the *Zohar*, this is, in some respects, the best of all possible worlds.

It must be allowed in any case that the <term>Kabalah</term> implicitly repudiates the axiom *ex nihilo nihil fit*, for the <term>non ens</term> dwelling in the unconditioned state where there is neither time nor place† is the fullness which contains the all. *Ex plenitudine ista omnia fiunt.* In this divine plenitude, pre-existing eternally, was the substance of all the worlds, which therefore came forth from God. &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The conjunction of *Chokmah* and *Binah* produced a quasi-emanation called *Daath* (knowledge), but it is not one of the *<term>Sephiroth</term>*.*
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Various profound meanings are attached to these ten emanations or numerations; indeed, the study of the Kabalistic system of the *<term>Sephiroth</term>* constitutes a science in itself, and one that is full of complexity. We are not concerned here with its exhaustive presentation, which would fill volumes, or with more than its elementary symbolism. We are concerned, in a

See especially the treatise *Beth Elohim* concerning *Kether*, in which the idea of circularity is involved. The author of the "Gates of Light" relates the term to the Hebrew word for sapphire; this stone, because of its brightness and purity, is a symbol of the *Sephiroth*. word—not with what it may have been designed to conceal for the benefit of a circle of initiates, which is the claim of occult science, but with what it was intended to explain. This explanation may offer some justification for concluding that outside of it, there is only the realm of fantasy.

Beyond a certain point, it is not reasonable to suppose a double meaning in any literature; the theory of many-sided allegories credits only the ingenuity of the critic, and we have had a typical instance of its general value in Talmudic exegesis.*

The initial purpose of the Sephirotic system was undoubtedly to provide intermediaries between the Deity and the material world. This is the goal of all doctrines of emanation. But while we reject conjectures for which no evidence is produced, we must be careful not to fall into the opposite error. To bridge the gulf between the finite and the infinite, and to establish a correspondence by stages between the inconceivable purity of the Divine Nature and the uncleanness attributed to matter by all the ancient theosophies, was not the sole purpose of the Sephirotic system—a point which is sometimes missed by the purely academic critic.

It is affirmed by hostile writers, for example, by

---

<note>* After an exhaustive study of modern occult literature, I doubt very much whether the occultist really concerns himself with discovering a concealed sense in the Kabalah. It is a sufficient exercise of his patience to codify and harmonize the outward sense, which is perhaps a little irradiated and transcendentalized by his methods, but is certainly not removed. Take, for example, the concept of <term>Ain Soph</term>: he certainly does not look for any idea more hidden than that of divine latency within it. The inner meaning of the Kabalah is its own proper and singular sense, which has been confused by the obscurity of its style and its subject.</note>

Dr. Ginsburg,* that since the earliest Kabbalistic literature does not contain the doctrine of *Ain Soph*, it also lacks that of the *Sephiroth*; however, it is beyond challenge that the germ of the Sephirotic scheme must be sought in the *Sepher Yetzirah*. The ten numerations of that treatise are, in fact, the *Sephiroth*, and it seems quite impossible to maintain the opposite opinion.†

### III. THE DOCTRINE OF THE FOUR WORLDS

The Sephirotic system was concerned first of all, as I have indicated, with the mystery of Divine Evolution. From that unsearchable condition which is above consciousness, through a mysterious operation, the Uncreated Will moved outward, and subsequently, three manifestations or relations of the Deity were established. By the first manifestation, the *Ain Soph* passed from latency into activity; the *non-Ego* became the *Ego*, still subsisting, however, in a condition that is humanly inconceivable—the state of pure abstract thought. The concentration of this thought is depicted in *Kether*, which is also the Divine Will in its primordial manifestation. The Supreme Crown‡ is, symbolically speaking, the base or sphere

Through the second manifestation, abstract thought entered into or developed the relationship of time, so that it could now be regarded as that which was, which is, and which is to come. Lastly, it established a relation with Nature—that is to say, its development produced the universe.

By a slight extension of the symbolism, *Kether* is also regarded as the Throne of the Ancient of Days,<note>*</note> and since the Divine Consciousness is the veil of the subsistent state, *Ain Soph* is further represented as the central point of *Kether*, viewed as a sphere, while the circumference is infinity, which is, as it were, the Divine Vestment. <term>Chokmah</term> is described by the "Book of Formation" as the Breath of the Spirit of God.

*Binah*, Intelligence or Understanding, is symbolically represented by the same fundamental authority as the moisture of the Breath of the Spirit. It is the highest <term>Sephira</term> with which man can establish correspondence, but it contains at the same time one mystery which was also concealed from Moses. The root of all roots and the foundation of all foundations is communicated to man by it; otherwise, man could have no knowledge of the antecedent states of the Divine Nature.*

Magnificence or Mercy, <term>Gedulah</term> or <term>Chesed</term>, the fourth emanation, is the warmth or fire contained within the moisture breathed forth by the Spirit of God. It expresses the eternal love and compassion, connecting with life and vitality. It is the base of the *beneplacitum termine carens* <note>Latin: "a goodwill lacking a limit."</note> mentioned in the last section and implicitly supposes the free will of the Divine Agent. It follows from this as a consequence that the universe was made or emanated, not because anything was lacking to the Divine completeness, but out of the fullness of goodwill. This is, however, in distinct opposition to some of the later Kabbalists, especially <term>Isaac de Loria</term>.† Symbolically speaking, <term>Chesed</term> is therefore the <term>Sephira</term> by which God constituted the world.

---

The fifth <term>Sephira</term> is <term>Geburah</term>, signifying Judgment, Justice, or Judicial Power; it is also known as <term>Pachad</term>, or Fear. It is the heavenly tribunal before which nothing can endure.

<term>Tiphereth</term>, or Beauty, the sixth <term>Sephira</term>, is, in a sense, the union of Mercy and Judgment and summarizes the Divine goodness; it is the heart of the pillar of kindness.

But the Divine Kindness is manifested by the victory signified in <term>Netzach</term>, the seventh <term>Sephira</term>. There are three rays spread from the radiance of Providence—Kindness, Beauty, and Victory. When they shine and are spread over the <term>Sephiroth</term>, the whole world is filled with joy and perfection, for the Divine goodness itself looks out upon all creatures, and all the worlds are in fullness and completeness.<note>Reference material for the Book of Zohar. *The Kabbalah Unveiled*, under the entry *Superatio* (Victory), that is, <term>Netzach</term>, p. 589 and following.</note> This <term>Sephira</term> is also called Eternity.

The eighth <term>Sephira</term>, <term>Hod</term>, signifies Glory, Adornment, or Splendor. In combination with <term>Netzach</term>, it is called the armies of Jehovah. All the greetings and praises contained in the Psalms of David belong to this emanation. It is the place of praise, the place of wars and victories, and the treasury of benefits.<note>Ibid. under the entry *Decus, Gloria* (Honor, Glory), that is, <term>Hod</term>, p. 268 and following. According to the Zohar, <term>Netzach</term> and <term>Hod</term> correspond to extension, multiplication, and force; from there, all the forces of the universe emerge, which is why these <term>Sephiroth</term> are also called the Armies of the Eternal. *Zohar*, iii., 296 a, Mantua.</note>

<term>Jesod</term>, the Basis or Foundation, the ninth <term>Sephira</term>, is the storehouse of all forces, the seat of life and vitality, and the nourishment of all the worlds.<note>*The Kabbalah Unveiled*, Reference material, under the entry *Fundamentum* (Foundation), that is, <term>Jesod</term>, p. 439 and following.</note>
