*<term>Malkuth</term> is the tenth <term>Sephira</term>, signifying Dominion, Royalty, or Kingdom. In the "Lesser Holy Synod" it is termed "the Mother of all the Living." It is the final manifestation, emanation, or development of the Divine Nature taking place in the Divine World; it is, therefore, the point at which the more external orders make contact with the supernal.*<note>Therefore it is said that the tenth <term>Sephira</term> is the <term>Shekinah</term>, that is, the place of the manifestation of Deity.</note>

To this brief general description, which rests on the authority and reproduces the words of the Kabalists, I will now add the main points of an occult interpretation. This is, of course, conjectural, but it presents a very reasonable perspective.

<term>Ain Soph</term>, the Unknowable and Absolute, manifests through the outflow of the spiritual and material universe, using the <term>Sephiroth</term> as its media. The first emanation symbolizes Abstract Thought—the Absolute assuming consciousness to manifest outwardly. The second emanation represents the association of abstract ideas in the intellect, which is Wisdom. The third emanation is Mind receiving the impressions of abstract ideas. These three constitute the Spirit of the World. The second triad of <term>Sephiroth</term>—Mercy, Judgment, and Beauty—includes the principles of construction and symbolizes the abstract dimensions of matter: length, breadth, depth, and their double polarity. <term>Chesed</term> and <term>Geburah</term> are the centripetal and centrifugal energies between the poles of these dimensions. In their junction with <term>Tiphereth</term>, they represent all ethical life and perfection. They correspond to the Soul of the

Now, it must be confessed that the distinction seems absolute between the Sephirotic system and any anthropomorphic conception of Deity. It is arbitrary to the point of being fantastic, and it seems to lie outside all human correspondences. Its perspective is that the visible world is the final consequence in the development of the attributes of God, or that God, in order to create, had to proceed outward from His eternal existence. The *<term>Sephiroth</term>* are the symbolism of these attributes, and the course of their outflow is the history

It is generally held that the *Sephiroth* were originally contained one within the other; that is, *Kether* enclosed all those below it, *Chokmah* enclosed *Binah*, while *Binah* contained the last seven, produced by a successive outpouring. “Originating as points, they expanded in a circular shape—ten circles under the mystery of the ten *Sephiroth*, and between them, ten spaces.”<note>*Introductio in Librum Sohar*, also known as *Vallis Regia*. The entire emanation, unlikely as it may seem, was gathered together under the concept of a heavenly man, Adam Kadmon, who is archetypal and primordial. It was similarly organized under the concept of a supreme world termed *Atziluth*, the World of Deity,<note>The "intelligible world" of the Zohar.</note> of which Adam Kadmon<note>That is, the Man from the East.</note> was the sole occupant.<note>Myer: "Philosophy of Ibn Gebirol," p. 418.</note> This is specifically termed the World of Emanations. From this proceeded a second world, also having its Sephirotic decade, the World of Creation, called *Briah*. However, as I infer, this is not to be understood in the usual sense of that term, for *Briah* also came forth or was emanated. In fact, it was a consequence of the superior world—namely, of the effectuating energies of the Supreme Will residing in the Archetypal World. The Sephirotic forces were carried forward into *Briah*, and through this prolonged emanation, the world of the highest finite intelligence was formed—technically that of the arch-

angels, were produced. But the Sephirotic extension was continued into a third world, that of *Yetzirah*, or formation, the home of the angelic choirs. Although it is further removed from Supreme Perfection, there is no trace of the material in this dwelling of bodiless beings. It is different with the World of Action, the fourth product of the tenfold emanation, *Assiah*, the region of matter and also the dwelling place of the demons, called shells or *Cortices* by the Kabalists. Like many other systems of emanation, this material world is viewed as the gross waste of the higher regions. It should be noted, however, that the *Sephiroth* permeate all four systems, but they diminish in purity as they proceed further from *Ain Soph*. The corruption of the infernal world, the formless region, and the seven hells of Kabalism are the extreme limits of the emanation which begins in *Kether*. Thus, in order to explain the imperfections found in the creation of a perfect author, the idea of the deterioration of his infinite energy is utilized as an explanation. It is easy to criticize such a system, or to dismiss it as beneath criticism, but again, this difficulty is common to the visions of all who believe in emanation. Finally, we have the clear authority of the *Zohar* for regarding the demons as products of the will of God, designed for a specific purpose; but this point may be reserved for further consideration at a later stage.*

Broadly speaking, the Four Worlds of the Kabalah may be regarded as corresponding in the physical order:

(a) ATZILUTH, to the *Primum Mobile* [the First Mover].
(b) BRIAH, to the sphere of the Zodiac.
(c) YETZIRAH, to the planetary chain.
(d) ASSIAH, to the world of the four elements.

Thus, astronomy is at the basis of this conception.

The doctrine of the Four Worlds was developed between the period of the *Sepher Yetzirah* and that of the publication of the *Zohar*, and it received many additions from the commentators on the latter work. It is first encountered in the “Book of Emanation,” which is a product of the school of Isaac the Blind. This treatise is attributed to Rabbi Jacob Nazir. Its distribution of the Four Worlds differs from the above list in one or two respects—as, for example, by assigning the souls of the righteous to *Briah*, the archangelic world. It should be added that the *Zohar* also recognizes a distribution of the *Sephiroth* into Three Worlds: (1) Intelligible, (2) Moral, (3) Natural.

## IV. THE DOCTRINE OF THE COUNTENANCES

The Four Worlds are also depicted in a single Sephirotic scheme, and this leads us to another order of symbolism established for a distinct purpose: namely, the justification of the relationship between man and God. This justification is founded—it is unnecessary to say—on God’s providence regarding the Jewish nation, as it is recorded in the sacred literature of the First Covenant. There are many respects,

The relations of God with man are, however, those which are portrayed in the dealings between Jehovah and the Jewish people, and the conception of the God of Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob had to be harmonized with the exalted or at least hidden Godhead of *Ain Soph* and the Sephirotic system. Now, *Ain Soph*, as such, the *latens Deitas* <note>hidden Deity</note>, being essentially unknowable and outside the region of correspondence and relationship, enters by manifestation into a somewhat knowable but still unsearchable and transcendent state in the heavenly triad of the *Sephiroth*. Therefore, this triad is taken to represent the entire Sephirotic system of the Atzilutic world. By this head, which is devoid of any lower physical form, the antithesis of an anthropomorphic Deity is represented, and the qualities attributed to it are also the antithesis of the embodied Jehovah. As *Ain Soph* is the Closed Eye of the Unknown Darkness, so in the Vast Countenance the eye is open and never closes,<note>"Book of Concealment," chapter 1, verse 14.</note> for the survival of the universe and all its worlds depends upon the light which shines from it.<note>The same notion is found in Indian mythology, and readers of Southey's "*Curse of Kehama*" will remember what followed the sudden veiling of the eyes of *Seeva*.</note> In this Vast Countenance there is neither wrath nor judgment, while all the wrath and judgment that operate in the world and in humanity are held in check by the mercy and long-suffering of *Macroprosopus* to such an extent that they may be regarded as counterbalanced and canceled out in the final arrangement. There is no need to elaborate here on the symbolism, which must be referenced again. It therefore includes the two lower triads of the *Sephiroth*, representing the worlds of Creation and Formation. However, just as *Kether* in the world of *Atziluth* is specifically assigned to the Vast Countenance, *Tiphereth* in *Briah* is assigned to the Lesser Countenance. Since patience is the characteristic of *Macroprosopus*, "swiftness to anger" is the characteristic of the inferior being, "having a small face, quickly angered."<note>*Apparatus in Librum Sohar, Kabbala Denudata*, vol. 1, p. 312, under the entry for *Macroprosopus*.</note> These contrasting qualities are symbolized by the length and shortness of the face.

*Microprosopus* is viewed by modern occultism as the shadow or reflection of the superior manifestation, though this perspective is not fully supported by Kabbalistic literature. Allowing for some inaccuracies, it generally represents what the symbolism intends to convey; the main point we wish to establish here is that *Microprosopus* acts as the mediator between God and humanity. However, this is not mediation in the Christian sense of intercession, because wrath, vengeance, and judgment do not exist within the Vast Countenance, just as they do not exist in *Ain Soph*. The mediation is instead communicative or sacramental. In the natural order, it is the human conception of the Deity, to which God descends and might be said, in a sense, to validate and even to shape.<note>Éliphas Lévi, *The Book of Splendors*, Paris, 1894, p. 69.</note> Conversely, when viewed from above, it is the structure of the Divine Nature, so that its

knowledge and its power may be extended to mankind. It is the adaptation of the infinite being to finite possibilities, so that the limited may receive a certain measure of the boundless. It is as much of God as man, by his nature and condition, is capable of comprehending; it is Divine Providence leading humanity by a governance suited to humanity, through struggle and reward, through kindness and severity, reward and punishment. God in Himself is above all these things, but they are necessary for His willful children. In a word, it is as much of Newton's *Principia* as would be understood in a village school—namely, the illustration of the falling apple.

That this is the significance of the symbolism is further demonstrated by the Sephirotic assignment of certain Divine Names which occur continually in the Bible. The affirmation of self-existence and self-consciousness is made in the Name which signifies "I am that I am," AHIH,* and this is assigned to *Kether*, while the world of *Atziluth* generally includes the mysterious name—never uttered and therefore regarded as incomprehensible and unpronounceable—JOD, HE, VAU, HE.† When an Israelite encounters this name in the Bible, he omits it altogether or substitutes ADNI, Adonai, meaning "the Lord." The relationship between *Macroprosopus* and *Microprosopus* is the philosophical hypothesis of the correspondence between the absolute as it is and the absolute as conceived by man. The disparity between them records the inadequacy of all human conceptions of the Divinity, which every Kabalist fully recognized as being abundant in that book which he regarded as more Divine than any other.

Like the Sephirotic system, the doctrine of the Four Worlds and the two Countenances is curiously involved, and though in its elements it is exceedingly simple, in its elaborations it is highly technical. As in the single Sephirotic scheme we have been dealing with here, the first triad of emanations is held to represent the world of *Atziluth*, so the second triad includes *Briah* and the third the world of Formation, or *Yetzirah*. *Assiah*, the fourth world, is contained in *Malkuth*. Now, with *Microprosopus* there is associated the symbol of a Bride, who is in fact *Malkuth*, or the Kingdom; in this sense, it follows that the visible creation is the Spouse of God, or rather of that lesser Divine Manifestation which alone communicates with man. ## V. THE INSTRUMENTS OF CREATION

It must be admitted that, so far, Kabalistic literature deserves to be considered philosophical. The doctrine of *Ain Soph* may be categorized alongside Platonic conceptions; the <term>Sephirotic</term> system does not suffer when compared with any other vision of emanation and may even challenge them all; the principle that underlies the metaphysics of the two Countenances is remarkably profound and may be regarded as the primary glory of the Kabalah. We must now, however, approach its more fanciful portions. We have seen that the World of *Briah* is that of Creation, but regardless of what reservations might be inferred from Kabalistic writers regarding the axiom *ex nihilo nihil fit* (out of nothing, nothing comes),† we have also seen that their use

Further, the world of *Briah* was not that in which anything material was formed, emanated, or otherwise brought into actual being; it was rather the Elohistic world, that of Panurgic force and intelligence, which became formative in *Yetzirah*, but did not produce matter until the fourth world. Now the materials used and shaped—or, perhaps, more properly speaking, the instruments and matrices of the material world—are said by the Kabalists to have been the letters of the Hebrew alphabet. According to the *Sepher Yetzirah*, God imparted to them form and weight by combining and transforming them in various manners: *Aleph* with all the rest and all the rest with *Aleph*; *Beth* with all the rest and all the rest with *Beth*; and so on with the others.* Hundreds of permutations were obtained in this manner, which are the origin not only of all languages but of all creatures. As these permutations can, by the hypothesis, be reduced to a single name, that of the *Tetragrammaton*—the JOD, HE, VAU, HE, which I have had occasion to mention previously—it is said that the entire universe proceeds from this name.† The

The reader will immediately discern the nature of this device, which can be organized through a simple process:

->The world emerged from God:<-
->But the name of God is יהוה;<-
->Therefore the world emerged from יהוה*<-

The fundamental letters of the *Book of Formation* are not, however, those that compose the Divine Name; they are *Aleph* (א), *Mem* (מ), and *Shin* (ש), distinguished as the Three Mothers and corresponding to Air, Water, and Fire. The heavens are formed of Fire, the Earth is of Water, and the Air is formed of the mediating Spirit.

Their correspondences are: in the year, the hot, cold, and temperate seasons; in man, the head, belly, and breast.

Besides the Three Mothers there are seven double letters—*Beth* (ב = B), *Ghimel* (ג = G), *Daleth* (ד = D), *Kaph* (כ = K), *Pe* (פ = P), *Resh* (ר = R), and *Tau* (ת = T, Th). These seven signs stand in the *Book of Formation* for:

| | | |
| :--- | :---: | :--- |
| Life | | Death |
| Peace | | Strife |
| Knowledge | | Ignorance |
| Wealth | **and their opposites** | Poverty |
| Grace | | Sin |
| Fruitfulness | | Sterility |
| Dominion | | Slavery |

bizarre system is to be interpreted. It has been regarded by one or two critics who have no occult leanings as a serious attempt to devise a philosophical cosmology; <note>Dr. Alfred Edersheim seems to speak in this sense in his "*History of the Jewish Nation after the Destruction of Jerusalem*." I have used the third, posthumous edition, revised by the Rev. H. A. White, M.A. London, 1896. See p. 408.</note> but for myself I must confess that I do not see in what manner it is superior to the familiar fable of the elephant and the tortoise. There are those, of course, who discern in it a secret meaning, who remember, for example, that the letters of the Hebrew alphabet also stand for numerals, and do not fail to cite the scriptural statement that God made everything by weight, number, and measure.

Indeed, the *Sepher Yetzirah* may be regarded as a commentary on this declaration. As a rule, however, I think that the Kabalists, like other makers of philosophy, meant what they said, and if they did not say all that they meant, the unexpressed remainder followed the lines of the sense expressed. When they affirmed, therefore, that the world was made by means of the letters of the alphabet, they really meant what they stated; but if it be asked whether they understood by those letters the symbols of arcane powers, it must be answered that they did. The letters are, however, more than mere symbols; they are vessels or manifestations of the concealed powers. The sense is therefore true *ex hypothesi* in a literal and arcane manner.

The justification for the hypothesis must be sought in the Talmudic system, which believed that the body of the sacred text was divine, just like the meaning which was

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Hebrew text's dual nature, moving from the concept of its "soul" to the literal letters of the alphabet as the material "body" of revelation, preserved by the science of the Massorah.</meta>

# The Doctrine and Literature of the Kabbalah

its soul, which, like man himself, had an inner spirit, the highest of all, namely, the concealed meaning. Now, the letters of the alphabet were the materials of the textual body, to the care and preservation of which the traditional science of the Massorah was devoted.<note>The Massorah was concerned with the body of the text, the rules for reading and writing the *Thorah*, and special considerations on the mystical sense of the sacred characters. It was therefore the criticism of the Hebrew text. It was also, as already seen, that which was openly delivered by the rabbis in contrast to what was supposed to have been communicated secretly. Thus, it taught the true reading of doubtful passages, the true pronunciation of uncertain words, the correct subdivisions of the books, and so forth. Buxtorf’s work entitled *Tiberias* (Basel, 1620, 4to) deals with the Massorah. Compare Molitor’s *Philosophy of Tradition*. Occultists claim that its external formulas were designed to conceal every trace of a secret sense in the *Thorah*. See *Mission des Juifs*, p. 646, by Saint-Yves d’Alveydre, who follows Fabre d’Olivet in *La Langue Hébraïque Restituée*. The Massorah compiled from manuscripts, alphabetically and lexically arranged, has been published by C. D. Ginsburg, 3 volumes, London, 1880–85.</note> For the mystical Jew, who perceived strange depths of mystery in the smallest peculiarities of the *Thorah*, there was a weird fascination in the fact that all the wonders and sanctities of the Law and the Prophets resulted from the diverse combinations of twenty-two letters, and he came to regard this handful of conventional hieroglyphs as so many sacraments or instruments by which divine wisdom was communicated to man. In a word, for him they ceased to be conventions; a divine revelation required a divine language to express it, and the alphabet of that language was a derivation from the noumenal world—vessels of unique selection, instruments of Deity—from which it was an easy transition to suppose that such channels of spiritual grace and life must have fulfilled some exalted role in the shaping of the universe itself.

It also defines the Massorah as the scholarly tradition dedicated to the preservation and literal analysis of the sacred text.</summary>

<page-num>65</page-num>

# The Doctrinal Content of the Kabbalah

This doctrine of the instruments of creation is the oldest part of Kabbalistic literature, which, consequently, improved significantly as it developed. It was intimately connected with the idea of the pre-existence of the *Thorah*, and to say that the world was created by the inscription of letters in the air was, in one sense, only another way of saying that it was created by the *Thorah*. This latter affirmation can be viewed in a philosophical manner with the help of the Kabbalistic doctrine of correspondences, in which the *Thorah* of Moses would be only the earthly type of the Eternal and Divine Law.

### VI. THE PATHS OF WISDOM

In the Latin collection of Pistorius, the essence of philosophical Kabbalism is presented in the form of certain terse propositions or dogmas,<note>They are the extremely interesting theses of Picus de Mirandola, which will be found in Book vii.</note> according to one of which the ways of eternity are thirty-two—*Viæ æternitatis sunt triginta duo.*<note>They are referred to the *Sephirah Chokmah* and are termed occult channels, at once hidden and revealed. In the "Faithful Shepherd," *Chokmah* is called the highest of all paths, embracing and including all that are beneath it, and the influence of all is derived from it. The same treatise connects with *Chokmah* the words in Job 28:7: "The bird has not known the path, neither has the eye of the vulture beheld it."—*Kabbala Denudata, Apparatus*, i. 601, 602.</note> These are the paths of the *Sepher Yetzirah*, namely, the ten *Sephiroth* and the letters of the Hebrew alphabet. The doctrine concerning them is an offshoot of this fundamental treatise, but of a much more recent date.

It lists the special graces and illuminations that may be communicated to man from above through these channels, and it is very interesting because it shows that the most philosophical part of Kabalism had a practical application to the human mind and was not merely a speculative system. It is outside the scope of this work to offer translations to the student, but as it would be difficult to summarize the table more briefly in this instance, I shall provide it *in full*, noting only that it has been translated into English more than once and is indeed available in a number of European languages.

The first path is called the Admirable Intelligence, the Supreme Crown. It is the light that imparts understanding of the beginning which is without beginning, and this is also the First Splendor. No created being can attain its essence.

The second path is called the Illuminating Intelligence. It is the Crown of Creation and the splendor of the Supreme Unity, to which it is closest in proximity. It is exalted above every head and is distinguished by Kabalists as the Second Splendor.

The third path is called the Sanctifying Intelligence and is the foundation of Primordial Wisdom, termed the Creation of Faith. Its roots are אמן <note>Amen</note>. It is the mother of Faith, which indeed emanates from it.

The fourth path is called the Arresting or Receiving Intelligence, because it arises like a boundary to receive the emanations of the higher intelligences which are sent down to it. From here all

The fifth path is called the Radical Intelligence, because it is more akin than any other to the Supreme Unity and emanates from the depths of the Primordial Wisdom.†

The sixth path is called the Intelligence of Mediating Influence, because the flow of the emanations is multiplied within it. It communicates this abundance to those blessed men who are united with it.‡

The seventh path is called the Hidden Intelligence, because it pours out a brilliant splendor on all intellectual virtues which are beheld with the eyes of the spirit and by the ecstasy of faith.

The eighth path is called the Perfect and Absolute Intelligence. The preparation of principles emanates from it.§ The roots to which it adheres are in the depths of the Sphere of Magnificence, from the very substance of which it emanates.

The ninth path is called the Purified Intelligence. It purifies the numerations, prevents and stops the fracture of their images,|| for it establishes their unity.

Because it rules the movement of the sun and the moon according to their constitution and causes each to gravitate in its respective orb.<note>According to Eliphas Lévi, this verse contains the secret of the great work of alchemy. The reason given is that path thirty-one corresponds to the Hebrew letter *Shin* (Sh), which represents the magic lamp, or the light between the horns of Baphomet. "It is the Kabbalistic sign of God or the Astral Light, with its two poles and balanced center." The sun mentioned in the paragraph represents gold, the moon silver, and the planets correspond to the other metals.—*La Clef des Grands Mystères*, p. 234. It is unnecessary to say that the *Sepher Yetzirah* and its developments have nothing to do with alchemy.</note>

The thirty-second path is called the Assisting Intelligence, because it directs all the operations of the seven planets, with their divisions, and concurs in them.

The modern tone of this table will be immediately apparent to the reader, but its quotation was necessary to show the intellectual profit believed to follow from the study of Kabbalism, and still more to show that it was, in the ultimate resource, the systematized understanding of man,<note>"Man is the Kabbalistic balance," according to Mr. W. B. Greene.—"The Blazing Star," p. 51.</note> and embracing, as such, the entire circle assigned to human knowledge.<note>However, it fell, as might be expected, into superstitious uses and became a kind of theosophical divination, based on the first chapter of Genesis, where the name Elohim is mentioned thirty-two times.</note>

# The Doctrine and Literature of the Kabalah

For the sake of completeness, and because occult importance has been attributed to it—though without any real justification—a word may be added concerning a somewhat conventional Kabbalistic classification titled the Fifty Gates of Understanding. It is associated with *Binah*, the third Sephira, and is an attempt to sketch the outlines of universal science, to embrace (as Éliphas Lévi observes) all possible departments of knowledge and to represent a complete encyclopedia. In the present day, such classifications have something of a ghostly aspect. There is, however, no intention to organize human science after the impossible manner of Raymond Lully and his *Ars Magna Sciendi*. I also infer that, despite the exalted themes included in the scheme, it concerns only intellectual knowledge acquired by external means. Thus, it constitutes a kind of academic introduction to the paths of *Chokmah* or of Wisdom,<note>According to Papus, the thirty-two paths are deductive like the *Sepher Yetzirah* itself, which starts from the concept of God and proceeds from there to natural phenomena, while the fifty gates are established on the inductive principle, ascending from Nature to Deity.—*La Kabbale*, p. 132.</note> by which the holy men of God may—as Kircher observes—after long labor, long experience of divine things, and long meditation upon them, penetrate to the hidden centers.<note>Kircher, *Oedipus Aegyptiacus*, Rome, 1623, folio.</note> The logic of this enumeration should perhaps be sought in the symbolism of the Hebrew word *Koll*, which signifies "All," and whose consonants are numerically equivalent to fifty.

---

The consultation of this chapter was accompanied by prayers extracted from the divine name in question and, according to Kircher, by appropriate ceremonies.

<summary>This page introduces the "Fifty Gates of Understanding," a Kabbalistic system associated with the Sephira Binah that attempts to categorize all human knowledge. The author notes that while it is often given occult significance, it primarily serves as an intellectual or inductive introduction to deeper spiritual wisdom.</summary>

<keywords>Fifty Gates of Understanding, Binah, Sephira, Éliphas Lévi, Raymond Lully, Chokmah, Kircher, Koll, Papus, inductive vs deductive, universal science</keywords>

--- Page 99 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Fifty Gates of Understanding (associated with Binah), explaining how they serve as a preliminary stage to the thirty-two paths of Chokmah.</meta>

# The Doctrinal Content of the Kabalah

The Gates of Understanding, considered as an introduction to the Paths of Wisdom, which diverge, as we have seen, from *Chokmah*, are essential to the higher knowledge approached by these.<note>They are called gates because no one can reach the paths unless he enters through them.—*Ibid.*</note> It would serve no purpose to list them all categorically; they begin with the first matter, the *Hyle* or Chaos, proceed through the various elements of ancient science to the theory of composite substances, and then to organic life and the physical, intellectual, and psychic nature of man. Afterwards, they move to the heaven of the planets, that of the fixed stars and the *primum mobile*, then to the nine orders of the angelic world, and, finally, to the supermundane and archetypal world—that of *Ain Soph*, unseen by mortal eye and transcending human intelligence. It is said that Moses did not reach this, the fiftieth, gate, and some stress seems to be laid on this point; one would think it a little unnecessary, as it is obvious that what is beyond all finite capacity must have been beyond the lawgiver of Israel.

The scheme of the Gates of Understanding is late in Kabbalism; it is found in the treatise entitled "The Gates of Light,"<note>By Rabbi Joseph Gikatilla ben Abraham.</note> which is full of references to the mystery of the word *Koll* (KL). All created things, it explains, have come out of these gates, so that in a sense their knowledge connects with the mystery of universal generation. In reference to this, it may be observed that the addition of the feminine letter H = 5 to the word KL = 50 gives KLH = the Bride of *Microprosopus*, the Lesser Countenance.

<summary>This page describes the Fifty Gates of Understanding as a hierarchical progression of knowledge from chaos and the physical world to the angelic and divine realms. It identifies the "Gates of Light" as the source for this late Kabbalistic scheme and links it to the mystery of universal generation through the word Koll.</summary>

<keywords>Gates of Understanding, Chokmah, Hyle, Chaos, primum mobile, Ain Soph, Moses, Gates of Light, Koll, KLH, Microprosopus, Joseph Gikatilla ben Abraham, universal generation</keywords>

--- Page 100 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the symbolism of the word KLH (the Bride) and the Fifty Gates of Understanding, transitioning into the study of the soul.</meta>

From this follows the whole mystery of spiritual generation in man, for KLH connects with KNSTL—that is, the Church, *Ecclesia Israel*—and brings us back to that place called mystically Zion and Jerusalem, in which the divine is communicated to man, as stated in the fourth section of this book. It is by little gleams of suggestion of this kind that the barren science of *Gematria* is occasionally illuminated.

### VII. THE DOCTRINE OF
### PNEUMATOLOGY

We have now ascertained the primary topics of Kabbalistic instruction regarding the essential nature of God, the transition from the divine unmanifest into the manifestation of divinity, the extension of the powers and attributes thus developed through the archetypal, creative, formative, and material worlds, the Kabbalistic hypothesis of creation, and the doctrine of transcendental and natural science. It remains for us to present in a brief outline the doctrine of spiritual essences according to Jewish theosophy. This is, perhaps, the favorite and certainly the most recurring subject of the *Zohar*, and it is this also which was destined to receive fuller development than any other in the later literature of Kabbalism. The history of its growth is also worth noting. Pre-existence and the subdivision of the spiritual nature are found in the Talmud, but the *Sepher Yetzirah* has nothing to tell us on the subject, and there is very little in the first commentators on that treatise. It may be said, with considerable truth, that the book and its connections

This study of spiritual essences is a central theme of the Zohar and later Kabbalistic literature, though it is absent from the Sepher Yetzirah.</summary>

were concerned rather with the physical forces which produced the universe, but the commentaries at least are wide-ranging enough to have included it in their scheme if they had anything to say upon the subject. It remains, therefore, that the fascinating hypothesis with which we are dealing here is mainly a late growth. The distinction between a holy intelligence and an animal soul in man is found, however, in the "Book of Concealment,"<note>"When the inferior man descends (namely, into this world) two spirits are found (in him), according to the heavenly form. Man (therefore) is constituted from the two sides, the right and the left. As from the right side he has a holy mind, so from the left he has an animal soul." The extension of the left side was the consequence of the Fall. —"Book of Concealment," chapter 4, paragraphs 7-9.</note> which, so far as can be judged from its form, is the most ancient portion of the Zohar. The latter cycle may be regarded, broadly speaking, as the chief source of pneumatology in Kabbalism proper. The indications contained within it became a vast and ponderous system in the schools of Isaac de Luria and Moses of Cordova. This system has at all times exercised the greatest influence on occultists, and, perhaps primarily because it was made available in Latin by Rosenroth, has superseded that of the Zohar itself. Franck states that it is not true Kabbalism and hints that it is full of distorted rabbinical fantasies, but it may be doubted whether there is any real standard of criticism. In any case, the later speculations are founded on the Zohar, and the following brief sketch contains the general elements of the subject.

We have seen that the world of *Briah* is that of so-called Creation, that is, of the emanation of creative forces. These forces are the *Elohim*, and *Briah* is

It would not be exact to say that the archangels are *<term>Elohim</term>*, much less that Michael, Raphael, Gabriel, <term>Metatron</term>, and so forth, are deities according to the <term>Kabalah</term>.* The system is much too complex to allow for such clear identifications. In a general way, it may perhaps be affirmed that the intelligent forces of the <term>Briatic</term> world, when adopted, so to speak, by the divine world, may be regarded as the *<term>Elohim</term>*. Thus, according to <term>Kabalism</term>, the three men who appeared to Abraham in the valley of Mamre to announce the impending destruction of the cities of the plain were three archangels, but they were also *<term>Adni</term>*, the Lord, for they were the forms taken by the Divinity.

Regarding the hierarchy of spiritual beings outside humanity, we encounter various classifications by different <term>Kabalistic</term> writers, the sources of which must be traced back to <term>Talmudic</term> times; but, concerning the descending scale specifically, later authorities do not hesitate to contradict <term>Zoharic</term> statements. # The Doctrinal Content of the Kabbalah

The tenth order required to complete the Sephirotic attribution is found in the <term>ISHIM</term>, or the blessed souls of righteous men, corresponding to *<term>Malkuth</term>* and the great multitude of the redeemed seen by St. John in the Apocalypse.

These orders are also summarized in the concept of a third Adam, *<term>Yetzirah</term>*, represented by the *<term>Malkuth</term>* of the Yetziratic world—man in the likeness of the angels; in a word, the unfallen Adam of Genesis.

The world of *<term>Assiah</term>*, or of matter, is the one into which Adam descended at the Fall, the abode of the evil spirits, the Shells, Envelopes, and *Cortices* of the <term>Kabbalah</term>.<note>For information on Kabbalistic demonology, see *Die Kabbala. Ihre Hauptlehren und ihr verhältniss zu Christenthum.* Innsbruck, 1885.</note> It contains the orders of retrograde spirits corresponding by inversion to the angels of *<term>Yetzirah</term>*, and the arch-demons corresponding in the same manner to the archangels of the Briatic world.<note>But there are also many material correspondences that are not related to shells and demons.</note> They are usually listed as follows:

I. <term>THAUMIEL</term>, the "doubles of God," said to be two-headed and named so because they pretend to be equal to the Supreme Crown. This is properly the title of the averse *<term>Sephirah</term>* corresponding to *<term>Kether</term>*. The cortex is <term>CATHARIEL</term>, according to the supplements of the <term>Zohar</term>. Satan and Moloch are said to be the arch-demons, but the attributions are hopelessly confused throughout, partly due to the obscure classifications of the <term>Zohar</term> and the contradictions of later <term>Kabbalists</term>.

II. <term>CHAIGIDIEL</term>, a term connected with the meaning of *placenta*, or, according to other authori-

and appears freely in the Talmuds. <note>“The immortality of the soul and the resurrection of the body appear in the Talmud as tenets of the Synagogue. They form the thirteenth and last article in the profession of faith of Maimonides.”—Leroy-Beaulieu, *Israel Among the Nations*, p. 17. This is not quite accurate, as that article concerns the resurrection only: “I firmly believe that there will be a resurrection of the dead, at the time when it shall please the Creator, blessed be His name!” —M. Friedländer, *Text Book of the Jewish Religion*. 4th ed. London. 1896.</note> Occultists, remembering that Moses was educated in all the wisdom of the Egyptians, find it impossible to believe that he knew nothing of doctrines known throughout Egypt. Consequently, they hold that he communicated them secretly to an initiated circle, through whom they were preserved by oral tradition. Others favor the idea that these doctrines were acquired by the Jews in Babylon. During the Greco-Egyptian period, it was naturally impossible for the learned rabbis of Alexandria to be unaware of the great speculation regarding a future life. One way or another, it was inevitable that the Jews would acquire this knowledge, which they did; the specific date or circumstances are a minor issue about which there can be no certainty. The doctrine taught by the Talmud, while recognizing five divisions of the soul with names familiar to <term>Kabalism</term>, is relatively simple. For example, it lacks the philosophical character found in Philo; even those who see Greek influence in early <term>Kabalism</term> must admit that its <term>pneumatology</term>, despite accounting for pre-existence, shows very little trace of <term>Platonism</term>. <note>For a good summary of <term>Kabalistic</term> <term>pneumatology</term>, the German student may consult, among others, Leiningen’s *Seelenlehre der Qabalah*. divides man into body, soul and spirit,* and thus the triad reigns in things below as in those which are above. These divisions are the animal nature, *Nephesh*;† the rational nature, *Ruach*; and the seat of individuality, *Neshamah*. The system is, however, in reality far more complicated. In the first place, *Nephesh* is the animal soul rather than the physical body, or, more exactly, it contains also the triad. There is thus a *Neshamah* of *Nephesh*, which is the principle of the whole, there is a *Ruach* of *Nephesh*, and a *Nephesh* of *Nephesh*, which is the physical part.‡ The whole together is, according to the Zohar, the living man in this world.§ It may be gathered also from the same work that the natural man is complete in this one division, and that it depends upon himself to attain or receive the others.|| The true

### The Doctrinal Content of the Kabalah

*Ruach*, or the rational principle, and the true *Neshamah*, or spiritual individuality, are for those "who deserve to perform the work of the Master." *Neshamah* corresponds to *Briah*, *Ruach* to *Yetzirah*, and *Nephesh* to *Assiah*. The union of *Ruach* with the natural man constitutes a state to which the Kabbalists give the term *Chiah*. The union of *Neshamah* with both adds another principle called *Jechidah*. *Jechidah*, *Chiah*, and *Neshamah* are also said to be the highest triad. The doctrine of the four worlds suggests, however, that there is an even more exalted part of man, corresponding to *Atziluth*, of which the *Neshamah* is only a reflected shadow. This is called *Tsure*, which signifies "Prototype," and it never leaves its exalted home in the archetypal world. It is connected to the *Neshamah* by an invisible thread, and the aspiration of the lower for the higher opens the path of ecstasy.* The doctrine of the Mystic Marriage in Christian transcendentalism has similarities with some developments of this theory. But its immediate connections within Israel are with the Ten Degrees of contemplation described in the Babylonian Talmud, and with the later ritual of the *Pardes*.

In the hands of the later Kabbalists, Zoharic pneumatology became even more complex. The Sephirotic attribution of the triple triad and the supernal part is,

<meta>This page continues the critical evaluation of Kabalistic doctrines, specifically discussing why systems of emanation like the Sephiroth are considered insufficient explanations for the universe in a modern philosophical context.</meta>

87
# The Doctrinal Content of the Kabalah

There is much in its modern presentation by some occultists, who most vigorously defend its antiquity and even its divine origin, which helps to place it outside the realm of philosophical consideration; in its fundamental nature, it is the common inheritance of all human speculation; it needs neither revelation nor oral tradition to perpetuate it. The doctrine of the *Sephiroth*, on the other hand, is at most only slightly better than any other system of emanation, and no such theory can be accepted as a satisfactory attempt to explain the universe. In the eras when these theories were possible, they were serious and excellent in their intent, and to that extent, their interest is permanent for the history of human thought. To revive them today is beyond all reason. Just as the necessity of final causation, along with all the difficulties it involves, is not resolved by turning to evolution, so the transition from infinite to finite, from eternal to temporal, from absolute perfection to the imperfect order of the physical and moral world—from God, in a word, to the material—is not assisted by assuming stages between them. This kind of compromise belongs to a period of human thought which has utterly passed away. It is the same with the doctrine of the two Countenances; it was admirable for its time as an *eirenicon* between the God of the philosophers and the God of ancient Israel, but today there is not much need to bridge that gulf. Occultists in particular—who have abandoned orthodox faith, who are also very proud of this fact, and very resolute about its importance—are less in need than others of such a compromise. Speaking generally, the Kabalah is

<summary>The author critiques the Kabalistic doctrine of the Sephiroth as an outdated system of emanation that fails to bridge the gap between the infinite and the material. He argues that while historically significant, these concepts are no longer necessary for modern thinkers or occultists who have moved beyond traditional religious frameworks.</summary>
<keywords>Kabalah, Sephiroth, emanation, final causation, evolution, two Countenances, eirenicon, occultism, philosophy</keywords>

an attempt to give depth and significance to a form of religious faith which occultism has, at least in its literal aspects, agreed to set aside. Its pneumatology <note>the study of spirits or spiritual beings</note> is important to those who take ceremonial magic seriously, and the doctrine of the power within words—implied in the doctrine of the Instruments of Creation—is much in favor with a certain school of occultism. However, taken altogether, the content of the Kabalah does not possess the momentous character that has been attributed to it by those whose beliefs have invested it with a sense of living interest. The next stage of our inquiry is to determine, as far as possible, its claims to antiquity.

# BOOK III

## SOURCE AND AUTHORITY OF THE KABALAH

### *ARGUMENT*

The two main cycles of Kabalistic literature, despite destructive criticism, can be traced by their materials to Talmudic times. There is no reason why the "Book of Formation" should not be the work of Rabbi Akiba, as tradition asserts. There is no solid evidence to support the theory that Rabbi Moses de Leon wrote the Zohar at the end of the thirteenth century. At the same time, the belief among occult students that these works represent a tradition dating from an early period of history also has little to support it. Attempts to refer the Kabalah directly to some prior philosophical system must be largely set aside; it has its influences everywhere, but its similarities to other systems are due to a natural likeness between independent conclusions on the fundamental problems of existence. Prior Jewish influence through the schools of Aristobulus and Philo must not be overlooked, but it also must not be exaggerated. The Kabalah is *sui generis* <note>unique; in a class of its own</note>. It has its scholastic connections, and it has its Islamic correspondences. If there is any leaning in a particular direction, its sphere of influence has been Christian rather than Jewish.

---

## I. DATE AND DOCTRINE OF THE "BOOK OF FORMATION"

WE are now in possession of the most important elements of Kabalistic doctrine and the main points of its philosophical instruction. There is much—very much—more in its literature, some of which must be set

The author suggests the Kabalah is a unique system with diverse influences, particularly noting its impact on Christian thought.</summary>
<keywords>Book of Formation, Sepher Yetzirah, Rabbi Akiba, Zohar, Moses de Leon, Talmudic, Aristobulus, Philo, sui generis, Christian Kabalah</keywords>

aside altogether from consideration in a work that would exceed its purpose if it entered into abstruse and technical matters, while some has been postponed until a later stage of our study; because, although it is highly important for a right understanding of the subject, it does not involve the points with which we are concerned at the beginning. First among these points is whether, and to what extent, we are justified—by evidence that can be produced openly—in regarding the literature containing the doctrines summarized in the preceding sections as having an authentic character, or the doctrines themselves as part of a tradition preserved in Israel from very early times.

For this purpose, it will be convenient to view the literature as divisible into four classes: (1) The “Book of Formation”; (2) The commentaries on that work which preceded the public appearance of the Zohar; (3) The Zohar itself; (4) The writings following it.*

Reports of an esoteric tradition in Israel did not begin to circulate through Christendom until the fourteenth century, and this, as we shall see later on, is explained by the fact that the chief collection of its archives was unknown, at least generally, within the Jewish community itself until about 1290 A.D. The work of Creation—that is, the Sepher Yetzirah—was known, as we have reason to believe, to at least one Christian student as far back as the middle of the ninth century, but there was no significance attached to it for Christendom. The Sepher Yetzirah is supposed to embody a tradition handed down from the time of Abraham, and there is no doubt that the uncritical spirit of several centuries depicted the patriarch as its author. This does not seem, however, as some modern criticism has loosely assumed,* to have been the view universally adopted by the Jewish scholars who accepted the document. That he received and transmitted it was undoubtedly believed, but the work itself does not claim to have been put into writing until after the destruction of Jerusalem, and tradition has attributed its formal authorship to Rabbi Akiba ben Joseph, the pupil of R. Joshua ben Chananja, who was himself the successor (and once the opponent) of Rabban Gamaliel. There is nothing highly improbable in this attribution, though it reaches us late in history. Akiba was a mystic whose ideas were in complete agreement with the structure of the Sepher Yetzirah, and he is the reputed author of another work dealing with the mysteries of the Hebrew alpha-

Similarly, Ginsburg says that it claims to be a monologue of the patriarch. It does nothing of the sort; however, the fifth chapter mentions "Abraham our father." Naturally, the legend of its patriarchal origin quickly became established. We must next distinguish between the date that may be guessed for the treatise and the date that must perhaps be assigned to the teaching contained within it. Do we have any ground for believing that the doctrine of the *Sepher Yetzirah* is older than the Egyptian captivity, as its legend claims? This question must be answered with a firm no. The doctrine being discussed emphasizes the sacred and divine nature of the Hebrew alphabet, and we have no justification for assuming that the art of writing was known to Abraham; every probability is against it and every authority agrees on this point. But the *Sepher Yetzirah* contains, at least by implication, the doctrine of an occult power and holiness inherent in certain divine names, and we know that this belief is very ancient in humanity, as it is found at an early period in Chaldea, Akkadia, and so forth. It is distressing to modern intelligence, but it is of great antiquity, and as it belongs to those countries with which Israel was in contact, we have reason to think that it became part of the religious heritage of the Hebrew people long before the *Sepher Yetzirah*, the *Alphabet of Akiba*, or the *Mishna* itself were even imagined. The alleged traces of Kabalism in writers of known dates prior to that event may likewise be left until we deal with the documents in sequence. We may, therefore, proceed at once to the absorbing problems connected with the Book of Splendour. Chief among these are the vital questions: (1) Whether modern criticism is right in ascribing the Zohar to the thirteenth century as its period, and to Rabbi Moses Shem Tob de Leon as its author. (2) Whether we have evidence that at least some of its doctrine existed at a much earlier period, or—as its legend states—at the time of the Roman Emperor Antoninus. To determine these points, we would do well to glance first of all at the history of the criticism directed at this vast document.

We will find very little help from the perspective of contemporary Jewry regarding either point. The Sepher Yetzirah was known and accepted before documentary criticism can be said to have been born; similarly, when the Zohar was promulgated, it was among a mixed audience who either accepted or rejected it based on *a priori* grounds. Those who hated the yoke of Aristotle, which Abraham ben David Ha Levi, Aben Ezra, and Moses Maimonides would have placed on the neck of Jewry, gave it a joyful welcome;<note>The opposition between the two systems is best shown by this fact. For all Christian students, the Kabalah was essentially the <term>Zohar</term>, and, as we will see later, the role attributed to it was almost entirely evangelical. This is to say, the discovery that a secret doctrine supposedly existed in Israel from time immemorial—one that seemed to contain analogies or even identities with the fundamental dogmas of Christianity—placed the Jews so clearly in the wrong, by their own evidence, that their conversion was considered inevitable.<note>“Some Christians have also valued them (meaning the Kabalistic books and their related works) because they found them more favorable to the Christian religion than the recent commentaries of the Rabbis. However, they failed to consider that these same allegorical books are filled with an infinite number of ridiculous fables, and that Jewish superstition is much more clearly proven by them than are the mysteries of our religion. William Postel has deceived several theologians in this matter by claiming to find Christianity in the books of the Zohar.”—Richard Simon, *Critical History of the Old Testament*, p. 371.</note> Thus, the antiquity of the tradition was not challenged in Christendom at that time; nor was it a period when documentary criticism was practiced with any intensity. The fourteenth century made the serious, yet excusable, mistake of assuming that most authors actually wrote the books attributed to them. They accepted the claims of the <term>Zohar</term> for much the same reason they were convinced of the antiquity of Homer. Given the state of scholarship at the time, to have challenged one might have opened an abyss beneath the other, potentially drawing all ancient literature into a shared uncertainty. The gullibility, or at least the incapacity, of early students has been more than compensated for by the spirit that has guided later critics of the <term>Kabalah</term>. I must confess that in some cases they seem, in their own way, to have prejudged the question much as that hardworking bibliographer Julius Bartolocci prejudged it in the seventeenth century. It was offensive to the dignity of the Latin Church to suppose that there was a rival tradition, full of enlightenment and wisdom, preserved unknown to the Church within the rejected house of Israel. fore said out of hand that the <term>Kabalah</term>, represented by the <term>Zohar</term>, is a forgery of the thirteenth century. We must endeavor to comprehend precisely what is involved in this standpoint.

There are some literary fabrications which do not need a high degree of scholarship to expose them, for they may be said to betray themselves, often at every point. In the department of *belles lettres* it is sufficient to mention the so-called Rowley poems. These, as everyone is well aware, were forgeries pure and simple, and their disguise is so entirely spurious that it can be peeled off without any difficulty. It is not necessary to add that they had their believers, and not further back than the days of the Bell edition of Chatterton, the race of Rowleyites still had a few survivors, for we find the editor describing their characteristics in terms which have a wider application than he was concerned with at the moment. A true Rowleyite, he says, is not open to conviction, and the statement holds true in the case of all persistent defenders of spurious literary productions. The position of the Rowley manuscripts is fairly paralleled by that of many occult documents, among which, as typical instances, we may select the handbooks of Ceremonial Magic. There are no works which betray themselves more transparently and abundantly than the “Key of Solomon” and the “Sacred Magic of Abramelin the Mage,” and yet they possess to this day their believers—enthusiasts for the good faith of their claims to a high antiquity or a Hebrew origin, as the case may be.

There are again some fabrications which possess a certain basis in fact, over which a mass of forgery

One ready instance in point is found in the poems of Ossian, for which there was undoubtedly a nucleus of floating Gaelic tradition that was wrought into his fabrication by Macpherson. The result may briefly deceive even sound scholarship, but its full exposure is only a matter of time. In this case, the epic of “Wallace” was fatal to the possibility of *Fingal*. Perhaps the Latin alchemical writings attributed to Geber may be regarded as typical instances in occult literature of this form of fraud. They bear scarcely any resemblance to the Arabic originals, but such originals do exist.

Finally, there are certain works which may or may not be fabrications, but they either incorporate so much genuine material belonging to the field of literature they pretend to represent, or else are so skillfully constructed that the balance of probability is poised almost equally concerning them, and it is nearly impossible to arrive, by impartial methods, at a final determination of their claims. I do not know whether there is any good instance in *belles lettres* of this kind of alleged fabrication. James Hogg’s “Jacobite Relics of Scotland” is perhaps the nearest approach to a parallel. That collection undoubtedly contains a large proportion of genuine material, but it is suspected that the “Ettrick Shepherd” <note>James Hogg’s pseudonym</note> supplied a portion of the collection through his own skill in verse-craft. Criticism, though it has not concerned itself very seriously with the matter, is perhaps fairly divided on the question. In occult literature, we have several notable examples of this suspected writing which has not been exposed to the satisfaction of the impartial mind.

<header>The Doctrine and Literature of the Kabalah</header>

For example, a few of the Hermetic books, which are usually classified by scholars as products of the Alexandrian period—and therefore post-Christian—are held by others to represent occult traditions of considerable antiquity. I do not think the case has been decided for all time regarding some of these works. But the most renowned of all these instances is the one we are concerned with here: the Kabalah itself. Destructive criticism has maintained that its most important work was forged at the end of the thirteenth century by a single writer of little intellectual repute. As we shall see, there is no positive evidence on this point worth mentioning, and the presumptive evidence is not at all strong. There is very good proof of late writing, but the theory that Moses de Leon fabricated the *Zohar* places an almost impossible burden on the shoulders of that questionable person. This theory is generally the work of writers who have not given enough consideration to the likely existence of much of the traditional doctrine summarized in the *Zohar* for several centuries before its appearance.

We admit, therefore, that Kabalistic literature belongs to a suspicious category, but how we are to view its indictment is a different question. The fact that this inquiry has been undertaken will indicate that, in my own opinion, the hostile critic must change the nature of his accusation. Regarding its material, and usually regarding its motive, spurious literature belongs to the class that is most easily explained. It falls into line readily. Where there are complex workings of the human mind, as in the *Zohar*, sincerity is

<summary>The author disputes the claim that the Zohar was a 13th-century forgery by Moses de Leon, arguing that the text likely contains much older traditional doctrines. He suggests that while the literature is viewed with suspicion, its complexity points toward a more sincere origin than simple fraud.</summary>
<keywords>Kabalah, Zohar, Moses de Leon, Hermetic books, Alexandrian period, literary forgery, occult tradition, destructive criticism</keywords>

--- Page 129 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that the Zohar is a complex compilation of older traditions rather than a simple forgery, moving into a discussion of its specific date and the role of Moses de Leon.</meta>

<page-num>103</page-num>
<header>Source and Authority of the Kabalah</header>

usually present. The Kabalah is much too unique in its structure to be attributed to a single author. So far as there is evidence on the subject, that evidence tends to show that it grew, and that in its final state it was neither entirely old nor completely new, but doctrine more or less familiar or derived from familiar doctrine.* These facts are now being recognized outside occult circles, in the academic institutions which rule general opinion. Of this Dr. Schiller-Szinessy offers the best evidence when he observes that “almost all that the latest critics have said concerning the age of the various Targumim and Midrashim,” including the Zohar, “will have to be retracted.”

### III. THE DATE AND AUTHORSHIP OF
### THE BOOK OF SPLENDOUR

The theory which accounts for the Zohar on the grounds that it was written by Moses de Leon in the latter half of the thirteenth century does not depend merely on internal evidence; it is not exclusively an inference made by modern criticism from allusions to late events found here and there in the work; it is

---

<note>This is very nearly the position of Solomon Munk, who maintains that the Zohar and its connections—that is, the various tracts and fragments which enter into the compilation—are not the inventions of an impostor, but that ancient documents were used by the editor, including Midrashim which are no longer extant.—*Mélanges de Philosophie, Juive et Arabe.* Paris. 1859, p. 275 *et seq.* In spite of this, Munk did not consider the Zohar, at least in its present form, as predating the seventh century, but rather that the Kabalistic developments which it represents took place in the thirteenth century, and were either influenced by Gebirol or by sources common to both.—*Ibid.* pp. 276, 277.</note>

There is no evidence, however, that he employed anyone; but if he worked single-handedly, he could not have “made large sums,” as alleged, by so slow a process. It will seem almost incredible that in this work, which so elaborately defends the Zohar, a narrative should appear which represents it as an imposture devised for mercenary motives by Moses de Leon; yet at first sight, this is the case, and as such, it has been accepted by those who challenge the Kabalah. The explanation is actually simple: the narrative in question is a fragment, and the proof that its missing conclusion is really to the credit of the Zohar—and exonerates the transcriber of that work—is found in the fact that the person whose adventures it relates subsequently became certain that the Zohar was not a sordid forgery, as he embodied some of its principles in one of his own treatises. The narrative is concerned with the adventures of Isaac de Acco,* a disciple of Nachmanides, who claimed to perform miracles by a transposition of the Hebrew letters according to a system which he pretended he had learned from the angels. Therefore, independently of the Zohar, he was a Kabbalist after his own fashion, and, we may suspect, a visionary. In either case, he was at Novara, in Italy, around 1293, when he heard that a Spanish Rabbi was in possession of the original Zohar manuscripts, and, being very anxious to see them, he made a journey into Spain. There he learned by report that the erudite Moses Nachmanides was said to have transmitted the book to his son in Catalonia from Palestine,† but that the ship which carried it was driven by the wind to Aragon‡ or to Catalonia, and the precious volume came into the hands of Moses de Leon. At Valladolid, Isaac de Acco made the acquaintance of the latter, who declared under oath that he was in possession of the manuscript and that it was at his home in Avila, where he would show it to Isaac. They undertook a journey together with this object, but Moses de Leon died at Arevalo on the way.§ His companion proceeded to Avila, and there

I have purposely passed over several minor details in this brief account that have raised suspicions about the honesty of the narrative, as it is unnecessary to complicate the issues. The point is that it ends with two solemn testimonies to the authenticity of the <term>Zohar</term>, and based on the course he subsequently took, Isaac de Acco must have decided to abide by them. Assuming the narrative is truthful, the evidence that was dismissed as insufficient by the only person who recorded it cannot be accepted by impartial criticism unless supported by other considerations. So far, therefore, as the account in the *Sepher Yuhasin* is concerned, it is not proved that Moses de Leon wrote the <term>Zohar</term> “out of his own head.”* Zakut himself mentions an opinion that he did write it under the guidance of the Writing Name, that is, by angelic revelation, but I do not believe it is necessary to discuss this possibility.

The state of the case as it stands is confusing, and most people who have taken part in the controversy have been led into more or less contradiction. The internal evidence against the Zohar may be reduced under the following heads:

(1) It refers to the vowel points which are alleged to have been invented in post-Talmudic times.<note>Elias Levita, a German Jew of the sixteenth century, was one of the first to affirm the late introduction of the points, which he attributed to the Jews of Tiberias about the beginning of the sixth century. Ginsburg, however, adopts Levita's theory, with the modification that they were introduced by the Karaite, Rabbi Mocha, at the end of the sixth century. David Levi, on the other hand, makes their acceptance by the Karaite Jews a proof of their antiquity, because they were "professed enemies to tradition and innovation." (5) It is written in Aramaic, whereas at the period to which it is ascribed Aramaic was the common language, while Hebrew was used in religious writings.

On this subject, the question raised by Franck in 1843 remains relevant and still unanswered:—How could Moses de Leon, at the beginning of the fourteenth century, discuss matters of the most elevated order in an idiom which the most distinguished scholars had for so long been content merely to understand and which, according to this hypothesis, had not produced a single work capable of serving as his model?—*La Kabbale*, p. 104.</note>

From these objections and these answers, the general conclusion must be that the internal evidence for the late origin of the majority of the *Zohar*, as it currently stands, lacks any real force. The two lists have by no means exhausted the difficulties or the counter-evidence; regarding these, even today, Franck remains in many cases the best and certainly the most clear-headed expositor. The absence of Christian influence—if not the absolute absence of all reference to Christianity—along with the lack of Aristotelian influence and certain points of the argument regarding the dialect in which the work is written, seem to be as compelling now as they were originally in 1843. But the strength of the case in favor of the *Zohar* is also the basis for the primary objection against it. It does quote later authorities, but this may simply show that it grew over time, much like the Talmudic writings and several of the canonical Hebrew books. <meta>This page continues the investigation into the Zohar's authorship, focusing on the criticisms leveled against Moses de Leon and the mixed reception of the work upon its publication.</meta>

<page-num>114</page-num>

word of its late name, but by the title of the Secret Learning, and by other titles which have been mentioned in this section. It would exceed the scope of an introductory work to pursue the subject further. The specific considerations are, of course, highly technical, and there are some arguments on both sides which it is wise to refrain from emphasizing. One of these is the argument that Moses de Leon was an unlikely person to have written such a work as the *Zohar*, because he was intellectually and morally unfit.<note>Dr. Schiller-Szinessy shows that he was proud of the authorship of his books, and therefore unlikely to hide his hand in the composition of any; however, this argument also must not be overemphasized. The same writer calls him an inferior Kabbalist, and it seems admitted by everyone that his original books are of poor quality. From these works, Jellinek has extracted passages that are parallel to others in the *Zohar*, and some critics have concluded from this that the authors were identical. In any other field of research, such parallels would be considered to prove nothing.</note> I have noted that he was an unlikely candidate, but possibilities of this kind can only be determined by the outcome. Many great books have been produced by persons who were previously considered improbable; and after all, at best, we know Moses de Leon only through the testimony of a hostile relative. There is no doubt that the *Zohar* was, to some extent, suddenly presented to the Jewish people at the time of its publication. Its reception was mixed; it was the kind of reception that would be given to a work that was old in its materials but unfamiliar—to a greater or lesser degree—in its form. This is sufficient to explain any silence from previous authorities, while the individual who distributed the copies may have had a hand in shaping those materials and establishing that form.<note>And by those who accept this view, it is believed that he intervened only to distort it.</note>

<summary>This page evaluates the likelihood of Moses de Leon's authorship of the Zohar, arguing that while he seemed an improbable candidate, he may have played a role in shaping and distributing older materials. The text notes that the Zohar received a mixed reception upon its publication due to its unfamiliar form despite its ancient contents.</summary>

## IV. THE AGE OF ZOHARIC TRADITION

It must not be supposed that the field of criticism is entirely occupied by a theory of pure fraud, or that this theory has always focused on the same person.<note>Basnage is inclined to attribute the original Zohar to the tenth century and, following Bartolocci (*Bibliotheca Rabbinica*, vol. iv. p. 82), depicts Moses de Leon as being in possession of several copies which he expanded.—*History of the Jews*, vol. ii. 781; vol. v. 1775, 1776.</note> The most popular suspect is, of course, Moses de Leon, because he is reported to have circulated the Zohar, but occasionally he appears as the tool of other conspirators. Thus, Samuel Cohen maintains that the Zoharic writings were composed by a gathering of converted Rabbis, convened for that purpose in a Spanish monastery, who employed Moses as their publisher; thus the Church itself seems to figure as an accomplice.<note>At the opposite extreme was Christianus Schoettgenius in his substantial work, *Horae Hebraicae et Talmudicae in Theologiam Judaeorum Dogmaticam antiquam et orthodoxam de Messia impensa* (Hebrew and Talmudic Hours Spent on the Ancient and Orthodox Dogmatic Theology of the Jews concerning the Messiah), 2 vols., Dresden and Leipzig, 1733, 1742. See vol. ii. *Rabbinicorum Lectionum Liber Secundus* (Second Book of Rabbinic Readings), ch. ii., teaching that Rabbi Simeon ben Jochai, the author of the Book of Zohar, followed the Christian religion. There are eight points to the argument, the most important being that the Zohar contains the precise, orthodox doctrine concerning the Messiah and His divine and human nature, and this not in one place or mysteriously, but in many places and openly.</note> Others, like M. H. Landauer, argue that the true author was Abraham ben Samuel Abulafia, while the voice of Dr. Graetz is raised in favor of the school of Abraham ben David of Beaucaire. Abraham ben David of Posquière and Isaac the Blind are also suggested names, to whom it is certain, in any case, that Kabbalism owed something of its development and momentum. Meanwhile, this extreme opinion in all its varieties is balanced by

counterviews which also denaturalize the literature. It may be suspected, therefore, with reason that on both sides there is an error of enthusiasm: there are the "children of intelligence" who look to find the secret doctrine of Judea a mere transcript from that of Egypt, or whatever land is for them the wellspring of all truth and all truly sacred knowledge. These remember, for example, that Abraham was in Egypt, and, accepting at once the fairy-tale attribution of the "*Book of Formation*" to the patriarch, conclude that this document is older than the *Ritual of the Dead*. It is useless to reason with those whose confidence is not shaken in the face of impossibilities, whose imagination can bridge all gaps in evidence with fantastic suppositions. On the other hand, there is the crude criticism which dismisses a literature with a single stroke of the pen into the region of forgery and imposture, just as it dismisses all occult psychology into that of imposture or hallucination. It does not matter that this criticism is always in disgrace. It proved the city of Troy to be a solar myth until the city of Troy was excavated; it undermined, as it believed, the book of Daniel until fresh archaeological discoveries cast it into the pit which it had dug. It is truly no less stupid, and it is far less engaging, than the opposite excess.<note>Its typical representative is Graetz, and one can hardly guess by what principle he was guided in his estimate of *Moses de Leon*. It is the height of exaggeration; the account in the *Yuhasin* is so transcendentalized that it almost exceeds recognition.</note>

The importance of the *Zohar* does not depend so much upon the date of its documents as on the possible claims of its tradition. The collapse of its claim to antiquity, in that respect, and this only, will

It is clear that the speculations, for example, of medieval or later Rabbis—if we ignore the dishonesty of false claims—will have at most some literary, historical, or exegetical interest. However, this interest differs in kind rather than degree from that which must attach to a tradition which connects with the distant past. We are therefore more concerned with determining the state in which modern criticism has left the content of the *Zohar* than the form in which it is presented to us. The early students of the work, who accepted and defended its antiquity, did not make this helpful distinction, and in many instances modern critics who are hostile to the work do not make it either. At first glance, the history of Kabalistic criticism naturally predisposes one against any hypothesis of antiquity, because such antiquity seems to have been admitted in days when scholarship was insufficiently equipped to decide such a question. In the light of more complete knowledge, it might be thought that the claim has expired, or remains only as a pious belief prevailing among an uncritical minority—as a few people are always found whose mental bias predisposes them to defend discredited views. In such a case, however, an indiscriminate rejection is hardly less superficial than an unquestioning acceptance of an unproven claim. The history of debated questions of this kind teaches another lesson: the closest approximation to truth is usually found in the middle ground between extreme views. Now, in the history of Zoharic criticism, we find that the old students not only accepted the tradition's claim to antiquity and were inclined to understand the genealogy more or less literally, but also that they

This reference to antiquity—as something that deserves to be considered separately from the date of publication—explains what I mean by the moderate middle view in which the truth must be sought. If we fail entirely in this regard, we may consider the case closed.

Now, I believe that a careful and unbiased comparison of all the evidence will lead us to conclude that there are elements of ancient doctrine in the *Zohar*; their exact antiquity is, in part, highly speculative, but it is quite sufficient to invest them with considerable interest. Like the *Sepher Yetzirah*, some of it may even be traced back to the period of Esdras. I refer here to the Yetziratic notions concerning the power of Divine Names, for this is found in the *Zohar* as it is abundantly found in the *Talmud*; a remainder of its teaching concerning angels and demons may also be an inheritance from Babylon. All of this, however, is the worthless part of the *Zohar*, just as it is the worthless, though curious, part of Talmudic literature.

With regard to the Scriptural interpretation which constitutes so large a portion of it, we will not be unreasonable by supposing that some of it may be a transmission from Talmudic times.* If we take the obscure but plentiful hints and references found in the Talmuds regarding the existence of a mystic tradition, and follow them through the extensive mystical literature that appeared between those works and the *Zohar* as we now have it, we shall be led not to the conclusion of the occultist—that there was a great body of secret doctrine which was revealed gradually—but rather that there was a kernel of tradition which was planted in the secret heart of Israel. This was watered and fostered by many, until the growth at length produced—not without a certain transformation and suddenness—the strange flower of the *Zohar*. Regarding its form, its most ancient part is probably the *Book of Concealment*, but it is completely unlikely that any significant portion could have existed in writing until after the sixth century, while the development of most of it is perhaps much later and subsequent to the latest date which can be ascribed to the *Sepher Yetzirah*.† It is

## V. ALLEGED SOURCES OF KABALISTIC DOCTRINE

We can now reach a specific conclusion regarding our subject and affirm that many of the materials collected in the Zohar are older than the time they were published. We cannot say if the Sepher Yetzirah dates much earlier than the ninth century.* But both works are connected to Talmudic times; therefore, within the span of the Christian era, there seems to have been an esoteric tradition in Israel.† Whether it existed before Christianity itself is the next focus of our investigation. At this point, the difficulties begin to multiply because the scope of research is extremely large, and it has been explored in every direction by successive generations of bold speculators. We must proceed step by step and should begin first with a general survey of the subject.

The doctrine of the Kabalah has been attributed to almost every philosophical and religious system of antiquity, and its points of similarity with each have been carefully charted. It has been argued that it derives from Akkadia, from India, from China, from ancient Egypt,‡ from Platonism and Neo-

---

<note>‡ This is the view most widely held among occultists. "It is in Egyptian science," says Stanislas de Guaita, "carried from..."</note>
