Platonism, from the categories of Aristotle, or from early Christian Gnosticism.* The most philosophical conclusion that can, I think, be drawn from all this conflicting evidence is that it is not derived from any one of them specifically and exclusively. Rather, it suggests that the human mind, when engaged with certain fundamental and perhaps insoluble problems of the universe, tends independently to reach conclusions that are similar and may even sometimes appear literally identical. The Kabalah is largely a result of such independent research; its results are mainly *sui generis*, yet they offer points of contact with other similar attempts across all ages and nations. We must, of course, distinguish the fundamental parts of the Kabalah from its later developments. The first class includes the doctrine of the Ten Emanations, that of *Ain Soph*, and those of the *Macroprosopus* and the *Microprosopus*. Some of these may reach far back into the history of post-Christian Jewish literature, indeed almost to Talmudic times. Subsequent developments possess a character derived from many sources, including the scholastic philosophy of Christian Europe during the Middle Ages.† *Ain Soph* is that ultimate concept of the Deity which is

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the philosophical origins of the Kabalah, specifically following the definition of *Ain Soph* as a concept reached through metaphysical reasoning.</meta>

<page-num>124</page-num>

reached by all metaphysics; it is not necessary to suppose that it was derived from Babylonian initiations during the exile of seventy years, or from Greek speculation at Alexandria; it may be regarded more reasonably as a product of the unfinished exile of the Christian centuries. But in either case, it is the ultimate point of theosophical speculation possible to the human mind, at which the mind always arrives. The doctrine of the *Sephiroth* is, in its turn, a very intelligible form of another widespread device of the world's thought when it seeks to bridge the gulf between the finite and the infinite, between absolute purity and that material world which, in one way or another, seems always to be regarded as unclean. The *Macro-prosopus* and the *Microprosopus*, whether late or early in Jewish literature, are late at least in the history of human speculation. They are an attempt to distinguish between God as He is in Himself and in His relation with His children. As might be expected, they are the most characteristic of Jewry and, as such, offer the least connection with any external system. Yet they have some points of contact. As regards each and all, the first point to be fixed firmly in the mind is that they are the natural results of legitimate intellectual inquiry. Given the times and the circumstances, they are the kind of speculative doctrine which one might have expected *a priori*.

When we remember the persistence of tradition which has always characterized the most persistent of all races, when we remember that the Jew of the Christian dispensation may be said to have

This civilization dates back probably earlier than the time of the Pyramids, was native and original, and from it Egypt borrowed much in the days of its greatest rulers.” — “Babylonian Discoveries,” *Edinburgh Review*, April, 1898.</note> just as the Jews of Babylon may later have had some contact with Confucianism in its earliest form. We may readily and reasonably admit that the Jew received influences everywhere and always retained what was received, provided that we grant him his own intellectual initiative in every case, and bear in mind that the process was always natural and informal, rather than arbitrary and conventional.

Philo, the Greek of Alexandria, to some extent Hellenized the Hebrew religion so that he might better Judaize the philosophy of Hellas. From this fusion there arose the nearest approach—if not in time and place, then at least in form and subject—to Kabalistic theosophy regarding its source in Jewry. There is no need in this elementary study to refer to Aristobulus, who a century before had received a similar calling. Philo, and the movement and mode of thought which he represents, cannot reasonably have been without an effect upon the literature of later ages in Jewry.<note>† I should observe here that Mr. Arthur Lillie, who has argued extensively for a process regarding the Buddhist origin of Christianity, has discovered in the Zohar not only the Trinity of Philo, but the Trinity of Buddhism. ...though the history of that influence and the mode of its transmission cannot be traced conclusively. We must not, however, fall into the error of supposing that the <term>Kabbalah</term> is Platonism derived through Philo and the Jewish school of Alexandria, or that it is Jewish tradition modified by Philo-ism. When we find in the *Sepher Yetzirah* the alphabetical symbols of the Logos used by God in the formation of the universe, it is very easy to attribute it to Greek influence, but the fact remains that the “Book of Formation” is essentially and characteristically Hebrew, and this fact lifts it altogether out of the category of Platonic succession. Yet we know where to look for the explanation of its points of contact. As regards the doctrine developed by the commentators on the *Sepher Yetzirah* prior to the appearance of the <term>Zohar</term>, as regards the literature which makes contact with these, and as regards the <term>Zohar</term> itself—to say nothing of the later literature, which consciously and openly had recourse to Greek sources—the case is much stronger.*

<meta>This page continues the comparison between the philosophy of Philo of Alexandria and the doctrines found in the Zohar and the wider Kabbalistic tradition, highlighting their shared views on the nature of God and the interpretation of Scripture.</meta>

<page-num>132</page-num>
<header>132 The Doctrine and Literature of the Kabbalah</header>

Philo insists on the antithesis between God and the material world, the infinite and the finite; so, let us say, does the <term>Zohar</term>, which may be taken to represent the entire literature. Philo affirms the absolute transcendence of God; so does Zoharic doctrine. Philo regards the divine nature as escaping definition in itself and as being without quality; <term>Kabbalism</term> denounces those who would attempt to describe God as He is in Himself, even by the attributes which He manifests. Philo's descriptions of God are all negative; compare the *latens Deitas* of the <term>Kabbalah</term>. Philo says that no name can be given to Him; all <term>Kabbalism</term> agrees. Philo regards the scriptural Deity as anthropomorphic and allegorizes all the descriptions, attributions, and manifestations of Deity found there; compare the doctrine of the Two Countenances, designed to explain the same anthropomorphisms. Philo regards the letter of Scripture as a veil; so does the <term>Zohar</term>. Philo interprets it literally or mystically according to his purpose; so does <term>Kabbalistic</term> exegesis. Philo regards the visible world as the gate to the unseen world; he believes in the possibility of an immediate contemplation of God, in the existence of an archetypal world, and that things seen are a counterpart of things unseen,\* in all of which we are enumerating specific points of <term>Kabbalistic</term> doctrine. These

---

<note>\* There is a twofold correspondence in <term>Kabbalism</term> between superior and inferior things: one transcendental, being that of phenomena with their archetypes in the noumenal world, and one natural in the narrower sense of the term, being that which is summed up in the axiom: "There is no herb on earth to which a certain star does not correspond in the heaven." See Kircher, *Mundus Subterraneus*, ii. 401*b*. The whole theory of natural magic is contained in this maxim.</note>

These analogies are too numerous, too close, and too consecutive to leave any room for doubt that the main points of <term>Kabbalistic</term> teaching existed earlier in the Jewish tradition, and we have further the explicit testimony of Philo regarding the existence of a mystic doctrine among the Jews. Spontaneity, initiation, and subsequent influences all remain unrestricted and are all necessary to explain the existence of the <term>Zohar</term> and its connections; however, its source is not necessarily Philo, much less Philo exclusively, but rather that which produced Philo. But more than anything, it is bold, independent speculation, wearing tradition like a veil that does not hide its essential individuality, and it is at times much closer to our own spirit than we would ever suspect from its form. Yet we may suspect this on philosophical grounds, for however much it is concealed behind the veil of symbolism, and however much it is distorted by strange visionary lenses, the sentiments and aspirations of humanity always share a common ground. Through the medium of the <term>Kabbalistic</term> system—under many covers and tinted by many fantastic colorings of art and artifice—we see that our own yearnings and longings find expression, in their own way, in this book of the words of the exile. Here is the justification of the ways of God to man, and here is the pious conviction of the believing heart that on the great day of the Lord, there shall be no shame for His children. Despite the darkness of our paths, we have rightly held that He is light; though we write Mercy in our hearts but see only Law and its inflexible order in everything around us, we shall one day know that it is Mercy on every side—the highest expression of the Law—or that Law is the order through which Divine Charity is manifested. It is in messages like this that the lasting beauty and significance of the <term>Kabbalah</term> are contained, not in the beard of *<term>Microprosopus</term>* or in the number of worlds suspended from the hair on the skull of *<term>Arikh Anpin</term>*. *<term>Gematria</term>* and *<term>Metathesis</term>* may be pastimes fit only for children, but the voice of the Rabbis of the *<term>Zohar</term>*—expressing the language of the heart of Israel—needs no *<term>Temurah</term>* to explain its meaning. It is through the resonance of such utterances that the true believer today is made electrically aware that the Holy Synods were composed of men who are our brothers.

As this view implicitly dismisses the claim of divine authorship and places the theory of an original tradition among fables, it also prevents us from supposing that <term>Kabbalistic</term> doctrines are the work of any single mind.

One feels instinctively, without any need for evidence, that these things are not and cannot be the unaided work of <term>Moses de Leon</term>.* They are a development and a result. However, as it is admitted that the <term>Zohar</term> took its present shape at a late period, it may credibly have received part of it from the hands of this Spanish Jew. That his other works are inferior is no argument. Cervantes wrote many worthless romances before and after the pinnacle of all chivalry. The *Galatea* did not make *Don Quixote* impossible. Likewise, <term>Beroalde de Verville</term> wrote books on alchemy which are despised even by alchemists, but he also wrote the *Moyen de Parvenir*. Every *magnum opus* is improbable beforehand, and the intellectual distance between the *Sorrows of Werther* and the second part of *Faust* is like the void between <term>Ain Soph</term> and <term>Malkuth</term>, which the <term>Sephiroth</term> were intended to fill.

But if all masterpieces are improbable beforehand, it is also true that they are impossible without precedents. There are certain dull old histories known to the *literati* which were necessary for the plays of Shakespeare. Likewise, the formulation of the <term>Zohar</term>

There is, of course, a point beyond which the reasonable critic will not pass. So far as it goes, we are on safe ground with the meager testimony of St. Agobard; with Rabbi Simeon Ben Jochai, we are on purely traditional ground, and it is not to be supposed for a moment that more authenticity resides in the *dramatis personae* of the Holy Synod than in those of the *Turba Philosophorum*. I do not mean that such names are entirely pretexts, for they may possess an honest basis in legend, but they are not literal or historical. They occupy a middle position between the script of a shorthand reporter and imaginary conversations like those of W. S. Landor.

## VI. ISLAMIC CONNECTIONS OF THE <term>KABBALAH</term>

When the Jew of the Exile sought a consolation in philosophy, and thus produced the higher part of <term>Kabbalism</term>, compounded of his traditions, his speculations, his external receptions, his longings, and the memories of his election and its glory, we must bear in mind that all exotics adjust themselves to their environment, not without certain changes even in the most persistent types. <meta>This page continues the discussion on how the Jewish people adapted to different environments during the Exile, specifically focusing on the development of Kabbalistic literature in Spain.</meta>

pological exotic in every country of the world. Because his endurance is so great that it is explained by a special law of Providence, we find that in all countries he has been modified enough to ensure his survival. Just as in physical matters and daily life, so too in the intellectual realm: he lost nothing, but he adopted much. The Jew of Salerno differed from the Jew of France, and the Jew of Spain presented contrasts to both. Without trying to add another hypothesis to the dozens already existing regarding the origin of <term>Kabbalism</term>, I intend to show that this literature is naturally, though only partially, explained by the characteristics of its birthplace.

Having allowed for originality within Jewish thought, and having noted its observed connections and similarities across distant times and places, it seems appropriate that we should now look at what was closest to it. Without arguing for or against the opinion that Israel may have possessed a tradition handed down orally from ancient times—for which we have enough evidence to assume its existence, but not enough to determine what it actually was—let us begin by considering where the <term>Kabbalistic</term> books first began to circulate. That was in Spain. Now, what was the environment of the Jews in the Peninsula during the period in question—say, from the ninth century onward? It differed significantly from the environment surrounding them in other countries of Western Europe. For Israel, Spain was not indeed a Garden of Paradise, but a kind of oasis in the great wilderness of the Exile, for the simple reason that

much of it was not under Christian rule at that time.<note>Similarly, the needs of Christian princes in Spain until the thirteenth century usually led them to protect the Jews.</note> The Spanish Jew enjoyed comparative immunity; he even possessed political influence and occasionally rose to high political power. Therefore, it is not surprising that Spain became a center of Jewish literature and philosophy. From there, Jewish treatises passed into France and Italy under the Arabic equivalents of their authors' names and were accepted as the speculations or teachings of the learned among the Muslims. Avicebron is a perfect example. There can be no doubt whatever that Islamic scholarship influenced the Rabbis,<note>The translation of the Talmud into Arabic by Rabbi Joseph, a disciple of Moses "the sack-clothed," during the reign of Hisham II, King of Córdoba, is the best evidence that can be cited on this point.</note> who in turn influenced the Muslim doctors.<note>Islamic mysticism is nearly contemporary with the mission of the great Islamic prophet. For example, the *Ghulat* sect, famous for the "extravagance" of its doctrines, is attributed to the time of Ali. See *Secret Societies of the Middle Ages*, London, 1846, pp. 29, 31.</note> Questions of priority and dominance can be set aside, as they are of no importance here.

We have already concluded that the Zohar presents the mystical thought of previous centuries in Israel in a transformed state. The traditional knowledge, whose existence in Talmudic times is evidenced, had been developed by many sources and influenced by many minds. There is reason to suppose that its core during the Christian era was first heard of in Palestine, which follows naturally if its connection to Rabbi Simeon is admitted.

Ben Jochai. But despite the legend that portrays the Zohar as being sent from Palestine by Nachmanides, all evidence points to Spain and the south of France as the primary locations for the final developments of the Kabalah. There is evidence to show that such influence was active outside the Zohar and perhaps prior to its existence in its current form. At that time, it was most likely part of the extensive influence of Avicebron. In post-Zoharic mysticism and in the commentaries on the Zohar written by Spanish Jews, this influence can be traced more thoroughly and clearly.

In no case does this justify the now-discredited criticism that would make the Zohar merely a reproduction or echo of Arabian theosophy, or that would regard all Kabalism as originating solely from Islamic mysticism combined with the Greek influence present in Islam. This was Tholuck's hypothesis. We are only concerned with the matter of character and tone; we have other criteria for judging the true significance of the doctrinal similarities between Sufis and Kabalists regarding the latent state of the Deity, the operation of the Divine Will at the beginning of creation, the emanation of the world, and so on. These analogies are interesting enough, and the Orientalist who first identified them was fully justified given the knowledge of his time.<note>See F. A. D. Tholuck: *Sufismus Sive Theosophia Persarum Pantheistica* (Sufism, or the Pantheistic Theosophy of the Persians), Berlin, 1831, chapter 5 *throughout*. Also *De Ortu Cabbalæ* (On the Origin of the Kabbalah), Hamburg, 1837.</note> As it may be of interest to cite a few—

<summary>This page argues that the Zohar's final development occurred in Spain and France rather than Palestine, influenced by thinkers like Avicebron. <page-num>140</page-num>
<header>The Doctrine and Literature of the Kabalah</header>

Regarding cases in point derived from other sources, let us take a fact—one of many concerning which we possess unshakeable testimony. About the middle of the fifteenth century, or, more exactly, from 1414 to 1492, there flourished a Sufi poet named Nuruddin Abdur-rahman, known as Jami of Herat, among whose works the *Seven Thrones* is most famous. One of the poems in this collection is titled "Salomon and Absal," a mystical story of earthly and heavenly love. In the epilogue to this poem, where the author unfolds his meaning, the following lines occur:

> The Incomprehensible Creator, when He
> created this world, created first of all
> the First Intelligence, the first of a chain
> of ten Intelligences, of which the last
> is the sole agent in our universe,
> called the Active Intelligence.

It may be admitted at once that if we are to accept the method and the quality of evidence that has satisfied various authorities until now—those who have referred Kabalism to definite sources in philosophy and religion—we are free to infer from this passage that around the year 1450, a Sufi poet as far from Spain as Herat was adapting the Sephirotic doctrine of the Kabalah with slight verbal variations. This was a century before the *Book of Formation* and the *Zohar* began to circulate through the medium of print. I have chosen this example because, being relatively recent, it proves nothing on its own; however, it provides a starting point for tracing a possible connection back between the mystical sects of Islam and the mystical sects of Israel.

<summary>The author cites the Persian Sufi poet Jami of Herat to demonstrate parallels between the Sephirotic doctrine and the "Ten Intelligences" of Islamic mysticism. This comparison serves as a starting point for investigating historical connections between Sufism and the Kabalah.</summary>

# Source and Authority of the Kabalah

Let us compare this for a moment with the doctrine developed in the “Celestial Desatir,” which has been described as “a very early attempt by the ancient Persians to form a cosmological theory.” <note>My knowledge is limited to the translation by Mirza Mohamed Hadi, which appeared in successive issues of *The Platonist*, volumes 3 and 4.</note> It should be observed that the *Desatir* is a revelation addressed to the great prophet Abad, who is identified with Abraham. “The nature of God cannot be known. Who can dare to know it but He (Himself)? The entity, the oneness, and the personality are ‘His very nature and nothing beside Him.’” From this Being, through free creation, proceeded “one whose name is *Bahnam*, called the Prime Intellect and First Reason,” and through him came “*Asham*, the second intellect,” who in turn created the intellect of the next lower heaven named “*Famesham*.” From these proceeded the “Intellect of the heaven of *Kanian*,” or Saturn; of *Harmuzd*, or Jupiter; of *Bahram*, or Mars; of *Khurshad*, or the Sun; of *Nahid*, or Venus; of *Zir*, or Mercury; and of *Mah*, or the Moon.

Here, again, we have the production of ten primary intelligences, recalling the Sephirotic emanations, which themselves have planetary attributions.

Let us now take another step. At the beginning of the twelfth century—specifically in the year 1100 A.D.—Abū Bakr Ibn Al-Tufail was born at Guadix in Spain. He was a noted Arabian physician, poet, mathematician, and Sufi philosopher who died in Morocco in 1186. His chief work is a type of philosophical romance called *The Life of Hai Ebn Yokdan*, the

<summary>The author highlights parallels between the Kabalah's ten Sephirot and the ten primary intelligences found in the Persian "Celestial Desatir" and the works of the Sufi philosopher Abū Bakr Ibn Al-Tufail.</summary>

self-taught Philosopher.” In this curious narrative, we find Ibn Al-Tufail using a form of comparison which occurs almost verbatim in the Kabalistic books: “The Divine Essence is like the rays of the material sun, which expand over opaque bodies and appear to proceed from the eye, though they are only reflected from its surface.” We also find, in substance: (*a*) The <term>Ain Soph</term> of the Kabalists under the name of that One True One; (*b*) the reflection of that Being dwelling “in the highest sphere, in and beyond which there is no body—a Being free from matter, which was not the Being of that One True One, nor the sphere itself, nor yet anything different from them both; but was like the image of the sun as it appears in a well-polished mirror, which is neither one nor the other, and yet not distinct from them”; (*c*) the immaterial essence of the sphere of the fixed stars; and (*d*) the Sphere of Saturn—and so with the rest in harmony with the scheme of the <term>Desatir</term>, ending at this world, which is subject to generation and corruption, and comprehending all that is contained within the sphere of the Moon. None of the material essences were identical, and yet none were different, either in relation to the rest or in comparison with the One True One. <note>See *The Improvement of Human Reason, exhibited in the Life of Hai Ebn Yokdhan*. Some refer it to India, others to a Gnostic origin, but the question does not concern us, for the significant fact is that this form of Islamic mysticism was one of the environments of the Kabalistic Jews to whom we are indebted for at least part of the Zohar. The influence of this environment was felt beyond the Kabalists and was acknowledged even by the most inflexible of the Jewish sects—that of the Kairites, or Literalists, who rejected all innovations in the original doctrine of Israel, who placed no value on tradition, and were thus as much opposed to the Talmud as to the Zoharic writings. The proof lies in their analogies—indeed, one might say their fusion—with the Motozales, a sect of scholastic Arabs.<note>Munk: *La Philosophie chez les Juifs*, p. 10.</note> A Kairite Jew of the period admits that his brethren followed the doctrines of this sect, and they even adopted its name.

The purpose of this section should not be misunderstood. Once more, it is by no means intended to suggest that the mystic sects of Islam are responsible for the unique system of the Kabalah, or that the Sufis drew from the rabbis. Such methods belong to a style of criticism that has deservedly passed away. If we know anything concerning the early connections of Sufism, it is that they are Neoplatonic, and that the Gnostics of the early Shiite sects were attracted to it because of these connections.<note>On this point the reader may profitably consult an excellent account of Islamic mysticism in “A Year Among the Persians,” by E. <meta>This page concludes the comparison between Sufic mysticism and Kabbalism, arguing for historical connections between Jewish and Islamic mystics in Spain, before beginning a new section on the Kabbalah's influence on the Jewish community.</meta>

mysticism is to cite analogies to Kabbalism. To say that Sufism has been traced back to a woman who died at Jerusalem in the first century of the Hijra is to say that Sufism began to live and move in an atmosphere of Jewish tradition. To say that Spain was the breeding ground of the Kabbalists is to say that the mystic doctors of Jewry encountered those of Islam, and to deny that there was any consequence of such contact is to deny nature. Sufism was pantheistic and based on emanation; Kabbalistic emanationism was saved from pantheism by the doctrine of divine immanence, and their literatures have no real likeness. However, between the metaphysics of Divine Love and the mystical absorption of Islam, and between the Kabbalistic return of the soul to God or its union with the transcendent principle—which never departs from *Atziluth*—and the theory of ecstasy in Israel, it seems reasonable to suppose that there was not only the connecting link of analogy between all mystics but even a historical bond.

To the extent that its operation was intellectual, there is very good reason to believe that its field of influence was the Christian mind rather than the Jewish mind.† And having established one useful point, there is an opportunity here to make another. Kabalistic influence on Christendom has been of two kinds, but it has been much more of one kind than the other. It has been an influence exercised by an occult claim upon the students and those who accept occult claims. But it has been much more significantly the influence of potential missionary material on the missionary efforts of the Christian Church. To begin at a more recent date—what provided the Kabalah of the Zohar to the Latin-reading scholars of Europe? The *magnum opus* (great work) of Rosenroth. What impelled Rosenroth? The "splendid vision" of the conversion of Jewry *en masse* (as a whole). And now, if we look back to the very

If we consider the beginning of the Christian interest in Kabalism, which was almost coincident, in fact, with the appearance of the Zohar, and suppose that Raymond Lully was really—as it has been said that he was—the first Christian student of the Kabalah, what was the lifelong labor of that amazing steward of Majorca, and for what did he renounce the world? Was it to wrest, as it has been said, from reluctant Nature the elusive mastery of Nature, the "Great Palingenesis" of alchemy? The Hermetic treatises falsely attributed to him may say yes, but we know that they are the products of the school of forgery which produced the spurious Geber,* and that this was by no means the ambition of Raymond Lully. But was it the attainment of the religion behind all religions? Nothing of the sort; that is modern fantasy. The work of Raymond Lully was apostolic and missionary, and it closed with martyrdom at Bugia, in a feverish attempt to evangelize "Mahound." What prompted the fiery energy of Pico della Mirandola, such that he filled the Papal Court with the rumor and the wonder of the Jewish tradition? It was the fact that he also regarded it as a certain mystic way by which the "Princes of the Exile" might be brought to the gates of the Eternal City and the Ghetto might be transformed into a baptistry. Suppose, lastly, that Nicholas Flamel was really initiated by the "Book of Abraham the Jew," so that Kabalism connects integrally with alchemy—what prompted the unpretentious scribe of old Paris to make precious metals by occult arts when his needs were few and his trade sufficient for a

Now, I do not need to say that there are very few occultists who would take any interest in the Kabalah when viewed from this perspective. As a class, they are not inspired by missionary zeal for any form of official religion, and their literature, as it stands, does not show more than a minimal respect for the great orthodoxies of Christendom. On the other hand, it is only because of some immense misunderstanding that the esoteric tradition of the Jews could be supposed to offer them the "religion behind all religions." What it actually offers them falls almost infinitely short of that. At its highest, it is a bizarre but truly strenuous attempt to solve the riddles of the universe—the most unaided of all metaphysics, a *systema mundi* <note>system of the world</note> devised in a darkened synagogue with the prayer shawl drawn over the eyes. # BOOK IV

## THE WRITTEN WORD OF KABBALISM:

### FIRST PERIOD

->*ARGUMENT*<-

The traces of Kabbalistic literature outside the *Sepher Yetzirah*, and prior to the publication of the *Zohar*, are briefly listed to indicate that there was a gradual growth of the tradition and to correct exaggerated notions concerning it. There are several ancient treatises which connect with Kabbalism, but are not regarded by modern scholarship as Kabbalistic in the technical sense. This is the case with the *Sepher Yetzirah* itself; however, there is no doubt that all these works claim to embody an occult tradition, or that the elements of oral tradition were later developed by the commentators on the *Sepher Yetzirah*, as well as by the *Zohar*, and, later still, by the expositors of Zoharic mysteries. The attention of early Kabbalists was concentrated on the “Book of Formation,” and numerous explanations of that work appeared between the eleventh and thirteenth centuries.

### I. EARLY KABBALISTIC LITERATURE

It is beyond controversy that a great body of mystic speculation and doctrine grew up within the Jewish community, the roots of which are to be found in the *Talmud*, while it is connected occasionally with brilliant and even with some great names. It is this transcendentalism which led ultimately to the *Zohar*,

The author argues that Kabbalistic tradition grew gradually from oral roots in the Talmud into a formal body of written commentary.</summary>

<header>The Written Word of Kabbalism</header>

<margin>* There can be, I think, little doubt that the Kabbalah was the "reception" of the *Bereshith* and *Mercabah* mysteries mentioned in the Talmud, or that this was the view always taken by Kabbalistic Jews.</margin>

And if scholarship should forbid us from giving it the distinctive designation of Kabbalism,* we must yield to scholarship, though with the mental reservation that if the question is more than a matter of words, it is at most one of stages of growth. For the mysticism that existed in Israel between the period of the Talmud and the period of the publication of the Zohar is that which, in the course of its evolution, became the Kabbalah and the Zohar.

The title of this section is to some extent tentative or speculative, but the modest conclusions of the previous book are sufficient justification for supposing that there are traces of Kabbalism—outside the *Sepher Yetzirah* and prior to the publication of the *Zohar*—which possess some literary remains. It is indeed essential to the historical development of the later work that it should have had its predecessors in literature. According to the most acceptable view, these were certain *Midrashim* which, for the most part, are no longer extant; it is fair to suppose that, assuming such records existed, they must have exercised some influence.

Similarly, the *Sepher Yetzirah*, whatever the date attributed to it, was of high authority, and the veneration in which it was held was the kind that produces literature. We must beware, however, of supposing that there was an unbroken line of Kabbalists from the second to the twelfth century, as some occult writers have claimed. Supposing the *Sepher Yetzirah* in its present form is later than the second century,

<summary>This page argues that Kabbalism evolved from early Talmudic mysteries into the Zohar, likely through now-lost literary works. It acknowledges scholarly debates over terminology while cautioning against the belief in an unbroken chain of Kabbalistic teachers from the second to the twelfth century.</summary>

# The Doctrine and Literature of the Kabbalah

We must regard the *Alphabet of Akiba*, a work already mentioned, as its prototype. Meanwhile, the antithesis to "The Book of Occultation"—one of the most important sections of the Zohar—must be sought in the anthropomorphic *Shiur Komah*, meaning "The Measure of the Height." This is, in other words, the "Description of the Body of God," or a development of the various Scriptural passages in which divine members are mentioned. The dates of these fragmentary works are speculative, but there can be no doubt—as indeed there is no question—of their relative antiquity. Connected with these are the *Greater Palace* and the *Lesser Palace*, known also as the "Delineation of the Heavenly Temples,"<note>This should not be confused with a work mentioned by Bartolocci under the name of Rabbi Eliezer, which deals with the measurements of the earthly temple.</note> which, in common with the others, are not regarded by modern critics as Kabbalistic; however, it is allowed that all were instrumental in bringing the Kabbalah into existence.<note>"Because of the difficulty," says Ginsburg, "they created for the Jews in the South of France and in Catalonia—who believed in them almost as much as in the Bible—these Jews were forced to devise this system. Through it, they could explain the crude descriptions of the Deity and the heavenly plains found in these Haggadic works." It may indeed be affirmed that a single spirit influenced the main mystical works that preceded the Zohar.</note>

In accordance with the requirements of his standpoint, Dr. Graetz—who may be taken to represent the most bitter and uncompromising hostility toward Jewish mysticism—dates the origin of Kabbalism to the tenth century. Thus, by implication, he denies the claim of the *Sepher Yetzirah* to be included in its literature. He is followed, as we have seen, by

...Ginsburg,* but it is beyond question that the work is indispensable to the Kabbalah, or that it is an integral and fundamental part of it. The tenth century is, however, an important period in Jewish history and Jewish literature, for at this time the awakening of the Arabian mind was followed by that of Israel† and was sometimes overshadowed by it. There was, for a moment, a lull in persecution; the academies in the East flourished, and in the West, the internal conflict between Christians and Muslims in Spain provided a breathing space for the Children of the Exile. Prior to that period, from the sixth century onward, there was a gap in the literature of Israel. The canons of the Talmud were closed, more by the terror and danger of the time than by any inherent necessity, and the history of Israel became one of bitter struggle for survival. A somewhat precarious shelter was found under Persian rule, and ultimately the intellectual lamp of Israel shone forth clearly and steadily during the Muslim rule of Spain. From that period until the beginning of the thirteenth century, this country was like a second Palestine to the Jews; under the tolerant and enlightened rule of the Spanish Caliphs, this land of refuge became almost as dear to their hearts as the Promised Land. Montpellier in France and Salerno in Italy were famous for their Jewish schools, but the one at Seville was, perhaps, more illustrious than either. Spain was also a nurturing ground for Kabbalistic literature, and the traces of the esoteric tradition

I. Rabbi Eliezer, whose mystical system, as presented in the *Pirke*, connects on one hand with the *Sepher Yetzirah*, and on the other with Zoharic teaching. We have, first, God existing alone with His Ineffable Name prior to the creation of the world; next, the creation—before the visible world—of the *Thorah* or Law, along with the Throne of Glory, the Name of the Messiah, Paradise, Hell, and the Temple of Jerusalem (that is, the archetype of the earthly temple); subsequently, the creation of the world by means of ten words. The interpretation of dreams was one of the daily occupations of the Jewish academies, and the skill they exhibited in it, or the credulity of the times, often earned toleration and respect for the Rabbis from the Caliphs. A treatise on this art is attributed to Rab Hay, which was printed at Venice.* Besides his alleged commentary on the "Book of Formation," his voluminous works contain many Kabbalistic references, especially the one titled "The Voice of God in its Power." It will be enough to mention among these the doctrine of correspondences, man as a microcosm, and a unique theory of mystic contemplation. He held enormous influence and later became the head of the academy of Pumbaditha near Baghdad. He died in 1038.

V. R. Chasdai† was a Prince of the Exile and temporal head of the Jews in Cordova. He was also a political minister under two Caliphs. He is said to link the school of Hay Gaon with that of Gebirol.‡

VI. Solomon ben Yehudah Ibn Gebirol, the scholastic Avicebron and in all respects—Kabbalistic and otherwise—a center of intellectual and literary interest, was a contemporary of the famous Nagrila.

VII. R. Abraham ben David, or Ben Dior Ha Levi, the great orthodox apologist of the twelfth century, has been included in the lineage of Kabbalism.

It may be noted, however, that he exhibits some Christian tendencies.

Those who defend the authenticity of Kabbalistic tradition find something for their purpose in all these writers and personalities, but they often proceed on a misconception. What, for example, is more likely to lead an unpracticed student astray than the treatise of Abraham ben David Ha Levi, by the mere fact of its title? It is called *Seder Ha Kabbalah*, the Order of the Tradition.† In fact, it is the least mystical of all productions, and though I have termed its author a great orthodox apologist, he had a strong Aristotelian influence. The occasion for his book was a Sadducean heresy prevalent in Castile and Leon, and represented by the work of Abu Alphrag, which maintained that the true synagogue was to be found among the Sadducees. The *Seder Ha Kabbalah* vindicates the authority of the orthodox claim under the two headings of succession and universality, or community of doctrine among all the synagogues. It embraces the entire history of the Jewish Church and the perpetuation of the Mosaic doctrine, which is the

The Jews were divided among themselves upon many questions, of which Sadducean claims were certainly not the most important. The great distinction of the time, for the purpose of our own inquiry, was between the Jews who had adopted Aristotelian principles and the Jews who opposed the innovation. Enlightenment and culture were undeniably on the side of the former; however, the fascination of mystical thought—in a word, everything we connect with the ideal of rabbinical Israel—went to the opposite side. There were great names on both sides. Rabbi Abraham and his <term>*Sepher*</term> exercised a large influence; his contemporary, Maimonides, who survived him by almost a quarter of a century, was described by the enthusiasts of his period as “the elect of the human race,” and by a wordplay on his name, it was said of him that “from Moses to Moses there was no one like Moses.”

The rival school was to some extent represented by Avicebron, and some of those who assert that the <term>*Zohar*</term> incorporated traditions belonging to preceding centuries are content to rest their case on the writings of this poet and philosopher. The evidence, however, is in a very confused state. On one hand, the system of Avicebron has many Aristotelian traces; on the other hand, it has been asserted that Maimonides has much to connect him with Avicebron, even though he was not acquainted with his works, while,

When we investigate the claim made regarding Avicebron, we must not be discouraged by finding that writers like Isaac Myer have greatly enhanced the actual strength of his Kabalistic connections. It is true that we find the doctrines of the Inaccessible God, the intermediaries between God and the universe, the emanation of the world, and even the universal knowledge attributed to the pre-existent soul of man by all Jewish mysticism. But what we would like to find in a mystic of the eleventh century is a distinct trace of typical Zoharic doctrine—for example, that of the Countenances—rather than Yetziratic references, Sephirotic correspondences, and so forth. The latter are to be expected by the middle of the eleventh century, but in this case, the former are lacking. There remains, however, enough to interest us, perhaps even to justify including Gebirol among the precursors of Zoharic Kabalism, and a short account of this author may be added as a conclusion to this section.

At that period when the influence of Arabian imagination was infused into the romantic literature of Western Europe, scholastic philosophy and theology were receiving the influence of Arabian thought. However, just as this influence was sometimes received without much awareness of its origin, on the other hand, influences were occasionally attributed to Arabian sources that were in reality attributable only to the Spanish Jews living under the protection of the Caliphate during the Muslim domination of the peninsula. A case in point was

the once renowned Avicebron, whose identity as Solomon ben Yehudah Ibn Gabirol, a Jew of Cordoba, was first demonstrated by Munk in the early part of the nineteenth century.* His principal treatise, titled the "Fountain of Life," in a Latin version attributed to the middle of the twelfth century, became widely circulated; Albertus Magnus, St. Thomas Aquinas, and Duns Scotus all cited it, and it is said to summarize the philosophy of the thirteenth century. According to Renan, Avicebron preceded the school of Arabian philosophy that arose in Spain. He wrote philosophy in Arabic and poetry in Hebrew; the Jews valued his poetry, but his metaphysics were not well-regarded among them; the Christian scholastics adopted his philosophical ideas and knew nothing whatever of his verses. Both groups of his admirers celebrated him as the greatest philosopher and the greatest poet of his time, respectively. However, the nominalists denounced him; realists like Duns Scotus brought their own condemnation upon him; and while he is said to have influenced the mystics of the Middle Ages, he was banned by the University of Paris at the time of the Zohar's publication on the grounds that he favored Aristotle. When the school of Averroes arose, he was unknown among them; at a later period, he was unknown to Maimonides; he was also unknown to the encyclopedic learning of Pico della Mirandola; and on the

threshold of the Reformation his memory may be said to have perished at the pyre of Giordano Bruno.

Avicebron was born about the year 1021 in Malaga; he was educated at the University of Saragossa, and he died in Valencia in 1070. He was patronized by Nagdilah—that is, Samuel-ha-Levi ben Josef Ibn Nagréla—a Prince of the Exile, who was also Prime Minister of Spain under the Caliphate of Habus. Nagdilah was the center and mainspring of Jewish learning in that country, and it is thought that through him the sacred tradition of the Hebrews was communicated to Avicebron at a period when the Zohar and its related works were still being formed. It seems certain, in any case, that some of the concepts and the system incorporated in these books may be found in his writings, more especially in the *Fountain of Life* and the *Crown of the Kingdom*. The first is claimed to be the earliest known display of "the secrets of the speculative Kabalah."* The second, composed toward the end of his life, is a hymn "celebrating the only one and true God, and the wonders of His creation."

The existence of the Zoharic tradition several centuries before the time of Moses de Leon, the alleged forger of the Zohar, has been based, among other evidence, on the writings of this Spanish Jew, and he seems to have been undoubtedly acquainted with the *Book of Formation*. In the second book and twenty-second section of the

---

* <note>There is some confusion here, as the *Sepher Yetzirah* is certainly speculative when contrasted with the so-called practical Kabalah, which was mainly the performing of miracles through the use of the Divine Names.</note>

<meta>This page continues the examination of Avicebron’s (Ibn Gabirol's) influence on Jewish mysticism, specifically how his philosophical works align with the "Book of Formation" and the "Thirty-Two Paths."</meta>

<header>The Doctrine and Literature of the Kabalah</header>

"Fountain of Life" this passage occurs: "Hence it has been said that the construction of the world was accomplished by the inscription of numbers and letters in the air," which is obviously the fundamental notion of the Kabalistic work in question. The table of the Thirty-Two Paths, which arises out of the *Book of Formation*, was the theme of one of his poems. Whether the later Kabalists derived from Avicebron or both from a common source cannot be conclusively determined, but considering the Jewish indifference to his philosophical writings, and the probable existence of a vast mass of floating esoteric tradition, there can be no doubt as to the direction in which probability points.*

The connection between Avicebron and the Kabalah is not sufficiently explicit on the surface of the "Fountain of Life" to have attracted the attention of critics like Ernest Renan; while Kabalistic critics attribute the system which it develops to the ten *Sephiroth*, others suppose it to be based on the ten categories of Aristotle, a pantheism analogous to that of the early realists. "On the one hand," says Renan, "his application of Peripatetic principles to Mosaic doctrine alarmed the theologians; on the other hand, his concessions to orthodoxy concerning the creation and the free will of the Creator did not satisfy the extreme Peripatetic Jews." Of his alleged Kabalistic connections Renan was either unaware, as already suggested, or they were ignored by him.

---

\* <note>Graetz takes the opposite view, saying that the Kabalah borrowed many principles from Ibn Gabirol. He, of course, offers no reasoning on the subject.</note>

<summary>This page discusses the conceptual links between Avicebron’s "Fountain of Life" and Kabalistic traditions like the "Book of Formation," while noting that some scholars viewed his work through the lens of Aristotelian philosophy rather than mysticism.</summary>
<keywords>Avicebron, Ibn Gabirol, Fountain of Life, Book of Formation, Thirty-Two Paths, Sephiroth, Aristotle, Peripatetic philosophy, Ernest Renan, Graetz, Jewish mysticism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the analysis of Avicebron’s (Ibn Gabirol's) philosophy, specifically examining how the "Fountain of Life" relates to Kabbalistic thought and identifying the historical schools that preceded the Zohar.</meta>

167
<header>The Written Word of Kabalism</header>

An impartial examination of the “Fountain of Life” makes the pantheism of Avicebron less apparent than his Kabalistic correspondences. Rather than identifying the universe with God, it establishes a clear contrast between them. In order to bridge the abyss and to make it conceivable that one derived its being from the other, he supposes nine intermediaries, *plus* the Divine Will, “through which the Absolutely Existing, who is above number,” is “attached to its corporeal universe.” The analogies that this conception offers to Yetziratic doctrine are self-evident and do not need emphasizing, and yet our impartial judgment must pronounce the philosophy of Avicebron to be Greek rather than Jewish in character. It is at least clear that the *Fons Vitæ*, which is a dialogue after the manner of Plato, is deeply influenced by Hellenic thought.

Modern scholarship has recognized three chief schools which led up to Zoharic Kabalism: (a) that of Isaac the Blind, to which belongs Azariel with his celebrated commentary on the Sepher Yetzirah; (b) that of Eliezar of Worms, which is largely of the theurgic order; and (c) that of Abulafia, which to some extent united the preceding schools and made use of theurgic formulas combined with contemplation to achieve union with God.

of the Book of Formation in connection with our investigation into the authority of the Kabalah, we have nearly exhausted the subject of the small treatise which is regarded by most scholars and by every occultist as the nucleus of all Kabalism. It is difficult, however, to omit it when giving an account of the documents, and it will perhaps be best to begin this brief bibliographical notice with a summary of the points which have been determined previously concerning it.

The legend which attributes it to the patriarch Abraham—who supposedly transmitted it orally to his sons, by whom it was passed down in turn until the “sages of Jerusalem” finally committed it to writing so that the tradition would not perish even when the chosen people themselves seemed to be on the verge of perishing—this, as we have seen, is a legend. It is interesting and respectable in its own way. At the time we first hear of the existence of such a tract, it was possibly already old, and most old books have myths designed to explain them. Those who take the myths historically convert honest legend into something approaching farce. We must be content, therefore, to say that the Sepher Yetzirah is first mentioned probably in the ninth century; there is some reason to suppose that it is quoted in the Talmud, but it is not entirely certain;<note>The treatise *Sanhedrim* contains the following passage: “By combining the letters of the ineffable names as recorded in SPR ITSIRH (that is, the sealing names listed in the first chapter, which are permutations of IHV), Rava once created a man and sent him to Rav Zeira. Since the man was unable to reply when spoken to, the Rabbi said to him, 'You are a creature of the company (those initiated in the mysteries of necromancy); return to your dust.'”</note> it

...may have existed before its first mention in literature by a generation, a century, or an age. Let us realize that we do not know, and that those who judge the question dogmatically on either side deserve to be classified as intemperate.

Let us now look a little more closely at the work itself. It is divided into five chapters: the first is concerned with the role of the *Sephiroth* in creation, and the remaining four deal with what have been termed the Instruments—namely, the letters of the Hebrew alphabet. It was after these mysteries were revealed to Abraham that he received the manifestation of God and that the covenant was established. According to the original text, God “bound the twenty-two letters” on the patriarch’s tongue and revealed all their secrets to him.*

We have no reason to reject an interpretation that has been consistently adopted, but it is only right to draw attention to the fact that the first work to mention the *Sephiroth* leaves this point obscure, while it certainly depicts God as the active architect of the universe, who engraved, sculpted, and built.

In 1887 Dr. Papus produced a French translation to which he added the *Thirty-two Paths of Wisdom* and the *Fifty Gates of Intelligence*. With characteristic sincerity, he admits that this was superseded by Mayer Lambert in 1891.

The one question that now remains for consideration is how we are to account for the importance attributed by occultism to such a work as the *Sepher Yetzirah*. Do its defenders believe that the combination of *Aleph* with all the other letters and all the rest with *Aleph*, *Beth* with all the others and all the rest with *Beth*, etc., etc., actually produced the universe? That seems an insupportable assumption. Do they regard the letters as symbols of forces and believe that the *Sepher Yetzirah* teaches that the universe originates in the orderly combination of certain forces? That is reasonable enough, but it is a commonplace that seems scarcely worth stating and certainly does not require a secret tradition to secure it. But do they consider that the letters represent occult forces, of a fixed, determinable character, and that initiation into the real meaning of Kabalistic tradition will discover their nature, explaining the secret behind the arbitrary doctrine of a virtue inherent in words and letters? This might indeed be valuable, but I have never met with an occultist who took such a view, or had anything to substantiate it if he did. In the absence of any light on this point, we can only conclude that it is the arbitrary doctrine in question which accounts for the interest taken in the *Sepher Yetzirah*; but the truly occult student, as I have defined him at the outset of this inquiry, can only

### III. CONNECTIONS AND DEPENDENCIES OF THE BOOK OF FORMATION

If there were evidence to warrant our believing that Moses de Leon did actually—as his hostile relative is reported to have affirmed—write the *Zohar* in its entirety "out of his own head," there would still be substantial evidence that the Kabalistic system it contains was not his invention. The existence of the *Sepher Yetzirah* is part of this evidence, which appears, however, more fully and more strongly in the commentaries and developments of that work. We have already seen that when the *Book of Formation* came to be printed at Mantua, it was accompanied by five such connections, which, at the same time, do not exhaust the list that might be given in a full bibliography. The best known is unquestionably the *Sepher Sephiroth*, or "Commentary on the Ten Sephiroth by way of Questions and Answers," the work of R. Azariel ben Menahem; that of Rabbi Abraham has been regarded as the most important from an occult standpoint, while the earliest in chronological order is the work of Saadya Gaon in the tenth century. Another, which has been attributed to Hai Gaon in the early part of the eleventh century, would rank next in antiquity, but it has usually been rejected as spurious. Commentaries are also attributed to R. Moses

The commentary of Rabbi Saadya Gaon was published in Hebrew at Mantua along with the *Sepher Yetzirah*,† but it was originally written in Arabic, and a copy is preserved in the Bodleian Library. After remaining in manuscript for over eight hundred years, this Arabic original was finally printed in Paris, along with a French translation, in 1892. In the introduction to his version, M. Lambert observes that Saadya Gaon appears as a theosophist in his commentary; this is equivalent to saying that the first explanatory treatise on the *Sepher Yetzirah* has a Kabbalistic character, even though the author is often regarded as a purely rationalistic writer. It must be admitted, however, that Saadya offers little connection to Zoharic doctrine. We have noted that the *Sephiroth* of the *Sepher Yetzirah* show scarcely any trace of an emanational system. For Saadya Gaon, there is one intermediary between God and the world, but this is the physical air and not the transcendental numerations. In this air, God is everywhere present, and it penetrates all bodies, even the most solid. There is also no real trace of the doctrine of <term>Ain Soph</term>. It is,

However, it is recognized, on the one hand, that we cannot have an adequate notion of the Divinity or His correspondences with the world; but, on the other hand, it is held that some approximate idea may be obtained regarding the latter and that they may be demonstrated by means of figures and comparisons. One of these illustrations tells us that God is the life of the world just as the soul is the life of the body, and as the soul is all-powerful in man, so God is omnipotent in the world. He is also its Supreme Reason; and as the rational faculty is the guide of life in man, so the Divine Power is directed by the Divine Reason. The commentary never rises above this elementary and commonplace form of natural theology, and we may indeed be justified in saying that the work, as a whole, has no intrinsic interest, though it is valuable as a historical document.

Unlike the *Sepher Yetzirah*, which makes no reference to <term>pneumatology</term>, Saadyah Gaon devotes a certain amount of space to the consideration of the soul in man. Here, in a sense, he connects with Zoharic Kabbalism, although he rejects <term>metempsychosis</term>, for he recognizes its five aspects and calls them by their conventional names—names which, as we have seen, occur in the Talmud.<note>* Despite his hostility to reincarnation as understood by the Kabbalah, he accepts the pre-existence of souls and teaches that the resurrection of the body will take place when all souls destined for earthly life have passed through it.</note> Unfortunately, his classification is exceedingly clumsy, and he begins by following Plato in the recognition of three faculties—reason, desire, and anger. on account of desire it is called *Nephesh*, and on account of anger it is called *Ruach*. The two other names, *Chaiah* (living) and *Jechidah* (unique), refer to the vitality of the soul and to the fact that no other creature resembles it.

The doctrine concerning divine and angelic names is also the subject of some references which are important to our inquiry because they establish the fact that Saadya Gaon did not attribute to them any occult power. The names of the angels vary according to the events which they are commissioned to perform, and, in the same way, those names applied to the Deity describe His operations. In the work of creation, He calls Himself *Elohim*; when ordaining the covenant of circumcision, He is called *El Shaddai*; He is the "I AM" in connection with the wonders of the ten plagues; and He is *Jah* when performing the great miracle of the Red Sea.<note>* The *Zohar* teaches that the name AHIH, which signifies "I AM," indicates the unification and concealment of all things in such a way that no distinction can be established between them. The name ASLR AHIR, "I WHO AM," represents God at the point of manifesting all things, including His Supreme Name. On the other hand, the name Jehovah or AHIH AShR AHIH, "I AM HE WHO IS," refers to the Deity, or is the name assumed by Him, on the occasion of the manifestation of the Universe.—*Zohar* iii. 65*b*, Mantua.</note> Just as it is with the names of God and the angels, so it is with the names of the stars, which vary according to their qualities—specifically, their greater or lesser brilliance, their hot or cold natures, etc.

relation, place, time, powers, position, activity, and passivity; and if these are to be regarded as referring to the numerations of the *Sepher Yetzirah*, it is clear that Saadya Gaon understood the latter from the perspective of an Aristotelian philosopher. With these categories, the ten commandments are also forced to correspond in an arbitrary manner. For example, the commandment against adultery corresponds to the category of position, for the act itself is a position and a contact.

Lastly, in his analysis of the Hebrew alphabet, the commentator seeks to account for its sequence. *Aleph* is the first sound pronounced—that is, it is vocalized at the back of the tongue. *Shin* is vocalized in the middle of the mouth and *Mem* on the lips. Unfortunately for this analogy, *Mem* precedes *Shin* in the alphabet, and indeed the logic of the speculation seems beyond conjecture.

About the commentary ascribed to Hay Gaon there is considerable confusion, which Isaac Myer increases by claiming that it deals with the “Book of Concealment,” instead of the “Book of Formation.” There are no historical notices and no traces whatsoever of the former text before the appearance of the *Zohar*, in which it was first made known. The work of Hay Gaon only needs to be mentioned in passing because its authenticity is disputed. Other works attributed to him are not above suspicion, but it may be admitted in a general way that he had more distinct Kabalistic connections than Saadya. The condemned commentary deals largely with the mysteries of the Tetragrammaton and gives, perhaps for the first time, the curious quadrilateral method of writing it by means of letters and circles, to which so much

<summary>This page describes Saadya Gaon's attempt to reconcile the Sepher Yetzirah with Aristotelian philosophy and phonetics, while also addressing the disputed authenticity of Hay Gaon's Kabalistic commentary.</summary>

We have admitted that the commentary of Saadya Gaon can hardly be called Kabbalistic; we have agreed to set aside another which is full of Kabbalistic material because its date and authorship have been challenged. In the work of Rabbi Abraham, however, there are Zoharic elements that are beyond question, and it is indeed to the school he represents that Graetz and others have attributed the authorship of the *Book of Splendour*. There is the peculiar distinction between upper and lower *Sephiroth* which is not only characteristic of the Zoharic period, but offers a connecting link between Rabbi Abraham and the late Kabbalism of Isaac Luria.* But more than this, there is the doctrine of the Unknowable God—of "the Cause of Causes which is not understood by anyone outside Itself"—being devoid of all distinction and every mode of existence. It has not yet taken the final shape in which it is presented by the *Zohar*, and it appears to be something more hidden and latent than the conception of *Ain Soph*, the *Non Ens* [Non-Being] or *Corona Summa* [Supreme Crown]. This is again distinguished by Rabbi Abraham from *Kether*, the Crown of Creation, on the grounds that "the accident is not made from the essence, nor the *Res* [Thing] from the *Non Res* or *Non Ens*," thus creating an insoluble difficulty regarding the emanation of the manifest universe. For the rest, the *Ain Soph* of our commentator is described in terms which are almost identical with

Among minor Zoharic correspondences, it may be noted that a peculiar importance is attributed to the letter *Aleph*; it is the form of all the letters, and all the paths of wisdom are contained within it, but in a universal way. There are also traces of the unique angelic system which was destined to receive such elaborate expansion from the commentators on the *Book of Splendour*.

However, it is worth stating that the attempt made by Éliphas Lévi to identify the Abraham mentioned by Flamel with the commentator on the *Sepher Yetzirah* not only establishes a connection between alchemy and Kabalism that is unjustified in itself, but also has no plausible evidence to cite in its own support, as there is no trace whatever of any alchemical meaning in the Hebrew commentator. Abraham the Kabalist belongs to the twelfth century. Flamel lived two hundred years later, and the book which he mentions could scarcely have existed in the Jewish community, according to the Lévi hypothesis, for such a length of time without something coming to light concerning it.

As a literary and philosophical work, the primary place among the works related to the *Sepher Yetzirah* seems correctly attributed to the commentary of Azariel. Its author was born at Valladolid in or about the year 1160. According to some authorities, he was a pupil of Isaac the Blind,* but others say that his teacher was Rabbi Jehuda, son of Rabad. He became, in turn, the instructor of Rabbi Moses Nachmanides, who also belongs to the lineage of the Yetziratic tradition.†

He is said to have traveled extensively in search of secret wisdom, but it was an age when men of learning were frequently wanderers, and perhaps less obscure motives motivated him. He connects with the Kabalistic system which was expounded by the school of Gerona, and there are no—

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Azariel of Valladolid, transitioning from his travels and influences to the specific content and impact of his commentary on the Ten Sephiroth.</meta>

real grounds for supposing that he acquired knowledge elsewhere, but he added the result of his own reflections. Many works have been attributed to him, of which some are lost and some have remained in manuscript. "The Explanation of the Ten Sephiroth by way of Questions and Answers" must have helped to shape the metaphysical speculations of the Kabalah and may very well have originated more than it derived.

The teachings of Azariel aroused the opposition of the Aristotelian Jews, and Isaac Myer thinks that the logical form of his commentary was a concession to this school of thought. Whatever its motive, the fact, broadly taken, is of importance to our inquiry; it shows that the Sephirotic notion in its earliest development could not really have been that of the categories, since it had to be conformed to the principles espoused by the disciples of Aristotle. The Jewish *literati* followed various schools, and the influence attributed to the Stagirite <note>Aristotle</note> has perhaps been exaggerated. The followers of the so-called secret wisdom were a small minority. Platonism, it goes without saying, was very little known in the West at the period in question, though it appears in later Kabalism.

As regards both matter and form, Azariel's commentary has been highly praised. It contains the doctrine of *Ain Soph*, which is not in the Sepher Yetzirah, and it has express views on the emanation of the *Sephiroth*, which are said to be contained in *Ain Soph* and have no effect when separated. Their emanation was possible because it must be within the omnipotence of the Deity to

The essence and the real principle of all finite things is the Thought of the Supreme Being; if that were withdrawn, they would be left as empty shells, and this is true not only of the visible world but of the intermediaries between God and creation. With his philosophical speculations, the Kabalist mingles something from the realm of the fantastic, attributing, for example, certain symbolic colors to the *Sephiroth*.* *Kether* is "like the Concealed Light," or the light which is veiled in darkness; the intended comparison is probably that of a luminous mist. *Binah* is sky-blue, because *Binah* is the great sea of Kabalism. *Chokmah* is yellow, *Chesed* white, and *Geburah* red; *Tiphereth* is white, red, or pink, *Netzach* is whitish-red, and *Hod* reddish-white. *Jesod* is the combination of the previous triad, while *Malkuth* is like the light which reflects all colors. Azariel also supports the Sephirotic division of the human body which is found in later Kabalism.

Moses ben Nahman, or Nachmanides, was born in 1194 at Girona. Before he became acquainted with the Kabalah, he is said to have had a prejudice against it, but he was afterward an enthusiastic student of both its speculative and practical parts, and through both his writings and influence, he contributed much to its development. His Kabalistic "Explanation of the Law" was completed in 1268, and among his many other works are that called the "Garden of Delight," and another on the "Secrets of the *Torah*,"

# BOOK V

## THE WRITTEN WORD OF KABALISM: SECOND PERIOD

->*ARGUMENT*<-

The divisions of the <term>Zohar</term> are set forth in successive sections to provide a clear and comprehensive idea of the materials incorporated into this composite work. Its doctrinal content is established by means of extensive quotations, selected with special reference to its importance from an occult standpoint.

## I. THE BOOK OF SPLENDOUR: ITS CONTENT AND DIVISIONS

The cycle of the <term>Sepher Yetzirah</term> lies within a manageable scope, and its most important related works are all available to the student through Latin translations. The <term>Sepher Ha Zohar</term>, on the other hand, is large in itself; it has considerable supplementary material belonging to a later period and an extensive connected literature. It has been translated neither into Latin nor into any other European language.<note>The Italian reader may, however, be referred to an analysis of the <term>Zohar</term> by the Abbé de Rossi, which appeared in his *Historical Dictionary of Hebrew Authors* (*Dizionario storico degli Autori Ebrei*). The writer follows Morin regarding the late date of the work.</note> The consequence, especially

<meta>This page continues the discussion regarding the difficulties of studying the Zohar, specifically criticizing Rosenroth's "Kabbala Denudata" for its disorganized structure and the confusion it caused for later scholars like Basnage.</meta>

Part of this must undoubtedly be attributed to the ambitious design of Rosenroth’s great collection. The *Kabbala Denudata*, by attempting to cover far too wide a field, gives no adequate idea of the work it is meant to clarify. It attributes an exaggerated, though not inexcusable, importance to three tracts introduced into the body of the Zohar and to a late commentary on them; the apparatus in the form of a lexicon which fills most of the first volume, though it has a methodical appearance, is little more than chaos, in which late and early commentators are bundled together in the uncritical manner of that period; in a later section, undue prominence is given to some personal discussions and correspondence between the editor and Henry More, the English Platonist; finally, the second volume includes an enormous treatise on the doctrine of the Revolutions of Souls by a Kabalist of the seventeenth century. With all its defects, the *Kabbala Denudata* remains of real value, but it would have been priceless had a clearer genius governed its arrangement. As it is, the class of people who are most

### The Written Word of Kabalism

who are interested in the subject have been content to follow Rosenroth's lead, and to accept a small treatise known as the "*Book of Concealment*" as the fundamental part of the entire Zohar, and to consider the developments of that treatise as having the next highest importance. There are, of course, many sources of information—mostly non-occult—that could have corrected this false impression—such as the work of Franck in France and Ginsburg in England, to name only two—but it has persisted nonetheless, and the most recent example is found in the latest work published by Dr. Papus. In that book, the bibliographical appendix states that "the only complete translation" of the Zohar is that of M. H. Chateau,* even though the project in question is limited only to the treatises translated into Latin by Rosenroth, and these have been available for years in the English version by Mathers. The *Book of Occultation* and its Zoharistic commentaries are only incidental parts of the Zohar, and they provide no real idea of the scope of that work. I should add that, from an occult perspective, the Zohar itself is only an incidental part of the Kabalah—an event in the life of the alleged tradition, much like how, from the perspective of Latin Christianity, the New Testament is not the exclusive foundation of the Church but an event in its development.

It records the long conversations between its author, Rabbi Simeon ben Jochai, and Moses; the short and pathetic prayers inserted within it; the religious anecdotes; and the attractive spiritual explanations of Scripture passages, appealing to human hearts and needs. The descriptions of the Deity and the *Sephiroth* under the tender forms of human relationships, comprehensible to the finite mind—such as father, mother, primeval man, matron, bride, white head, the great and small face, the luminous mirror, the higher heaven, the higher earth, etc.—which it provides on every page, made the Zohar a welcome textbook for students of the Kabalah. Through its vivid descriptions of Divine Love,<note>In this respect, the Zohar suggests analogies with Arabian *Sufism*.</note> these students could lose themselves in rapturous embraces with the Deity."

<meta>This page continues the sentence from the previous page, finishing the discussion on how Kabalistic literature influenced Jewish thought and critiquing occultist interpretations of the Zohar.</meta>

# The Written Word of Kabalism

of Kabalism affected the fervent imagination of the Jew and the kind of influence it had on him, which is well illustrated in the fascinating and terrible histories of Messianic enthusiasm and illusion, as already noted. We can understand, secondly, how much there is to correct in the occult theory that has settled upon the *Zohar* as embodying the traditional knowledge of a religion behind all religions. No system responds less readily to what is involved in such a conception; no person could be less disposed than the occultist to accept the full Kabalistic notion of religion, if he were really acquainted with it. The philosophical doctrines which I have sketched in the early sections of the previous book do certainly connect superficially with occult philosophy, which itself seems to connect magnetically with everything that is unsound in faith and unreasonable in doctrine. The connection in the present instance can only show that neither is of serious account in the last analysis. That God is immanent in the material world is a much simpler and more rational hypothesis than to establish intermediaries between the finite and the infinite, which create innumerable difficulties without resolving any. Far more truly philosophical is the doctrine of the Countenances, which I have described already as the chief glory of Kabalism; but, as far as any effect is concerned, it has passed away, like the Shekinah from the Holy Place. At the present day, we have other and better means of explaining the anthropomorphisms of the Bible, and, for the rest, no attempt to explain them has any connection, approximate or remote, with the ground which occult philosophy assumes to cover.

# The Doctrine and Literature of the Kabalah

The Zohar is divided by Rosenroth, in a somewhat artificial manner, into internal and external parts.*

I. The internal parts are those which are combined together in one scheme. They are:

(a) The text of the Zohar, properly so called. Apart from all its additions, this is not of unmanageable dimensions.
(b) The *Sepher Dzenioutha*, or Book of Concealment.
(c) The *Idrah Rabbah*, or Greater Synod.
(d) The *Idrah Zuta*, or Lesser Synod.
(e) *Sabah D'Mishpatim*, the Discourse or Story of the Ancient One in the *Mishpatim* section.
(f) *Midrash Ruth*, or a Commentary on the Scriptural book of that name. Only fragments exist.
(g) *Sepher Ha Bahir*, the Renowned or Illustrious Book, sometimes called the Book of Brightness.
(h) *Tosephthoth*, *Addenda*, or Additions.
(i) *Rayah Mehemnah*, or the Faithful Shepherd.
(j) *Haikluth*, that is, Palaces, Mansions, or Abodes.
(k) *Sithrai Thorah*, or Mysteries of the *Thorah*, that is, the Law.
(l) *Midrash Hannelam*, or the Secret Commentary.
(m) *Razé Derazin*, or the Secret of Secrets.

From this account are omitted the following tracts and fragments, on the ground that they do not

The reason why these portions are set aside seems insufficient, as sections *e*, *f*, *g*, *j*, and *m* in the first list are also missing from the Mantua edition. The Great Zohar, the Cremona edition (1558–60), contains all the treatises listed in both of the above lists. I am not aware of any superior authority that resides in the Mantua Zohar.†

II. As understood by Rosenroth, the external parts are those added to the earlier editions. These are:

> (*a*) *Tikkunim H'Zohar*, or Supplements of the Book of Splendor, also called the Ancient Supplements, to distinguish them from further and later additions.
> (*b*) *Zohar Chadash*—the New Zohar, containing matters omitted in the printed editions. This has four parts.

Rosenroth also highly recommends and largely reproduces the vast manuscript treatises of Isaac de Loria, compiled by R. Chaiïm Vital, and further acknowledges his indebtedness to two other unpublished works, a Kabalistic commentary on the whole Law and a treatise entitled *Chesed Abraham*.

The Zohar proper—the conversations of Simeon ben Jochai with the prophets by whom he was visited, with the disciples by whom he was surrounded, and of these, as we have seen, with each other—is not a work that is to be judged by the same standard as the purely allegorical portions which have been incorporated into it, and to which Christian students of Kabalism have given so much prominence. It is, in the first place, far more natural and comprehensible, less distorted by monstrous symbolism, having occasionally a touch of nature to indicate its kinship with humanity, and even condescending at times to a Rabelaisian episode.* Finally, it does not betray much trace

---

<note>* About a hundred rabbinical histories, fables, and apologues are narrated in it, sometimes elucidating a difficult point of Scripture—as, for example, whether the destruction of animal life at the Deluge may indicate that the beasts also sinned—sometimes recounting the death of a righteous man, sometimes describing visions and narrating tales of wonder.</note>
