of that inner meaning which is broadly assumed to exist throughout its entire content, for the most part, by those who would and do likewise discern a hidden transcendentalism in <term>Pantagruelism</term>.<note>Following the lead of Éliphas Lévi, especially in *Le Sorcier de Meudon*.</note> To determine whether the work, as a whole, is important from the standpoint of modern <term>occultism</term>, I shall now give a short synopsis of its doctrine on several vital questions of religion and philosophy. We have seen that in a certain manner—somewhat occasional and informal—it is a commentary on the <term>Pentateuch</term>, and it is generally accepted and ignored that it is a forced, arbitrary commentary, which has nothing in harmony with the simple sense of Scripture. It would serve no purpose to expand upon this fact, which applies to all <term>Kabalistic exegesis</term>. The governing principle of its interpretation or treatment is the existence of several senses in the written word. These are differently enumerated, and there seems no reason why they should not be extended indefinitely, but they are broadly reducible to three heads, which are compared by the <term>Zohar</term> to the garment, the body which is within it, and the soul which is within the body. "There are those unwise," it says, "who see how a man is dressed in a beautiful garment, but see no further, and take the garment for the body, whereas there is something more precious <note>than either</note>, namely, the soul. The Law also has its body. Some of the commandments may be called the body of the Law, and the ordinary"

<meta>This page continues the quote from the Zohar regarding the layers of meaning in the Law and the Kabalistic approach to biblical interpretation.</meta>

# The Written Word of Kabalism

> *narratives mixed into it are the garments which clothe this body. Simple folk observe only these garments—that is, the stories of the Law—perceiving not what they hide. Others more instructed do not give heed to the clothing but to the body which it covers. And there are the Wise, the servants of the Great King, who dwell on the heights of Sinai and concern themselves only with the soul, which is the foundation of all and the true Law. These shall be ready in the time to come to contemplate the 'soul of that soul' which breathes in the Law."*

This passage illustrates what I mean by the added depth and significance which the <term>Kabalah</term> reads into the Bible,† and it is, I think, also an instance of the intellectual humility of the great rabbis of the Exile who confessed to a meaning in Scripture which exceeded their loving insight,‡ so that after all the subtleties of exegesis, all the gatherings of synods, the Word of God resulted in a mystery. The key to this mystery was the reward of the just and wise man in the world to come.

The necessity of the multiple layers of meaning followed from the insufficiency of the literal text. Simple narratives and common words suggested only the human lawgiver; if those alone were the sum of the <term>Thorah</term>, it would be possible to equal, perhaps even to excel

<meta>This page continues the argument from the previous page that the literal text of the Torah is insufficient, necessitating the hidden meanings explored in the Zohar.</meta>

it. Moreover, the sayings of Esau, Hagar, Laban, of Balaam and Balaam’s ass, could not be “the Law of Truth, the Perfect Law, the faithful witness of God.”* And for this reason, the transcendental meaning, which contained the true Law, was assumed in order to save Israel from skepticism, and it postponed rationalistic criticism for several centuries. It led, of course, into extravagance; the second meaning became in its turn inadequate, and a more concealed one was inferred. So also, besides a general hidden meaning, there was that more particular triple significance attributed to every individual word. As the possibilities suggested by such a method are infinite, it is unnecessary to say that these senses were never systematized, or that the <term>Zohar</term> does not unfold in a consecutive form either the allegorical or mystical meaning. It gives glimpses only, and it may be in this sense that the original <term>Zohar</term> is said to have been a camel’s load. That original was a latent concept in the minds of Kabbalistic rabbis, but it was never written with a pen.

As the <term>Zohar</term> establishes the necessity of the concealed meaning based on the insufficiency of the outward text, and as the sense of such insufficiency is undoubtedly a late event in the history of sacred documents, we have full evidence for deciding the value of the claim which it elsewhere makes for a high antiquity for its interpretation. Had the Jew never come in contact with culture outside Judea, he would never have conceived the “tradition,” and the kind of culture which helped him to the sense

Having established the general principle of Zoharic exegesis, let us see next the kind of light which it cast upon the letter of Scripture and, in the first place, upon the creation of man. We know that, according to Genesis, he was made of the dust of the earth, that the breath of life passed over him and that he became a living soul. The <term>Zohar</term> is able to tell us what transpired before this event: “When it entered into the will of the Holy One to create man, He called before Him many hosts of the superior angels and said unto them, ‘It is my will to create man.’ They answered Him, ‘Man will not continue one night in his dignity.’† Then the Holy One extended His finger and burned them. Thereafter He summoned other hosts into His presence, saying unto them, ‘It is my will to create man.’ They answered, ‘What is man that You remember him?’‡ He said unto them, ‘But this man shall be in our image, so that his wisdom shall be greater than your wisdom.’ When *Elohim* had created man, when man had fallen into sin, going forth as one guilty, there came *Uzza* and *Azaël*, saying to the Holy One, ‘We have an

Behold this son of man whom You have made, how he has sinned before You.’ He answered them, ‘If you had been in a similar situation, you would have done worse than he has.’ What then did the Holy One do? He cast them down from their sacred station, even from heaven.* It must be confessed that this ridiculous fable has neither an inward meaning nor an outward light. We know that Latin Christianity has a legend of the fall of the angels which connects that event in some misty manner with the scheme of redemption; it is sufficiently childish, but it speaks with the tongue of seraphs in comparison with this history, in which the superior good sense of the hierarchy is punished by burning and expulsion.

There are, of course, many other passages in the <term>Zohar</term> which are explanatory of the creation of man. The breath of life is said to be the holy soul which has its origin from the Life Divine, and thus man started on his mundane course with a sanctified natural life (<term>Nephesh</term>) communicated from the heavenly <term>Chaiah</term>. And in this connection there is a suggestive and beautiful thought.

there is put in action a spirit which goes up and down in the world, resting only in those places from which sanctity has withdrawn, and thus the child of man is corrupted.”* We learn also that the Throne of Glory had six steps,† and man was created on the sixth day “because he is worthy to sit on this throne.”‡ There are again obscure references to the original physical condition of humanity. Adam and Eve were created side by side, not face to face; that is, seemingly, there was no “desire of the female for the male.”§ It does not, however, appear that the awakening of this desire was the real cause of the fall from perfection—a notion quite inconsistent with rabbinical views on the sanctity and symbolism of sexual intercourse. The Kabalah and the mysticism which led up to it offer a very high ideal of the marriage state. It is true that in the fragments of the *Bahir* the sin of Eve is presented as sexual, but if this is to be understood literally, it was one of monstrous intercourse. According to Maimonides, however, the serpent represented the imaginative faculty.

Enough has been quoted to show that there is a curious theosophic lore in the *Zohar* concerning the creation of man, but there is nothing of any real occult significance; it is suggestive occasionally with the kind of suggestion that is never lacking in fable and myth, and it is most interesting within its own sphere, which is that of mythology and certainly not

We are told that he was clothed "in the garment of the Upper Splendor,"<note>Folio 103b. Compare Mantua Edition, ii. 229b.</note> for which Jehovah Elohim afterwards substituted the garment of skin, thus indicating that the fall was followed by a change of physical condition. There is no need to mention that this legend prevailed in many Eastern mythologies; it is not, on the one hand, the special inheritance of the Kabalah, nor can the latter show that it really came by it through an oral succession from early times. As often happens in the Zohar, there is a poetical inference from the fable which is better than the fable itself. 
> "The good deeds which the son of man accomplishes in this world draw from the Light of Glory above, and he prepares for himself a garment against the day when he shall enter that world and appear before the Holy Blessed One."<note>Ibid. Compare the parallel mythos of Talmudic times, which represents good deeds as creating good angels.</note>

The Fall of man in the Zohar, as in its connections, insists upon the fact that it was the serpent Samaël who brought death into the world; but this serpent is to some extent allegorical, and the lapse was a lapse of the soul by the desire of the things below it. The death seems to be spiritual, for it is said that "the serpent takes away the higher souls [<term>Neshamoth</term>] of all flesh."<note>Cremona edition, part i. folio 28a.</note> The tree of the trespass was banished from Paradise,<note>Ibid. folio 126b.</note> which sounds fantastic; but the tree is allegorical and moves with man

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Zoharic interpretations of the Fall, moving from the symbolic nature of the serpent and spiritual death to the consequences of Adam's actions for his descendants and the introduction of the dual spirits—good and evil—that accompany a person through life.</meta>

throughout his pilgrimage. Adam was also the cause of the eternal banishment of his descendants. In dealing with other sections of the Zohar, we will have the opportunity to refer again to the myth of the Fall and will observe that it is further complicated by the introduction of monstrous elements.

If we turn now to the mystery of original sin, we find that it is intensified rather than explained. In Psalm 91:11, it is said: "He shall give His angels charge over you, to keep you in all your ways," a passage which the New Testament applies in a special manner to Christ. The Zohar explains it as follows: "When man comes into the world, there appears in him at that moment the evil spirit which always accuses him. . . . This evil spirit never forsakes man from the day that he is born into the world. But the good spirit comes to the man from the day that he is made clean. When does a man become clean? At the age of thirteen years. Then he joins himself with both spirits, one on the right hand and one on the left—the good to the right and the evil to the left—and these are the two spirits which are appointed ever to remain by man. If he strives after perfection, the evil spirit is restrained, so that the right rules the left, and then both unite to secure him in all his ways."<note>*Ibid.* folio 95b.</note> It follows from this statement that the child is delivered over to iniquity in a special manner, as he does not receive the good angel until he reaches the age of puberty.

Closely connected with the doctrines of the Fall of man and original sin is that of the Messiah, who,

for Jew and Christian alike, is a deliverer and an atonement, and much of what we find in the Zohar recalls a host of Talmudic speculations on the same subject. In common with all souls, that of the Messiah is pre-existent. It was the spirit of the King Messiah which brooded over the waters at the creation.* He governs both above and below, gladdening the heart of Israel, but bringing a judgment on idolatrous nations. When the souls in the Garden of Eden behold the righteous suffering for their Master, they return and inform the Messiah. “When they inform Him of the afflictions of Israel in exile, and of sinners in Israel who do not reflect so that they may know their Lord, He lifts up His voice and weeps, as it is written, ‘He was wounded for our transgressions’” [Isaiah 53:5].† We read, again, of a Palace of Wicked Children in the Garden of Eden, to which the Messiah goes, and there invokes upon Himself all the tribulations of Israel. “If He had not thus taken upon Himself the punishments for the transgressions of the law, no man would be able to endure them.”‡ More curious and more significant is the passage which follows immediately. “While the children of Israel lived in the Holy Land, they averted from the world all pain and suffering <note>In the sense of punishment for trespass</note> by their prayers, worship, and sacrifice, but now this is performed by the Messiah,” yet only so long as man remains in the world, for he must receive his retribution afterward. deeds, this notion of vicarious atonement is quite fantastic. Elsewhere, however, it is said that the righteous are the sacrifice and the atonement for the world. We must remember that the Zohar is a collection of various materials, and that such collections are not consistent in all respects. I must be content to refer briefly to a very long passage that has been the subject of much controversy regarding the date of the Zohar and is concerned with the appearance of the Messiah. It will be a time of woe and salvation, and of special affliction for Israel, but "he who is persistent in the faith shall attain the joy of the King." After this tribulation, further distress shall follow from the alliance of nations and kings, and then a pillar of fire extending from heaven to earth will become visible to all men, at which time the Messiah will come forth from the Garden of Eden and appear in Galilee, which was the first province to suffer destruction in the Holy Land and is the first, therefore, in which He must reveal himself. From there, He will stir up war against the whole world. There will be many signs in the heavens, stars warring against the star of the East, after which the Messiah will be invisible for twelve months; at the end of that period, He will be taken up into heaven to receive the Crown of the Kingdom. He will reveal Himself again and wage war against the whole world, etc.*

It is pointless to suppose that such a Messianic doctrine,† or, speaking generally, the scheme of

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Messianic doctrine from the previous page, transitioning into an analysis of Kabbalistic scriptural interpretation as a uniquely Jewish theosophical system rather than a universal ancient religion.</meta>

<page-num>206</page-num>
<header>The Doctrine and Literature of the Kabbalah</header>

Kabbalistic scriptural interpretation can have the slightest occult importance; it is essentially a Jewish scheme, assuming the exclusive claim of the Jewish people to divine election. It is the last source we should look to for confirmation of the romantic notion that a transcendental doctrine of absolute religion has been maintained from the distant past. Understood as it actually is—a treasury of Jewish theosophy, visionary doctrines, yearnings, and aspirations—it is a priceless memorial. Because the Jewish people are part of humanity, this work connects at a thousand points with the aspirations and yearnings of the heart of the whole world; however, it loses all its significance when one attempts to misplace its context. Because it is theosophical, though specifically Jewish, it naturally has points of connection with other occult systems. It also frequently touches upon matters that are beyond the range of what is typically understood as occultism—in a word, with the things of mysticism. Examples include its transcendental speculations on the identity of subject and object in God, and perhaps even in the mystical experience of the soul. It plays a major role in that strange doctrine of correspondences which we meet everywhere in the field embraced by the higher understanding of the term <term>Magic</term>. Indeed, it might be described as the extended mystery of correspondence. "Whatever is found on earth," says the <term>Zohar</term>, "has its spiritual counterpart above and is dependent on it. When the lower part is influenced, that which is set over it in the upper world is also affected, because all are united." The art of talismanic magic is the first logical consequence of this doctrine.

<summary>This page argues that Kabbalistic interpretation should be understood as a treasury of specifically Jewish theosophy rather than a universal ancient religion. It explains that the Kabbalistic doctrine of correspondences—where the physical world reflects the spiritual—is the foundational principle behind talismanic magic.</summary>
<keywords>Kabbalah, Jewish theosophy, mysticism, doctrine of correspondences, Zohar, talismanic magic, occultism</keywords>

Elsewhere it is said: “That which is above is in the likeness of that which is below, and the likeness of that which is below is in the sea [regarded as the mirror of the inferior heaven], but all is one.” <note>*Zohar*, Cremona, part ii. folio 9a. Compare part i. folio 91a: “As it is in all things below, so it is above.”</note> This is, of course, identical with the pseudo-Hermetic maxim, *Quod superius est sicut quod inferius et quod inferius est sicut quod superius, etc.*, which may even have been formulated along the lines of the Zohar.

The peculiar philosophical system of the Kabbalah, of course, receives a very full development in its chief literary memorial, but we are sufficiently acquainted with the main points of this system to make extensive quotation unnecessary. The primordial manifestation of the unknown God was the production of a luminous point, which is <term>Kether</term>, and it seems certain that by this the Zohar understands the Ego, for it says expressly that this was the light which <term>Elohim</term> created before everything. The nucleus of the luminous point is Thought, which is the beginning of all things. In its union with the Spirit <note><term>Kether</term> is itself the Spirit, according to the <term>Sepher Yetzirah</term>.</note> it is called <term>Binah</term>, and the diffusion of this Spirit brings forth a Voice, which in turn produces the Word, for the Zohar is a philosophy of the Word. <note>Cremona edition, part i. folio 131a.</note> In this Thought the forms of all things were evolved. The light of the Divine Consciousness is therefore the first matter of the manifested universe.

world," or <term>Atziluth</term>, from which the other worlds were subsequently emanated.* It is also said, as we have seen elsewhere, that "in the beginning was the will of the King, prior to any existence which came into being through emanation from this will."† With this will is connected the notion of thought as the principle of all things, but unrealized in the primordial condition and contained within itself. The expansion of thought produces spirit, which next assumes the title of intelligence; in this state, the thought is no longer self-centered. The spirit, in the course of its development, produces a voice or word, but the thought, the understanding, and the word are one alone, while the thought itself is bound up with <term>Ain Soph</term> and is never separated from it. This is the significance of the Scriptural statement that Jehovah is one and His Name is one.‡

Among the most suggestive and also the most recurrent teachings is the importance and universality of man in the scheme of things. For the <term>Zohar</term>, as for the poet, the world is "a disguised humanity," and "all that interests a man is man." It was the form of celestial man that God assumed at the beginning of His manifestation. This is the <term>Shekinah</term>, this is the <term>Mercabah</term> or chariot, and this also is the sacred name of Jehovah. The form of man comprises all that is in heaven or on earth, and prior to its manifestation, no form could subsist. It is the perfection of faith in all things and the absolute form of all. It is the summary and the most exalted term of creation. As soon as man appeared, all was

Likewise, in his threefold nature, he is a faithful image of what occurs on high, and the souls of the righteous are above all the powers and all the servants who are on high.* There is even a certain hidden and inconceivable sense in which man, through <term>Sephirotic</term> mediation, brings the <term>latens Deitas</term> into manifestation; and as all things exist and survive for man, so the problem of evil in the universe is solved for his benefit, as it is the condition of his development. Meanwhile, with a universal comprehensiveness that has no parallel in any sacred literature, the scheme of human existence is regarded by the <term>Zohar</term> with an optimism strange in its profundity—from man’s pre-existence in the <term>archetypal world</term> to the <term>beatific vision</term>, the absorption, and the eternal nuptials which await him.

The <term>pneumatology</term> of the <term>Zohar</term> is more fully developed in the associated sections of that work than in the commentary itself. The spiritual man is the highest level of creation; in him it was completed and he encompasses everything. The soul originates in the <term>Supreme Intelligence</term>. “At the time when the Holy, Blessed One desired to create the world . . . . , He formed all the souls which should afterwards be given to the children of men.”† It is also stated explicitly that the soul enters the body, when her time has come, with protests, grief, and unwillingness.

It is equally clear that the soul gains nothing from the experience, unless it is the dignity of an ordeal that has been successfully endured; for "all that souls learn in this world they already knew before they came into the world," which follows from the <term>Talmudic</term> doctrine that they were familiar with the entire <term>Thorah</term>.<note>Ibid., part iii. folio 28a. Compare, however, Franck, *La Kabbale*, p. 236, where the exact opposite teaching is attributed to the Zohar.</note> Their true purpose is to return to the <term>Deity</term>, but there are various dwelling places and destinies for the higher spiritual principles, and even in this life, they may depart from an unclean person.<note>Ibid., part i. folio 37b.</note> It may be added that souls are created in pairs, but these pairs do not always enter the world at the same time.

In the excerpts that have been provided, the <term>Zohar</term> appears at its best, but I should observe in conclusion that—much like a diverse collection—it combines precious insights with others of little value and many that are incredibly foolish. There are, for example, blatant absurdities in the <term>pneumatological</term> portions. When the soul enters the infant's body, it cannot find enough room and must wait for its physical shell to grow while experiencing significant discomfort. Examples of this kind could be multiplied indefinitely. They extend to every department of the doctrine and are especially prevalent concerning the <term>resurrection</term> of the dead, which, according to the <term>Zohar</term> (as one might expect), is the restoration of the physical body along with its original soul. Since this resurrection can only take place in <term>Palestine</term>, the bones of Israelites in exile will be transported there.

It should be added that the <term>Zohar</term> took the <term>Sepher Yetzirah</term> into its heart of hearts, dwelt upon it, extended, magnified, and almost transformed its symbolism. The Hebrew letters which figure in the earlier tract as the instruments of creation are, for the <term>Zohar</term>, the ciphers or garments of the written law—the expression of the <term>Thorah</term>—and the <term>Thorah</term> is the archetype of all the worlds. Whether or not we are able to agree with <term>Franck</term> that the <term>Sepher Yetzirah</term> ends where the <term>Zohar</term> begins, and that they are the exact complements of each other, it is certain that the instinct of the early students who singled out the “<term>Book of Formation</term>” from the rest of the pre-Zoharic <term>Midrashim</term> was not mistaken in regarding it as the head and source of <term>Kabalism</term>.

But, in conclusion, just as there was occultism and mysticism in Israel prior to the <term>Sepher Raziel</term> and to the <term>Zohar</term>, both were incorporated into the latter; in the process, both underwent a type of transmutation. As I venture to think, this process—like the “sea-change” of the poet—produced something more strange and rich. There are, at least, flights of mystic thought and aspiration in this great book of theosophy which were unknown to <term>Gebirol</term> and <term>Ibn Ezra</term>, and they are more direct and strong in their appeal to the inner consciousness of humanity in this dawn of the twentieth century than the famous commentary of <term>Azariel</term> or the School of <term>Isaac the Blind</term>. And to confess this is to admit immediately that the <term>Zohar</term> still has a message for the mystic. Perhaps all that is of value within it could be contained within a few pages,

## II. THE BOOK OF CONCEALMENT

The <term>Sepher Dzenioutha</term>, the <term>Book of Concealment</term> or of Occultation, to which occult writers have given so much prominence, is not, therefore—as they occasionally seem to suppose—the beginning of the great cycle titled the <term>Zohar</term>. In the <term>Sulzbach</term> edition, edited by <term>Rosenroth</term>, it begins at folio 176b and ends at folio 178b of the second volume. Most editions are either paged to correspond with one another or refer readers to the pagination of the previous codices. Among early codices, that of <term>Lublinensis</term> follows the <term>Cremona</term> edition, which, although used by <term>Rosenroth</term> for his references, was regarded by him as inferior to the simultaneous or slightly prior edition of <term>Mantua</term>. The latter he invariably terms <term>Codex correctus</term>. From the silence of occultists regarding the <term>Zohar</term> proper, one might judge that they do not consider it to be of great occult importance; but there is a simpler explanation, which, as has been seen, is not hard to find. The "<term>Book of Concealment</term>," on the contrary, though small in its dimensions, is of the highest occult importance; it is regarded as the root and foundation of the <term>Zohar</term>,<note>Mathers, *Kabbalah Unveiled*, p. 14.</note> as it is generally understood, and also as the most ancient portion of that collection, which is almost

The first chapter deals with the development of the <term>Vast Countenance</term>—the image of the Father of all things, the <term>Macroprosopus</term>—once equilibrium had been established in the universe of unbalanced forces. When equilibrium was established, the Countenance was manifested, the <term>Ancient of Days</term> appeared, and God emerged from His concealment.<note>See *Commentarius Generalis Methodicus . . . è Libro Emek Hammelech* in *Kabbala Denudata*, vol. ii. p. 47 and following of the second part.</note> This symbolism of the balance, depicting the harmony of the universal order

It will be seen, therefore, that the primal manifestation of Deity, which is connected with the concept of the Crown, has no other name than that which proclaims His self-existence, and that the Hebrew Jehovah is in a sense a reflected God. *Macroprosopus*, although manifesting in the Crown, is still regarded as forever hidden and concealed, in contrast to *Microprosopus*, who is both manifest and unmanifest. When the life-giving influx rushes forth from the Ancient One, amid the unbearable radiance of that great light, the likeness of a head appears. The distinction between the two Countenances is the distinction between the profile and the full face; for while the God of reflection is fully manifested, the Great Countenance is only partially revealed, which is why it is perhaps inexact

The <term>Book of Concealment</term> is described in its final words as the hidden and intricate mystery of the King; and since it adds that "blessed is he who comes and goes within it, knowing its paths and ways," there is an urgent need for an explanation of its meaning. This, as we shall see later, was explained in many rabbinical commentaries, all of which are admittedly later than the period when the <term>Zohar</term> was publicly released. There are, however, two works possessing the same authority as the <term>Book of Concealment</term>, for which the same authorship is also claimed, and which serve as extensions of that work. The first of these will be considered in the next section.

### III. THE GREATER HOLY SYNOD

The *Book of Concealment* has been simplified as much as possible in the preceding account. It may now be added that the work is anonymous; it quotes no rabbinical writers and contains no references that might provide a clue to its date. It has, however, two characteristics that give it the appearance of being a much older document than those which follow it immediately, which are designed—as already mentioned—to develop and explain it. These are its crude nature and the damage it appears to have undergone. The first translator, Rosenroth, fills the gaps and omissions caused by these issues with speculative words and passages placed within brackets, but even with these additions, it remains in an extremely flawed state. 

The treatise currently under consideration is entirely different in most respects. It possesses an almost literary quality, begins in a narrative form, and organizes the subsequent dialogues in a perfectly clear manner that requires few corrections. It deals, of course, with the crude symbolism of the previous book and is therefore admittedly off-putting to modern taste—a fact which has been noted by at least one sympathetic critic who was himself an elegant and highly suggestive writer.<note>Éliphas Lévi, *The Key to the Great Mysteries*.</note> The first point that otherwise deserves notice is that the *Greater Holy Synod* claims Rabbi Simeon Ben Jochai as the author of the *Book of Concealment*, and it contains the discourses of this Master in Israel, delivered in a

---

<note>Éliphas Lévi, *The Key to the Great Mysteries*.</note>

To find an account of Rabbi Simeon himself, we must turn to the tract *Sabbath* of the Babylonian version of the *Talmud*, which contains the story that I will provide here in its essence:

> "On a certain occasion, Rabbi Jehudah, Rabbi Josi, and Rabbi Shimeon were sitting together, and with them also was Jehudah, the son of proselytes. Rabbi Jehudah opened the conversation, saying, 'How beautiful are the works of this nation (the Romans). They have established markets; they have built bridges; they have opened bathhouses.' At this, Rabbi Josi remained silent. But Rabbi Shimeon ben Jochai answered, saying: 'All these things they have established for their own sake. Their markets are meeting places for prostitutes; they have built baths for their own pleasure, and bridges to collect tolls from those who cross them.' Jehudah, the son of proselytes, repeated this conversation, and it reached the ears of Caesar, who proclaimed: 'Jehudah, who praises us, shall be honored; Josi, who said nothing, shall be exiled to Saphoris (that is, Cyprus); Shimeon, who has criticized us, shall be put to death.' Rabbi Shimeon and his son then went out and hid themselves in the lecture hall, but afterwards in a cave, where a miracle occurred: a date tree and a spring of water appeared for them. The <term>Talmud</term> expressly mentions the learning obtained during this period, but without specifying its kind. According to the tradition of the <term>Kabbalists</term>, the “Book of Concealment” was the first form in which it was put into writing. The discourses of the “Greater Sacred Synod” were recorded by Rabbi Abba, and the same was done in the case of the “Lesser Synod.” When the conversation was about to begin, a voice heard in the air showed that the Supernal Synod had assembled in heaven to listen, and the commentators add that the souls of the righteous gathered around the speakers, coming from their rest in Paradise, as did the Holy <term>Shekinah</term> of the Divine Presence.

The explanations and developments concern the world in its void state before the manifestation of the Supreme Countenance, the configurations of that Countenance, or <term>Macroprosopus</term>, as well as <term>Microprosopus</term>, the Lesser Countenance, and in what manner the inferior depends on the superior. It must be said that the explanation and the extension

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Book of Concealment" and the "Greater Sacred Synod," concluding the thought that these texts do not fully reveal the hidden meanings of their symbolism to the uninitiated.</meta>

<page-num>220</page-num>

they neither are nor claim to be explanatory in the sense that they reveal the real significance of the symbolism. In fact, the treatise ends—like all treatises concerned with the mysteries of initiation—by saying that he is blessed who has known and beheld the hidden words and does not go astray in them. In an account like this one, which does not even pretend to be a summary, it is impossible to attempt a categorization of the unique symbolism with which the <term>Greater Synod</term> is concerned. It should be noted in this connection that the few modern writers on <term>Kabbalism</term> who claim to speak with authority and from within an occult circle of knowledge may have shown us glimpses in one or two rare instances of the system on which the symbolism is built, but they have done nothing to clarify or recommend it to our understanding. It is a question that is hard to approach from a literal perspective; and in the absence of initiation, the secret aspects of the literature are mostly a matter of curious speculation. Fortunately, it has other aspects that make it worth considering; otherwise, there would be no purpose for this inquiry.

The unbalanced forces of the universe—the world in its empty state—are considered under the symbolism of the kings who reigned in <term>Edom</term> before a king was raised up to rule over the children of <term>Israel</term>; that is to say, before the emanation of *<term>Microprosopus</term>.*\*

At that time there was neither beginning nor end, and the <term>Edomite kings</term> were without foundation. According to <term>Rosenroth</term>, this signifies the fall of creatures partly into a state of rest, such as that of matter, and partly into a state of excessive activity, such as that of the evil spirits. In this case, we are dealing not so much with cosmology as with the legends of souls. Similarly, when the “<term>Greater Synod</term>” represents the <term>Ancient of Ancients</term> creating and producing the essence of light, the same interpreter—who speaks with the authority of immense knowledge regarding the literature of <term>Kabbalism</term>—observes that the reference is to the Law,* in other words, to the letters of the alphabet, through the rearrangements of which the Law was later recorded. Furthermore, symbolism of this type is not simplified by its expansion; the record of <term>Rabbi Simeon</term>’s discourses is merely the “<term>Book of Concealment</term>” expanded through a visionary lens. Compare, for example, the description of *<term>Macroprosopus</term>* with the details on the same subject found in the previous section.

> “His garments are as white as snow, and His appearance is like a revealed face. He is seated upon a throne of glittering brightness so that He may subdue them. The whiteness of His bald head extends into forty thousand worlds, and from the

“Greater Synod” is that wrath may dwell with *Microprosopus*, but not in the *Ancient of Days*. So also the eyes of the *Lesser Countenance* possess lids; when the lids are closed, judgments subdue the Israelites, and the Gentiles have dominion over them. But the eyes, when they are open, are as beautiful as those of a dove, for they are then illuminated by the good eye. With one of those moving touches which occasionally soften the unyielding lines of Kabbalistic symbolism for a moment, it is said that two tears dwell in the eyes of the *Lesser Countenance*. When the *Holy of Holies* wills to have mercy on the Israelites, He sends down these two tears to grow sweet in the great sea of wisdom, and they emerge from there in mercy upon the chosen people. The specific seat of severity in *Microprosopus* is the nose, and judgment goes forth from it, except when the forehead of the *Vast Countenance* is uncovered, at which time mercy is found in all things.

As in the case of the *Ancient of Ancients*, the discourse pertaining to the beard of *Microprosopus* fills many chapters. These are full of strange <gloss>scholarly commentaries</gloss> on various passages of Scripture and detail minutely the structures of its nine divisions: what it conceals of the *Lesser Countenance*, what it permits to be revealed, observations on the descent of a holy and magnificent oil from the beard of *Macroprosopus*, and a general description of the similarities and differences between the two adornments.

The summary of the entire treatise may be given in the words of the original:
> "The Ancient of Ancients is in Microprosopus; all things are one; He was all things; He is all things; He will be all things; He shall know no change; He knows no change; He has known no change."<note>*Idra Rabba, or the Greater Synod, section xxxix, paragraph 920, in Kabbala Denudata, volume ii.*</note>

Thus God in manifestation is not truly separable from God in concealment; if the symbolism depicts Him in the likeness of humanity, it is by way of similarity and analogy.

At the conclusion of the "Greater Synod," we are told that three of the company died during the deliberations, and their souls were seen by the survivors being carried by angels behind the "expanded veil above."<note>*Ibid., section xlv, paragraph 1138.*</note>

western world, declaring with no uncertain voice that God is entirely without change or alteration—that wrath and judgment belong to man alone. It thus gives a new interpretation to the divine warning, “Judge not, lest you be judged,” and shows the higher significance of the equally divine promise, “I will repay.” For the true Kabalist, this could never mean that God would repay the sinner in kind—outrage for outrage, hate for hate. God’s repayment is either the compensation of eternal justice or the gift of eternal bounty. In a sense, the writers of the Zohar anticipated the most liberal conclusions of modern eschatology.<note>Franck summarizes the position as follows: Nothing is absolutely evil, nothing is eternally accursed—not even the archangel of evil—for a time will come when his name and angelic nature will be restored to him. *La Kabbale*, p. 217.</note> Among the fiery zealots of the Papal Church, it proclaimed for the first time the real meaning of the forgiveness of sins.

### IV. THE LESSER HOLY SYNOD

Similar in most of its characteristics to the longer discourse that preceded it, the “Lesser Holy Synod,” or *Idra Zuta*, is called by Rosenroth the swan song of Simeon ben Jochai—a supplement to the subjects that were not fully treated in the Greater Assembly. Since the master’s death is recorded at the end of the treatise, the translator’s words must be understood as referring to the instruction it contains, rather than to its narrative setting. The Synod consists

Simeon begins by affirming that it is a time of grace; he is conscious of his approaching end; he desires to enter the world to come without confusion; and he intends to reveal those sacred things in the presence of the Shekinah which until now have been kept secret. Rabbi Abba is appointed as scribe, and Simeon is the sole speaker. The discourse still concerns *Macroprosopus* and *Microprosopus*, with the correspondences between them, but it sketches only the subject of the concealed Deity and deals at great length with the manifestation of the Lower Countenance. In both cases, as would indeed be expected, it repeats, substantially and verbally, much of the preceding Synod; but it gives some additional symbolism, as, for example, concerning the three heads of *Macroprosopus*, "one within the other and the other above the other," and at a later stage a very considerable extension of symbolism regarding the first manifestation of the Ancient One under the form of male and female, which is, in fact, the emanation or "forming forth" of the supernal *Sephiroth*—*Chokmah*, or Wisdom, and *Binah*, or Understanding. So also the instruction concerning *Microprosopus*, when it is not a close reflection of the "Greater Synod," deals with His androgyne nature and His union with the Bride, who cleaves to the side of the male until she is separated, *et accedat ut copuletur cum eo*, face to face. Out of this comes the great Kabalistic doctrine of the sexes, so much in advance of its time, in whatever Christian century we may choose to place the literature, namely, that male and female

### V. THE DISCOURSE OF THE AGED MAN

The prominence given by Rosenroth to the *Book of Concealment* and its sequels was justified, as they are certainly the most arresting—I might almost say sensational—of all the tracts embedded in the *Zohar*. Those that remain to be examined will now be taken in the order in which they are placed in the *Kabbala Denudata*, and their lesser, or at least more sober, interest will be evident from the short analyses that will accompany their listing. The first to be listed is the one entitled *Sabah D’Mishpatim* (*The History of a Certain Old Man in the Section of Mishpatim*). The term *Sabah* signifies "ancient man" and *Mishpat* is "judgment," referring to Exodus, from the beginning of chapter 21—“Now these are the judgments”—to the conclusion of chapter 24. This discourse occurs in the Cremona edition, part 2, folio 43, column 169; in the Mantua edition, volume 2, folio 94; and in the Sulzbach edition, volume 2, folio 94a. It narrates a conversation between the prophet Elias and Rabbi Simeon ben Jochai on the subject of the ordeals and <term>metempsychosis</term> <note>The transmigration of souls or reincarnation.</note> of the soul, to which there are allusions at

<summary>This page concludes the description of Rabbi Simeon ben Jochai's miraculous funeral and begins an analysis of the "Sabah D’Mishpatim," a Zoharic tract concerning the soul's journeys and reincarnation.</summary>
<keywords>Rabbi Simeon ben Jochai, Zohar, Kabbala Denudata, Sabah D’Mishpatim, metempsychosis, reincarnation, Rosenroth, Exodus, Elias</keywords>

--- Page 256 ---

Isaac de Loria’s elaborate doctrine concerning the “Revolutions of Souls” is drawn from this discourse of Elias with the mystic light of Kabalism. We shall have to consider this doctrine again in connection with later Kabalism, in order to rid occultists of the idea that any reasonable view of reincarnation is contained in the Kabalistic writings. A specimen of the original text may, however, be given in this place, separated from many technicalities which would be burdensome to the beginner.

> “All souls go up with the revolutions or windings <note>that is, are subjected to the law of transmigration</note>, but the children of men do not know the ways of the Holy One; they do not know how He judges the children of men every day at all times, how the spirits (<term>Neshamoth</term>, the higher soul, the *anima animæ* of Christian theology) ascend to be judged before they descend into this world, or again how they go up to judgment after they have departed from this world; to how many revolutions and mysterious ordeals they (or their essential substances) are subjected by the Holy Blessed One; how many naked souls and how many naked spirits enter the other world, yet not through the King’s curtain; how many worlds revolve with them and how many wonders the world itself turns about in many concealed ways. and below. Therefore, when a human being deserves the *Nephesh* from the side of the daughter *Jechida*, this is to say: "She shall not go out as the men-servants do" <note>probably meaning that he shall serve God in His house forever, Exodus 21:7</note>.

This passage is worth quoting not only as an illustration of the discourse in which it occurs, but because it gives a clue to the probable meaning of occultists when they speak of a concealed sense in the Zohar. One should not suppose that when Kabbalism divides and subdivides the soul, it means anything other than to distinguish certain essences and qualities within it; in a word, it means what it says, just as modern theosophy does today when it affirms seven principles in man. The concealed sense of the Zohar, as previously indicated, is simply the extraction of some method from its vast and confused mass, which at first sight appears altogether incoherent. In the present instance, it will be seen that the soul-like nature of man has a sevenfold aspect, whereas other Kabbalistic teachings actually extend it to ten. When these discrepancies are harmonized, we have the concealed sense of the Zohar regarding the inner nature of man.

Perhaps we might understand it by supposing that the discourse of Elias really describes the development of mystical experiences in seven stages, ending, as it states literally, in the communication of the divine to man.

## VI. THE ILLUSTRIOUS BOOK

Long excerpts, claiming to come from a work titled *Sepher Ha Bahir*, or *Liber Illustris*, are found in the Cremona edition of the Zohar in the following places: Part I, columns 76, 79, 82, 86, 88, 104, 110, 112, 122, 125, 127, 130, 137, 138, 185, 241, 462; Part II, columns 145 and 259; Part III, columns 151, 176, 301, and 333. These are omitted in the so-called “Little Zohar” of Mantua, but they reappear in Rosenroth’s Sulzbach edition and in later editions based on it. In 1651, these excerpts were compiled into a single volume and published in Amsterdam, which was a major stronghold of the Jewish community at that time. A reprint of this volume appeared in Berlin in 1706.

Some interesting but complex questions arise when considering this work, which we know only through these quotations. On one hand, it is claimed to be older than any other Kabbalistic book and therefore of greater importance than the <term>Sepher Yetzirah</term> itself; on the other hand, it is asserted to be an obvious forgery, included in the same condemnation as the Zohar, and by implication, also the product of the imagination of Moses de Leon. Between these extreme views is the opinion that the existing extracts are unauthentic, but that an older Kabbalistic treatise once existed under the same title and is now lost. An examination of the verifiable facts does not, I believe, require us to accept any of these opinions, and a more modest, undecided conclusion will perhaps be the

<summary>This page introduces the Sepher Ha Bahir (The Illustrious Book), detailing its inclusion in various editions of the Zohar and its publication history in Amsterdam and Berlin. It outlines the scholarly debate regarding the book's authenticity, with theories ranging from it being the oldest Kabbalistic text to it being a forgery by Moses de Leon.</summary>

<keywords>Sepher Ha Bahir, Liber Illustris, Zohar, Cremona edition, Rosenroth, Amsterdam, Moses de Leon, Sepher Yetzirah, Kabbalistic literature</keywords>

In other words, there is evidence that the <term>Sepher Ha Bahir</term> existed before the publication of the <term>Zohar</term>,* but there is no evidence that it preceded it by a significant period, and there is no way of knowing whether or not the extracts occurring in the <term>Zohar</term> represent the original work in some sense.

It is regrettable that occult students have overlooked the fragments of the <term>Sepher Ha Bahir</term> just as they have overlooked the <term>Zohar</term> itself, and for the same reason—namely, because it is not available in translation. It would have been interesting to know whether they would have accepted the Kabbalistic legend that has spread concerning it. One aspect of that legend appears in the bibliography of Papus; however, it indicates no first-hand research and simply reproduces information for which Molitor is the stated source. In the "Methodized Summary of the Kabalah," the president of all <term>Martinism</term> attributes the <term>Sepher Ha Bahir</term>—which he translates as "Light in the Darkness"—to Rabbi Nechoniah ben Hakannah, the master of Rabbi Ismaelis ben Eliezer, the high priest, who flourished during the half-century before the birth of Christ. Such is the Kabbalistic legend regarding the authorship of the work. Some notable sayings of Nechoniah are preserved in the Talmudic collections, and other works are also attributed to him, namely:

relate the same story, but none of them challenge the excerpts found in the *Zohar*, accepting them explicitly as genuine, while all also agree that the *Bahir* was regarded by Kabbalists as their oldest document. The question of authenticity was first raised in recent times by Simon, who, speaking of the book printed in Holland, observes: "It does not appear that this is the ancient *Bahir* of the Jews, which is much more extensive and has not yet been printed."* It is obvious that this is neither the language of criticism nor of knowledge; we may infer that Simon was unaware of the fact that the Amsterdam publication only collected the Zoharic extracts, and that he might not have challenged the extracts had he been aware of that circumstance. Bartolocci mentions a general opinion that manuscript copies of the *Bahir* were to be found in many Continental libraries and specifies one such manuscript in the Vatican collection.

The challenging of the Zoharic excerpts naturally became part of the general charge against the *Zohar* itself; the theory which attributed that work to Moses de Leon was exceedingly comprehensive and made a clean sweep of everything included in it. It finds an almost exact parallel in the consistent application of those principles which are held to prove the Baconian authorship of Shakespeare's plays; serving equally well for Marlowe, Massinger, and all Elizabethan literature, that literature is directly or indirectly attributed to Bacon. What Raymond did, as they say, could not have been impossible for Moses. Graetz, the German historian of Jewry, whose distinctive criticism of Kabbalistic literature has gained much popularity, lays down a principle of criticism which ought to be written in capital letters at the head of most accusations against the Zohar: namely, that it is not compulsory for a hostile critic to be more careful in his arguments than those who plead in its defense. Without seeking to determine what is compulsory in criticism, it may be observed that there is also no binding law to enforce serious consideration for a scholar who adopts that principle. What Graetz did openly has been done silently or unconsciously by others. ...production from the same formative period as the work that is supposed to quote it. We do not know when the extracts were inserted into it; since they are absent from the Mantua edition, which was published at the same time as the Cremona codex that contains them, it is possible they were first added when the *Zohar* was prepared for printing under the supervision of Rabbi Isaac Delates—that unknown but “highly learned Jew, unsurpassed in all the branches of knowledge required,” whom the publisher describes. In this case, they have no connection with Moses de Leon.

There is, of course, little agreement in hostile or, indeed, any Kabbalistic criticism. Just as one defender of the *Zohar* challenges the *Bahir* excerpts, those same excerpts have been praised as the prototype and actual inspiration for the *Zohar*. This view, though of little importance in any case, implies that the *Bahir* existed before the alleged date when the *Zohar* was produced from the head of Moses de Leon—like Minerva from the head of Jupiter—ready-made and in a single leap. Morinus, who left perhaps the most sensible review of the subject, bases his opinion that the *Bahir* was a product of the thirteenth century on the silence of writers before that date, particularly Moses Nachmanides, a Kabbalistic Jew of Jerusalem whose literary work belongs to the period around 1250. According to Wolf, the first reference to the *Bahir* was made by Rabbi Shem Tob, who was a contemporary of Moses de Leon but belonged to a younger generation. This, however, is a mistake, because Rabbi Azariel—the author of the great treatise on the Sephirotic system and born, as we have seen, around 1160—in his commentary on the Canticle of

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the historical dating and origins of the Sepher Ha-Bahir, refuting the claims that it was a 13th-century invention by showing it was quoted in earlier works under different titles.</meta>

<page-num>241</page-num>
<header>The Written Word of Kabbalism</header>

The commentary on the Canticles, sometimes attributed to Nachmanides, quotes the *Bahir*, not by its own name but under the title of *Yerushalmi*. The proof is that the Italian Jew Recanati, a contemporary of Moses de Leon, used these quotations and, misled by the name, inferred that they were from the Jerusalem Talmud; however, he later discovered them in the *Bahir*, to which a Palestinian origin is attributed. We have no grounds for guessing by how much time this lost treatise preceded Azariel, but the position held by Wolf and Morinus is destroyed by the fact recorded here. This leaves the *Bahir* exactly where we are inclined to place it: between the date when the *Sepher Yetzirah* is first mentioned and the publication of the *Zohar*.

The name *Bahir* refers to Job 37:21: "And now men see not the bright light which is in the clouds," according to the Authorized Version, or according to Dr. Durell's amended translation, "And now men see not the light which is above (or within) the clouds, etc." Hence, "light in darkness" is a good equivalent for the Hebrew word. The subject matter of the book, which takes the form of a dialogue between certain enlightened doctors, is the mystery inherent in the divine names, and it contains a very full exposition of the celebrated *Shem HaMephorash* <note>The explicit or articulated name of God</note>, the expounded name of deity. It belongs, therefore, to the least philosophical part of Kabbalism, and we can understand and sympathize with Franck's instinctive dislike of accepting the excerpts by which the work is known to us. Facts, however, must take precedence over preferences; and though the later history of the doctrine of divine names may well make an admirer of the higher Kabbalism ashamed of

The first concerns *Jechidah*, the fifth principle of the human soul.

> "It is written: 'The silver is mine and the gold is mine,' says the Lord of Hosts. What does this mean? It is like a king having two treasures, one of silver and one of gold. The first he put to his right and the second to his left, saying: 'This is prepared so that it is easy to spend it.' He has done everything in an easy way. Hence it is said: 'Your right hand, O Lord, has become glorious in power.' (Exodus 15:6). If a man may rejoice in his inheritance, it is good, but if not, it is said: 'Your right hand, O Lord, has dashed the enemy in pieces.' (Ibid.) What does this signify? Surely this is the gold. It is written: 'The silver is mine and the gold is mine.' Why do they call it gold? Because three measures are included in it. <note>That is to say, the word consists of three letters: Z, H, B—*Zayin*, *He*, *Bet*</note>. The *Zayin* is seven measures <note>that is, this letter represents the number 7</note>; the *He* is Unity <note>that is, A-Ch-D-V-Th, Unity=419=5 according to Kabbalistic addition, and 5 is the number of *He*</note>; the *Bet* <note>representing the number 2</note> signifies *Chokmah* and *Binah*, and they are called *Neshamah* because of the last five *Sephiroth*. The *Neshamah* has five names: *Nephesh*, *Ruach*, *Neshamah*, *Chaiah*, and *Jechidah*."

The second excerpt concerns the Fall of Man, and its quotation is of some importance for the purpose of this study.

> "It is written: 'Now the serpent was more subtle than any beast of the field' (Genesis 3:1). Rabbi Isaac said: 'The serpent is the evil inclination.' Rabbi Judah said: 'It is a real serpent.' It remains to be said that William Postel is reported to have translated the *Bahir* into Latin, but if so, I can find no record that the translation was ever printed.

### VII. THE FAITHFUL SHEPHERD

The Zoharistic treatise bearing this title records conversations between Simeon ben Jochai and Moses, who appeared to the great light of Kabalism and gave him many instructions and revelations. Elias took part in the conference, and the witnesses included not only Abraham, Isaac, Jacob, Aaron, David, and Solomon, but God Himself. This indicates that in spite of the transcendental doctrine of *Ain Soph* and the Two Countenances, the *Zohar* occasionally returns to the same anthropomorphic conceptions found in the Talmud. Ginsburg says: "The main purpose of this section is to show the dual and allegorical significance of the Mosaic commandments and prohibitions, as well as the Rabbinical injunctions and religious practices which prevailed in the course

In either case, the “Discourse of the Faithful Shepherd” contains much that is important to our purpose. Its views on vicarious atonement and on the Messiah to come will enable us to further appreciate the value of the occult standpoint; some of its moral teachings will illustrate its ethical position; its references to the <term>Shekinah</term> will cast light on this curious feature of Kabalism; and its speculations on angels and demons will show the Zoharistic foundation for the later system of <term>pneumatology</term> <note>The study of spirits or spiritual beings.</note> which was developed by Isaac de Loria.

The discourse introduces two phases of vicarious atonement: the one effected through the sufferings of righteous men and the other by means of the Messiah.

This may be illustrated by the parable of a shepherd who saw a wolf approaching to tear apart and destroy his sheep. What did this shepherd do? Being wise, he gave the wolf the strongest and sturdiest bell-wether—even the one that the flock was accustomed to follow—and while the wolf was carrying it away, the shepherd hurried with his sheep to a place of safety. Then, returning, he rescued the bell-wether from the wolf. So does the Holy Blessed One deal with a generation: He surrenders a righteous man into the power of the accuser for the salvation of the generation through him. Theologians, I believe, have had some difficulty in defining the role of Job’s accuser, and it is perhaps most accurate to say, along with the poet, that “He, too, is God’s minister.” However, the Zoharic commentary on Job makes him, in most respects, a match for the Almighty, who must resort to a trick in order to save His people. Thus, not only the occasional good actions of those who are habitually wicked, but even all natural goodness, can find their reward only in this world. The Kabbalah is not marred by systematic atrocities of this kind. There are also times in which it loses its grotesqueness for a moment and, by some successful reference to Scripture, illustrates a basic spiritual truth—as, for example, concerning the change necessary for sinners.

> "Those who are burdened with sin need a change of place, a change of name, and a change in their actions, just as it was said to Abraham: *Get out of your country* (Gen. 12:1). A change of deeds: he changed from his former evil actions to good actions."†

<page-num>252</page-num>

# The Doctrine and Literature of the Kabalah

The Zohar indeed has no special mystical importance; it concerns only the initial fact of spiritual life.

We have seen in the fragments of the *Bahir* that the scriptural history of the Fall of man is distorted rather than clarified by Zoharic commentary. The consequences of the first sin are described in the *Faithful Shepherd* as follows:

> "Come and see! If Adam had not sinned, man would not have known the taste of death before he ascended to the superior world; but because he sinned, he became acquainted with the taste of death before ascending to the world above. Through Adam’s sin, the rational spirit (*Ruach*) is separated from the body, which remains in this world. It must cleanse itself in the river of fire (Daniel 7:10) to receive punishment, after which it goes up to the Garden of Eden, which is above the earth. There, other garments of light are prepared for it, in conformity with the appearance of the body in the present world; it is clothed in them, and there is its habitation forever."<note>Ibid., part iii, folio 79b. According to the *Idra Zuta*, the Upper or true Eden is the principle of life and understanding.</note>

This passage is important because it shows that Jewish theosophy has nothing better to offer us than the old Biblical instruction that sin "brought death into the world and all our woes." It is not our purpose here to question that doctrine, but simply to demonstrate that the Zohar, where it is intelligible, does not improve upon accepted religious instruction.

Let us now select an instance from one of those portions which are more peculiarly Kabalistic in their

subject. The Zoharistic speculations on the Shekinah have an air of mystic symbolism that would perhaps naturally be missing in a commentary on ordinary doctrinal matters. It is said that the relationship of the Shekinah to the other lights of creation is like that of the soul to the body, but *she*—for the divine manifestation is presented under a feminine aspect—"stands to the Holy Blessed One as the body stands to the soul." In this, of course, there is nothing profound; the Shekinah is the garment of the Almighty. But the discourse of the Faithful Shepherd adds that all are one—that is, God is one with His manifestation. This may be illustrated by the much more interesting and spiritual doctrine of the Eucharist: the bread is the garment of Christ, the manner of His manifestation in His Church, but Christ, by the hypothesis of the doctrine, is one with the veil that He assumes. It is different in man, says the Zohar. "His body is earth, but the soul is called reason. The one is death, the other is life." This is the ascetic notion that modern occultism has agreed to reject. "But the Holy Blessed One is life, and the Shekinah also is life. Hence it is written: She <note>meaning the Shekinah, although the Scriptural reference is to Wisdom</note> is a tree of life to those who can take hold of her" (Proverbs 3:18). The Shekinah of Kabbalism is not, however, merely the visible splendor that shone in the Holy of Holies. The Faithful Shepherd affirms that the Holy Blessed One is concealed in the mysteries of the *Torah* and is known or manifested through the commandments, for these are His Shekinah and this is His image. This is a very beautiful, spiritual and poetic conception, and it does

Today, we may not feel inclined to accept the alleged manifestation in the Temple literally or, so to speak, physically; here the <term>Zohar</term> helps us toward something truer and more profound than the literal legend, and we gladly acknowledge that the small nation of Palestine, surrounded by idolatrous nations, truly had something of the divine in the law given to them. The passage continues: “As He is humble, so the <term>Shekinah</term> is humility; as He is benevolent, so she is benevolence; as He is strong, so she is the strength of all the nations of the world; as He is the truth, so she is the truth; as He is the prophet, so she is the prophetess; as He is righteous, so she is righteousness; as He is King, so she is Queen; as He is wise, so she is wisdom; as He is intelligent, so she is His intelligence; as He is the crown, so she is His diadem, the diadem of glory. Therefore, the masters have decided that all those whose inner nature does not match their outward appearance shall have no admission to the house of doctrine. As the image of the Holy Blessed One—whose interior He is, and whose outward splendor is the <term>Shekinah</term>—He is the interior internally, and she is the exterior externally, so that no difference exists between her, the outward, and Him, the inward. Since she is an outflow from Him, all difference between the external and internal is removed; and as, further, the inner nature of <term>YHVH</term> is concealed, He is therefore only named by the name of the <term>Shekinah</term>,

<term>Adonai</term>; hence the masters say, "Not as I am written [<term>YHVH</term>] am I read."*

The connection between the <term>Shekinah</term> and <term>Malkuth</term>, in light of the alleged unity of God and the vestment that conceals Him, suggests the identity of the divine and the universe, but it is only in the sense of immanence. The <term>Kabalah</term>, as we have seen many times, is in some respects the very opposite of pantheism.

Our quotations must close with a few references to the pneumatology of the *Faithful Shepherd*. They concern, first, the great Presence Angel <term>Metatron</term>, who is the sole occupant of the Briatic world, just as the supernal <term>Adam</term> is of the world of <term>Atziluth</term>. He is the garment of <term>Shaddai</term>. According to some, his form is that of a boy, while others ascribe a female aspect to this angel. This shows a connection with the <term>Shekinah</term>, and indeed <term>Metatron</term>, with the difference of one added letter, signifies the "cohabiting glory."† There is, secondly, some information concerning <term>Samaël</term>, or Satan, and his wife <term>Lilith</term>. The former was once a servant of the Holy Blessed One, and the latter was a maid of <term>Matroncetha</term>.‡ Their ultimate destruction is hinted at, but in the meantime, <term>Lilith</term> is the devastation of the world and the lash in the hands of the Holy Blessed One to strike the guilty. So she, too, is God’s minister.

## VIII. THE HIDDEN THINGS OF THE LAW

The surviving fragments of this work are located by Rosenroth in the Cremona edition as follows: Part I, columns 221, 258, 262, 370; Part II, column 250. Dr. Ginsburg has discovered others in the Amsterdam edition, to which his references are made. They cover topics addressed by other sections of the <term>Zohar</term>, such as the evolution of the <term>Sephiroth</term>, the emanation of the primordial light, and so forth. In short, the contents contain nothing that requires much of our time. As an example of the childishness and pretentiousness of its interpretation, let us look at the following passage:

> "It is written: 'If you faint in the day of adversity, your strength is small' (Proverbs 24:10). 'Your strength'; this means if his grasp on the <term>Torah</term> becomes weak. 'In the day of adversity'; this means that when he weakens in this way, his strength becomes small <note>thus changing the simple statement of Scripture into a foolish platitude</note>. What is meant by 'your strength becomes small'? It refers to <term>Ko-ach</term> <note>the initials of words signifying the Throne of Glory</note>. Then evil is expelled, so that it cannot come near a person and cannot accuse them <note>the meaning seems to be that when a person does not weaken in adversity, their strength is like that of the right hand, as the Throne of Glory is on the right side of God</note>. But when a person deviates from the <term>Torah</term> <note>or has a weak hold on it</note>, then the strength is the strength of the left hand, because the evil of the left side rules over the person and sets aside the Throne of

Those who prefer the mysteries of the <term>Torah</term> have full opportunities for entering more deeply into the significance of Solomon's simple aphorism, for they have only to remember that the Throne of Glory is a title of <term>Tiphereth</term>, and occasionally of <term>Malkuth</term>, because it is the seat of <term>Tiphereth</term>.

In a more notable passage, which is supposed by Myer to distinguish certain stages of mystic vision, it is said that the will of the King is discovered in three colors. The first is above and so far away that no eye can perceive it in its purity, but it is distinguished (dimly) by contracting the range of vision <note>that is, by the closing of the eyelids, as in blinking</note>. The second color is seen with one eye shut, and cannot be seen by the other eye except when that eye is shut so that it can see little, as in blinking. The brilliance of the light could not be endured otherwise. Of this it is written, "What do you see?" <note>Jeremiah 1:11. The prophet, however, did not see the will of God but a branch of an almond tree</note>. The third color is that bright luminous mirror which cannot be looked into at all, except between the rolling of the eyes when the lids are altogether closed and they move in their sockets. There can then be seen in that rolling the light of the luminous mirror, and the color of it can be understood only by him who beholds the shining with eyes shut, hence it is written, "The hand of the Lord was upon me" (Ezekiel 37:1), and "The"

No doubt the Kabbalists had visions and means of inducing visions, as also did Boehme, St. John of the Cross, and all the seers and mystics; but this clumsy process confuses cause and effect while offering no intelligible result.

As another example, let us see what the Mysteries of the *Torah* can tell us of the three angels who appeared to Abraham.

> "It is written, 'And lo, three men stood by him' (Gen. xviii. 2). These are three angels, clothed in ether, who came down to this world and were seen in appearance even as a child of man. And they were three like that which is above, because the rainbow is only seen in three colors <note>this point should interest ethnologists</note>. Surely this is so. And these are three men; three colors: white, red, and green. The white is Michael, because he is the right side; the red is Gabriel, because he is the left side; and the green is Raphael. And these three colors are those of the rainbow, because it is never seen otherwise than with them. Hence it is written, 'And the Lord appeared unto him [Abraham] in the plains of Mamre' (Gen. xviii. 1); that is, the Shekinah revealed itself in these three colors. It is also written, 'And they that be wise shall shine as the brightness of the firmament' (Dan. xii. 3). They shall shine with a light which is enkindled by igniting a splendor. That brilliant light which is hidden—the spark of all sparks, of all lights—is therein invisible and hidden, concealed and made known, seen and yet not beheld. This shining

## IX. THE SECRET COMMENTARY

We know that Scott provided headings to many chapters of his romances using fake quotations from old plays that existed only in his imagination, and it occasionally happened that these mythical excerpts contained stronger lines than much of his acknowledged poetry. Those who believe that Moses de Leon wrote the Zohar out of his own head will account in a similar way for the many fragments of unknown treatises that are found only in that work. Of some of these, it may also be said that they are more interesting than the Zohar proper. As we have seen, the whole world of Kabbalism has agreed to exalt the "Book of Concealment" over all the other discourses attributed to Simeon ben Jochai, but for the purposes of our present inquiry, it must be confessed that considerable interest attaches to the "Secret Commentary." The surviving fragments of this work are found in the Cremona edition at the following places: Part I, columns 257, 260, 261, 264, 265, 268, 269, 272, 273, 276, 296, 370. The field which they cover is chiefly that of the destiny of souls, future punishments and rewards, the resurrection of the body and the doctrine

Setting aside such grotesque details as the pausing of the soul in the throat, and the simple confusion caused by the mention of an oral statement, what is described in the passage above is almost identical to the Catholic concept of the <term>particular judgment</term>. According to this, the Christian soul—whatever sentence is about to be pronounced upon it—sees Christ, just as the soul of the Jewish <term>Kabalist</term> sees the glory of the Presence. We find, therefore, that the <term>Zohar</term> at its best has no richer gift to offer us than a variation upon everything that the occultist has agreed to set aside as belonging to the literal word and convention. It is, perhaps, unnecessary—and for the <term>Kabalist</term> it is certainly useless—to point out that the appearance of the angels to <term>Abraham</term> on the plains of <term>Mamre</term> cannot, by any natural process of <term>exegesis</term>, support the interpretation placed on it. It is understood, of course, that the <term>Kabalah</term> has no such processes.

Having seen how the soul is judged, another quotation will provide us some vague idea of the future happiness of the righteous:

> “<term>Rabbi Joseph</term> said: At that time the righteous person shall attain full knowledge; namely, on the day when the <term>Holy Blessed One</term> shall rejoice over His works, the righteous shall know Him in their hearts, and their understanding shall be as great as if they had seen Him with their eyes, for it is written: *And it shall be said in that day, Lo, this is our God* (<term>Isaiah 25:9</term>). The joy of the soul when dwelling in the body surpasses..."

It must be acknowledged that in this very interesting passage, the Zoharic doctrine is a great advance upon the monstrous allegory of the salted Leviathan in the Talmud. Regarding the future condition of the wicked, the Secret Commentary provides the following statement by Rabbi Samuel, speaking on the authority of Rabbi Jacob: "The souls of the wicked are delivered into the hands of the angel Dumah <note>The Angel of Silence who, according to the Talmud, has charge of disembodied spirits</note>, who conducts them to Gehennon for judgment."† Regarding the duration of punishment, the opinions expressed in the Zohar are confused, if not conflicting, but it does not seem to be everlasting.‡

Two other passages in the Secret Commentary refer to the resurrection of the dead:

> "Rabbi Isaac spoke and said: *The mandrakes give forth a fragrance* (Song of Solomon 7:13). Our rabbis have learned: In the time which is to come, the Holy Blessed One will quicken the dead and will awaken them from the dust, so that they are no longer an earthly habitation. Previously, they were created from the dust of the earth, which has no permanence, as it is written: *And the Lord God formed man of the dust of the ground* (Gen. 2:7). But in that time, they shall be sifted from the dust of that structure and shall stand as a firm building, and the same shall be for them a lasting habitation. Thus it is written: *Shake yourself from the dust; arise . . . . O captive daughter of Zion* (Isa. 52:2).

> "They shall stand firm; they will rise up from under the earth and receive their *Neshamoth* in the land of Israel. For at that time, the Holy Blessed One will diffuse over them all kinds of fragrances from the garden of Eden, as it is written: *The mandrakes give forth a scent*. Rabbi Isaac added: Do not call it mandrakes <gloss>*Dudaim*</gloss> but friendship <gloss>*Dodim*</gloss>, meaning that body and soul are friends and companions to each other. Rabbi Na'hman said: This word truly means *Dudaim*, for the *Dudaim* <note>understood as the love-apple</note> bring forth love in the world. What does this mean? 'They give a pleasant scent.' It describes the integrity of their deeds, through which their Creator becomes known and understood by their generation. 'And at our gates are all kinds of pleasant fruits, new and old' (Song of Solomon 7:13). 'Our gates,' that is, the gates of heaven, which are open and through which the *Neshamoth* shall descend into the bodies. 'All kinds of pleasant fruits'; these are the *Neshamoth*. 'New and old,' that is, those whose souls have departed from them for many years and those which have left them for just a few days past, yet deserve, through the integrity of their actions, to enter the world to come."*

From this, it seems to follow undoubtedly that the Kabbalistic world to come is the millennial earth of Christianity, and therefore the destiny of the righteous is essentially identical in the Zohar and in the Apocalypse. The theory of the risen body in the "Secret Commentary" also recalls that which was expressed much better by St. Paul when he said: "It is sown a natural body, it is raised a spiritual body."

# The Written Word of Kabalism

The other passage is as follows:—
> "While the <term>*Neshamah*</term> receives its nourishment from the supernal splendor, the Holy Blessed One says to the angel <term>*Dumah*</term>: 'Go and proclaim to that body that I am prepared to bring it back to life at the time when I shall raise up the righteous in the age to come.' But the body answers: 'Will I have pleasure when I have decayed?' <note>Referring to Genesis 18:12 where Sarah says: 'After I have grown old, shall I have pleasure?'</note> 'Even when I have decayed in the dust, and have lived in the earth where worms and moles have eaten my flesh, will it be possible for me to be renewed?' Then the Holy Blessed One says to the <term>*Neshamah*</term>: 'It is therefore written: And the Lord said to Abraham . . . is anything too hard for the Lord?' (Genesis 18:13, 24). 'At a time which is known to Me for raising the dead, I shall bring back that body to you, made completely new, just as it was before, so that it may be like the holy angels. And that day is set apart for Me to rejoice with them, as it is written: The glory of the Lord shall endure forever; the Lord shall rejoice in His works (Psalm 104:31).'"*

We see, therefore, that on the most important of all subjects—namely, the destiny of the soul—the <term>Kabalah</term> has nothing to offer us but what Jews and Christians possess independently of any secret doctrine. We do not have to consider here whether this common doctrine is insufficient, but only to remember that it is held this way by occultists. That the risen body is transfigured we learn from the following passage, which is also an example of <term>Kabalistic</term> angelology.
