> “When it is said: *His servant* (Genesis 24:2), this means the servant of <term>Elohim</term>. ‘The oldest servant of his house’ (*Ibid.*). Who is this? It is <term>Metatron</term>, who is appointed to glorify the body in the grave. Hence it is written: *And Abraham said to his eldest servant* (*Ibid.*), namely, to <term>Metatron</term>, the eldest of his house, for he is the first of the creatures of <term>Elohim</term>, governing all that belongs to Him. The Holy Blessed One has given him dominion over all His hosts. And we have learned, said <term>R. Simeon</term>, that <term>R. Joseph</term> said on the authority of <term>Rab</term> that all the hosts of the servant take delight and felicity in the purity of the soul. We have learned also that the light of the soul in the world to come is greater than the light of the Throne and that the soul takes the light from the Throne.” *

The last statement seems to mean that the glory of God is essential to the <term>Godhead</term>, but the soul must acquire her luster.

We must leave these interesting fragments at this point. Much as we may despise the imbecile theory which would attribute them to the inventive genius of <term>Moses de Leon</term>, it seems quite clear that they provide no support for the occult estimate of the <term>Zohar</term>.

Beyond these, there are various lesser sections which may be noted for the sake of completeness. They fall under two heads:

*a.* Certain fragments, not of considerable importance, which are common to the two *editiones principes* <note>first editions</note>, namely: 1, “Additional Pieces”; 2, Excerpts from a commentary on Ruth; 3, Excerpts from a work entitled “Mansions or Abodes”; 4, One portion of a treatise on the Secret of Secrets.

*b.* Other fragments which are unique to the Cremona edition and are therefore missing from that of Mantua, namely: 1, The quaint history and discourse of a Young Man; 2, Excerpts from an Explanation of the Law; 3, Excerpts from a Commentary on the Song of Solomon; a discourse beginning “Come and See!”; 4, Some pieces entitled “Traditional Receptions.”

## C. THE MANSIONS OR ABODES

We have already become acquainted with a work prior to the appearance of the <term>Zohar</term> that contains a methodical description of heaven. It must not be confused with the excerpts which, under the title mentioned above—termed <term>HIKLVTh</term> in the original and translated as "Palaces" by Rosenroth—provide an account of the structure of Paradise and the infernal regions. Their locations in the Cremona edition can be found in Part I, column 116 *and following*; Part II, column 358 *and following*, and column 438. The mansions are seven in number and were the original habitations of the earthly Adam. After the fall of man, they were reconstituted and became the abode of the saints. The term that signifies Mansion, Temple, or Palace is applied to <term>Malkuth</term>, in which <term>Tiphereth</term> is said to be concealed as if in a palace. Likewise, the name Adonai <note>ADNI</note>, meaning Lord, is the Palace of the Tetragrammaton, because it has the same numerical value as HIKL (Palatium), which is 65. This name is attributed to <term>Binah</term>, and in a special manner to <term>Kether</term>, on the authority of the <term>Zohar</term> itself; for HIKL, Palace, is the place in which HKL, that is, the

The *<term>Zohar</term>* proper also has a good deal to tell us concerning the seven heavens, one above the other, like the layers of an onion. “Each heaven trembles with fear of its Lord, through whom they all exist and all are taken away. Over all the Holy Blessed One holds all in His power.” There are also seven earths below, arranged in the same manner. “These earths are disposed according to their names, and between them is the <term>Garden of Eden</term> and <term>Gehenna</term>.” They are inhabited by creatures of whom some have four faces, some two, while others have a single face, like humanity. They are not the children of Adam; some of them are clothed in skins and others in shells, “like the worms which are found in the earth.” It would serve no purpose to enlarge upon monstrous inventions of this kind. The concealed meaning which some occultists suppose them to possess is again evidently the plan upon which they are based, and to understand them is to know the method by which they can be calculated, so to speak. In neither case are they justified by reason. For example, we have just seen that *<term>ADNI</term>* is the mansion of *<term>JHVH</term>*; by counting the numerical values of these names, we see why the <term>Kabalists</term> said this, but we do not see that it served any reasonable purpose to say it. It is entertaining and curious,

### D. THE SECRET OF SECRETS

The only surviving fragment of this treatise is found in Part II of the Cremona edition, beginning at column 134. It deals, first, with the connection between the soul and the body—of which a sufficient idea has been provided by previous quotations—and, second, with physiognomy, which undoubtedly connects in a certain way with the lower branches of occult science but is not very important to our inquiry. It may, however, be worth mentioning that Kabbalistic physiognomy proves—as might be expected—to have no connection at all with any accepted principles that this empirical art might be thought to have achieved; indeed, it is purely arbitrary and conventional. Lavater was something of a mystic, but fortunately for his subject, he borrowed nothing from—and likely knew nothing of—the *<term>Zohar</term>* and its associations. Four general types of the human face are distinguished, and these are linked to the faces of the four living creatures in Ezekiel’s vision. We thus have the leonine, bovine, and aquiline types, and another that is less easy to describe but corresponds to the “living creature” which “had the likeness of a man.” How closely any individual resembles a specific type depends on their intellectual and moral rank. Physiognomy, however, according to the *Secret of Secrets*, “does not consist in the external features, but in the traits which are mysteriously drawn within us. The features of the face vary,

<summary>This page examines a fragment of the treatise "The Secret of Secrets," which details a Kabbalistic system of physiognomy based on the four creatures of Ezekiel's vision. This system suggests that a person's physical features are determined by their inner moral and intellectual status rather than external biology.</summary>

The spirit alone produces all those physiognomical peculiarities that are known to the wise, and it is through the spirit alone that the features possess meaning. When spirits and souls pass out of Eden <note>that is, the Supreme Wisdom</note> they possess a certain form which is afterwards reflected in the face." M. The "Secret of Secrets" also claims that every feature in a given countenance indicates to those who can read them whether it is possible or not for the possessor to be initiated into divine mysteries.<note>In the writings of the Gaon R. They recur in the supplements of the <term>Zohar</term>.</note> It is, perhaps, unnecessary to say that the fragment does not disclose the rules which governed the sages in their discernment, so that the <term>Kabalah</term> is not likely to be of much practical use to the few occultists who may be inclined to include physiognomy within the charmed circle of the secret wisdom.

### E. THE DISCOURSE OF THE YOUNG MAN

The short history which has passed under this name will be found in the Cremona edition of the <term>Zohar</term>, Part II, comprised in a few columns, 91 etc.

and following, which follow shortly after the "Book of Concealment." It is the childish account of an impossible or certainly an extremely uninteresting rabbinical prodigy, the son of R. Hammnuna, who lived at the time in question with his widowed mother in a certain village. One day two disciples of R. Simeon ben Jochai, namely, R. Isaac and R. Judah, passed through this village on a journey and paid a visit to the widow. When her son returned from school, she wished to present him to the rabbis to receive their blessing, but he declined to approach them, in the unfriendly manner of such prodigies. The reason assigned by the narrative is that he discerned by the odor of their garments that they had not recited the necessary "Hear, O Israel!" in honor of the unity of God. He did not, however, refuse to converse at the table, delivering various discourses (1) On the symbolism of washing the hands, a ritual of some mystery, because it is written, *So they shall wash their hands and their feet, that they die not* (Exodus 30:21), that is, Aaron and his sons, when entering the tabernacle of the congregation; (2) On grace before meals; (3) On the <term>Shekinah</term>; (4) On the utterance of Jacob, "*The Angel which redeemed me from all evil, bless the lads*" (Genesis 48:16); and on other matters. The cautious critic will not be prepared to deny that the invention of this story was within the capacity of R. Moses de Leon. However, the discourses convinced the disciples of Simeon ben Jochai that such a precocious youth could not be the child of human parents, and the great light of Kabalism, when the remarks were repeated to him, agreed with this opinion.

<meta>This page continues the textual analysis of the Zohar, moving from specific fragments like "The Discourse of the Young Man" to a broader discussion of the Zohar's editorial history and its variants in the Mantua and Cremona editions.</meta>

<page-num>279</page-num>
<header>The Written Word of Kabalism</header>

Our analysis of the Zohar, viewed as a literary document, has reached its limit; this example of the material not included in the Mantua version must serve to represent the whole. Although he considered the Mantua edition to be the "corrected codex" (*Codex correctus*), Rosenroth gathered all the tracts and fragments included in the Cremona edition when he produced his own careful volume. However, we must remember that the Zohar likely grew through the work of transcribers and commentators during the nearly three hundred years that passed between its first appearance and the date it was first printed. We have no way of knowing how much of it was actually present in the original writing of Moses de Leon. The suspicion that has surrounded it can be partly explained by its frequent citation of unknown works that have been thought fictitious; but the Zohar was edited before the persecution of the Jews began under the cruel edict of Ferdinand and Isabella, and many documents then in Spain may have been destroyed during that violent era. Furthermore, it is impossible to claim that European libraries contain no manuscripts (MSS.) that could justify the excerpts found in the Zohar. The unpublished literature of the Jewish people has been catalogued by various bibliographers, but a critical understanding of its contents cannot be achieved simply by looking at bibliographies. For example, let us look at the passages from a commentary on the Canticle of Canticles <note>Also known as the Song of Solomon.</note>, which is unique—as we have seen—to the Cremona first edition (*editio princeps*). I believe these excerpts have not yet been identified, but there is a manuscript (*MS.*) in the Vatican Library mentioned by Buxtorf

under the very same title, namely, *Midrash Chazeeth.* <note>Among later manuscript (MS) commentaries, also in the Vatican, Bartolocci mentions that of Rabbi Abraham ben Isaac Tze’mach Levi, the physician, and that of Rabbi Immanuel ben Solomon, written toward the end of the fifteenth century.</note> It is the work, as he tells us, of an unknown author, but a consistent tradition assigns it to a Tanaite commentator around 100 A.D. The existence of such a work, of course, proves nothing; but why should a commentary on the Song of Solomon be called *MDRSh ChDzITh*, which is understood as a reference to Proverbs 22:29? I speak subject to correction, but I know of no reason for this except in the quirk of the author, and I am therefore inclined to infer that the same catchword would not have been used by two different writers, but that the editor of the Zohar quoted the alleged Tanaite treatise.

## XI. THE ANCIENT AND LATER SUPPLEMENTS

The sudden public appearance of a work which either has or claims to have remained hidden for several centuries may be expected to lead to the discovery or manufacture of continuations or connections to it. Thus, we have two series of Zoharic writings produced after the Book of Splendour and distinguished as its ancient and later supplements. As productions of this kind multiply, their authenticity does not tend to appear more convincing, and the reader would do well to regard the documents we shall deal with in this section as being without a determined

<keywords>Midrash Chazeeth, Tanaite, Song of Solomon, Zohar, Book of Splendour, Zoharic supplements, Bartolocci, Abraham ben Isaac Tze’mach Levi, Immanuel ben Solomon</keywords>

--- Page 307 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Zoharic supplements, specifically examining the "Ancient Supplements" and their historical publication and content.</meta>

<page-num>281</page-num>
<header>The Written Word of Kabalism</header>

claims. I should add, however, that Kabalists have always ascribed considerable importance and authority to the *Ancient Supplements*, and according to Franck, they have been known for as long as the *Zohar* itself. They contain seventy different explanations of the term *BRAShITh* by Rabbi Simeon ben Jochai, and for this reason, the work is divided into seventy chapters, with eleven further chapters added at the end. It was printed by Jacob ben Naphtali at Mantua in 1557 under the editorship of Immanuele di Benevento and appeared again at Kraków.

Among the notable matters in these *Ancient Supplements*, we find the attribution of the members of the human body to the *Sephiroth*, from which Western practical magic may have later derived its notion of divine and angelic names ruling those members. <note>According to the *Zohar* itself, the upright human figure displays the letters of the *Tetragram* stacked one upon the other. ii. 42a, Mantua.</note> The top of the head and the brain are associated with *Kether*, the brain as a whole with *Chokmah*, the heart with *Binah*, the back and chest are attributed to *Tiphereth*, the arms to *Chesed* and *Geburah*, the legs to *Netzach* and *Hod*, the reproductive organs to *Jesod*, and the feet to *Malkuth*. Later Kabalism recognizes other correspondences, though their arbitrary nature is sometimes hidden by an appearance of methodical precision.

There are better things than this in the supplements to the Zoharic books, and it may well be supposed that some of the seventy ways of interpreting the much-debated word translated as “beginning” in Genesis should be

> "'In the beginning God created.' This is the soul when it emerges from the bosom of its mother and is taught about it. That French school of occultism which is just beginning to recognize in the plays of Shakespeare a veiled scheme of initiation has, it must be admitted, an influential mystic precedent in the biblical exegesis of the *Zohar*, of which the above passage seems to be a very neat instance—arbitrary beyond all words, and yet not without a certain grace of concept.

The distinction between God and His attributes, and hence between God and the *Sephiroth*, which in a sense are His emanated attributes, is insisted upon elsewhere in the Supplements with the help of a striking illustration:

> “Woe to those whose hearts are so hardened, whose eyes so blinded, that they regard God as the totality of His attributes; they are like a madman who would describe the King as the totality of his insignia. Behold, a king wears his insignia only so that he may be known through them, and truly, the King of Kings, the Concealed of all that is hidden, the Cause of all causes, is disguised in a splendid garment only so that He may be known by it, and through it may impart to the dwellers on this earth a conception of His sacred nature.” <note>Supplement, 21.</note>

From what has been quoted above, it will be seen that the *Ancient Supplements* are identical in their teachings to the *Zohar* itself, and some affirm that the original work had existed from time immemorial at Fez in Africa.<note>* Compare the statement, which rests on the authority of the Supplements, that the full publication of the Zohar is reserved for the end of time.</note> We have no means of checking this statement, nor is there any authority for supposing, along with Isaac Myer, that it was brought there by disciples of Rab Hay, the Gaon of the Sages of Chirvan on the Caspian Sea.<note>† “*Philosophy of Ibn Gebirol*,” p. 47.</note> There is, on the other hand, no need to say that hostile critics make use of weak points in the *Ancient Supplements* as if there were no distinction between these and the *Zohar* proper.

In the section on the bibliographical content of the *Book of Splendour*, we have seen what is broadly embraced by the *New Zohar*: namely, a sequel to the “Hidden Commentary,” certain additional supplements, a commentary on the *Canticle of Canticles*, and another on the *Book of Lamentations*. This list conveys no idea of importance, and perhaps it will be unnecessary to say that occultists are for the most part unaware that these tracts are in existence. # BOOK VI

## THE WRITTEN WORD OF KABALISM:

### THIRD PERIOD

->*ARGUMENT*<-

The growth of Kabalistic literature is sketched, first, in the commentaries on the Zohar and, second, in some independent treatises which connect with the general tradition. Two works are chosen for separate consideration: one on the Mysteries of Love, because of its wide diffusion, and one on the application of the Kabalah to Alchemy, because of the importance of its design and the credit which it has obtained in the modern school of occultism.

### I. EXPOSITORS OF THE BOOK OF SPLENDOUR

THE works which are recommended by Rosenroth as assisting toward a better understanding of the Zohar fall under two categories—namely, those which are designed to clarify technical matters and those which may claim to be original expository treatises. In the first are included the "Words of Understanding," which is actually a Zoharic lexicon or vocabulary; the "Gate of the Eyes," which is concerned with the Scriptural passages in the Zohar and Ancient Supplements; and the *Zar Zahab*. The second section contains

the famous “Garden of Pomegranates,” the “Way of Truth,” with its sequel the “Fount of Wisdom,” and the masterly digest of the Zohar itself, entitled the “Vision of the Priest.” Beyond these, there are a few works which may be regarded as extensions or developments of Zoharic doctrine, specifically focusing on the part concerned with spiritual essences. The scope of our inquiry is too simple and elementary for the discussion of technical matters or the claims of lexicons and other collections that deal with them. Except for the extent that they have been used in the *Apparatus* of Rosenroth, they will be available only to—as they concern only—the Hebrew and Aramaic scholar who has a first-hand acquaintance with the Zohar. Therefore, mentioning them here will be sufficient, and we may proceed at once to the consideration of the commentaries and developments to which that work gave rise, and to the names, illustrious in later Kabbalism, which are connected with them.

### A. MOSES OF CORDOVA

Assuming that the Zohar first became known in Spain toward the end of the thirteenth century, there was a gap of two hundred and fifty years, according to the dates fixed by modern scholarship, before any literature followed it. In either case, Moses of Cordova is the first commentator on the *Zohar*; for, assuming that Bartolocci was mistaken and that Basnage reproduced his error, Joseph Gigatella—called the divine Kabbalist and miracle-worker, who lived during the time of Ferdinand and Isabella—was a writer on the *Sephiroth* and connects with the *Sepher Yetzirah* rather than the *Zohar*, though he refers to the Kabbalistic Work of the Chariot.

As his name indicates, Moses of Cordova was a Spaniard, but he traveled to Palestine, and it is conjectured that he was instrumental in founding the Academy of Safed in Upper Galilee, nine miles from Bethsaida. In either case, he was one of its teachers and helped to make it illustrious, for he was regarded by his fellow theosophists as the greatest light of Kabbalism since Simeon ben Jochai. Franck says that he adhered to the real significance of the original monuments of Kabbalism, but, although this appears worthy of praise, he seems to complain that Rabbi Moses lacked originality. However this may be, the work by which he is known is of high authority in <term>Kabalism</term>. It is entitled the "<term>Garden of Pomegranates</term>" (*<term>Pardes Rimmonim</term>*), referring to the verse in the Canticle of Canticles, 4:13: "Your plants are an orchard of pomegranates." <term>Basnage</term> says that, in the manner of <term>Kabalists</term>, he discovers whatever he pleases in that single sentence.<note>* *History of the Jews*, book 7, chapter 24, vol. 5, p. 1943.</note> The pomegranate, with its innumerable seeds, is a favorite object for symbolism, and the garden, orchard, or paradise has deep lessons for all mysticism. Here, in a general sense, it is the treasury of Scriptural meanings; and as the word by which it is described has four consonants, these meanings are classified into four: <term>PRDS</term>. The P signifies the literal sense, R the mystic sense, D the enigmatic sense, and S the secret and concealed sense.

Dwelling upon these involved meanings, as may be well imagined, the "<term>Garden of Pomegranates</term>" is an obscure and difficult treatise, and the attempt made by <term>Rosenroth</term> to dismember it for the purpose of his *Apparatus*, while it gives no idea of its contents, creates a lively image of its complexity. The attribution of the letters of the <term>Tetragrammaton</term> to the <term>Sephiroth</term>, the mystic meaning of words deprived of their context, the names applied to the <term>Sephiroth</term>, their superincession and their union with <term>Ain-Soph</term>, the mystery of the Throne and the <term>Shekinah</term>, primeval <term>Tohu</term> and <term>Bohu</term>, and the unknown darkness—these are specimens here and there of the subject matter. There is, therefore, no need to say that a special tract in the "<term>Garden of Pomegranates</term>"<note>Namely, Tract 31, translated in the *Kabbala Denudata*, *Apparatus in Librum Sohar*, part two, i. 100 and following.</note> is dedicated to the subject of the soul. It discusses the region from which it emanates, its purpose in the world, the benefit of its creation, its union with matter, its superiority over the angels, its chief divisions and their relationship to one another, the <term>Sephiroth</term> to which they are referred, the places they go after death, the absence of one or both of the higher divisions in many individuals, and the good and evil angels accompanying each human being. 

The tract also devotes a very curious chapter to the <term>simulacrum</term> which presides at generation—a phantasmal image of humanity which descends on the male head *cum copula maritalis exercetur inferius* <note>Latin: "when marital intercourse is performed below."</note>. It is affirmed to be sent from the Lord, and no procreation can take place without its presence. It is not, of course, visible, yet it might be seen if permission were given to the eye. This phantom or *imago* is prepared for each man before he enters the world, and he grows in its likeness. With the Israelites, this <term>simulacrum</term> is holy, and it comes to them from the Holy Place. To those of another religion, it descends from the side of impurity, and therefore the chosen people must not mingle their <term>simulacrum</term> with that of the Gentile.<note>This concept rests on the authority of the <term>Zohar</term>, which states that it is an emanation of the celestial form of each man, that is, <term>Jechidah</term>. — Mantua edition, iii. 107.</note>

--- Page 318 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Moses of Cordovero’s teachings, specifically regarding the mystical belief that good deeds manifest as spiritual garments in the afterlife, before transitioning to a biographical account of the influential Kabalist Isaac Luria.</meta>

Another curious speculation is based on a text of the *Zohar* which says that the good works performed by a person in this world become valuable garments for him in the world to come. This was a poetic sentiment which had to be methodized and taken literally, inevitably, by a later Kabalist. When a man who has performed many good works finally strays from righteousness and is lost, what becomes of his earlier works? Though the sinner may perish, they, says Rabbi Moses, remain. If, therefore, there is a just man walking in the ways of the Supernal King, yet lacking something of his garments, God will supply the deficiency from the good works of the wicked person. Preference is given to those who, having died in their youth, were unable to fulfill all the commandments of the Law.

### B. ISAAC DE LORIA

Regarding this Kabalist, Bartolocci and Basnage have very little to tell us, and it goes without saying that he is ignored by writers like Graetz. He is attributed by Basnage to the seventeenth century* and by Franck, as already seen, to the century that preceded it. He has been regarded as the greatest rabbinical doctor of his period in Germany. However, he died at Sapheth <note>Modern Safed</note> or Separth, having published nothing himself, though some doubtful treatises are attributed to him. The general authenticity of the large body of his doctrines collected by his disciple, Rabbi Hayyim Vital, has not, however, been challenged, and Franck bases his hostile judgment of Loria upon them.

It is certain, however, that Loria did innovate or extend, and that this is also his claim to interest. He is not a mere echo or reflection, and he makes for good reading because he is a wild fantasist. Rosenroth calls him the eagle of the Kabbalists. It is, of course, impossible to say how far his scribe and disciple, Rabbi Hayyim, may have further developed his ideas and elaborated on his fantasies. The vast treasury which represents both seems never to have been printed, except in so far as it is given in the *Kabbala Denudata*, where the excerpts, embodying entire treatises, fill some three hundred quarto pages of close print. III. The “Book of the Revolutions of Souls,”* forming the first tract in the fifth volume of the collection, seems to have been even larger than the *Zohar* itself—in fact, almost the "camel’s load" mentioned in the legend. A portion of this tract seems to have been printed, or perhaps another under a similar title and attribution—namely, *De percussione Sepulchri* (On the Beating of the Grave)—at Venice in 1620, together with *De Precibus* (On Prayers), recalling another subject treated in the collection.

The *Liber Drushim* is a metaphysical introduction to the Kabbalah which discusses a variety of subtle and abstruse questions much in the manner of scholastic philosophy. There is no doubt that Isaac de Loria would have shed a great light of reasoning at Salamanca had he been a Christian Doctor instead of a Jewish Rabbi. His first point, as he tells us, is one over which the Kabbalists, both late and early, had already exhausted themselves: for what reason were the worlds created and was their creation necessary? Assuredly, from the period of the "Angel of the Schools," the halls of Salamanca, of Padua, of Louvain, and the other seats of scholastic learning had echoed with this debate. Perhaps the Kabbalists owed something to the Scholastics, or perhaps they both drew from one another. In the early centuries of Christianity, the so-called Areopagite offers curious points of contact with the system of Sephirotic emanation in the angelic world, and the Wisdom of the Exile was surrounded on all sides by the great debate of Christian speculation. It would

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Isaac de Loria’s metaphysical views, specifically concerning the necessity of creation in relation to the names of God, following the comparison between Kabbalistic and Scholastic philosophy.</meta>

# The Written Word of Kabalism

be interesting to distinguish the extent of this influence and to determine whether the sounding-line of Kabbalism reached lower depths than the Scholastics, but I doubt whether the length of the present volume would be sufficient for this one investigation. Therefore, let me point out the answer provided by Isaac de Loria, and perhaps some student browsing through scholastic volumes will find illuminating parallels in the Scholastic philosophers.* The answer is that God cannot lack perfection in all the works and names of His magnificence, His excellence, and His glory; but unless those works were brought from potentiality into actuality, they could not have been called perfect, regarding either the works or the names themselves. The name *Tetragrammaton* signifies perpetual existence—past, present, and future—in the state of creation before the actual creation, and thereafter in the unchanging nature of things. But if the worlds had not been created, along with everything in them, the name could not have represented the continuity of existence at every moment in time, and *Tetragrammaton* would have been an empty name. How very strange is the logical flaw in this reasoning, which attributes to a name of the Deity an existence independent of the intelligent creatures who established the term! However, we would probably find many similar logical flaws among the Scholastic reasoners, if anyone were available to unearth them. Similarly, the name *Adonai*, or the Lord, implies the idea of ministers or servants, and if there were no servants, God could not be called by this name. But after the creation of the worlds and the production of the

divine works from potentiality into fulfillment, God has completed His perfection in every operation of His powers and in all His names without any exception.

The next point discussed by the *Liber Drushim* is why the world was created at the specific time and moment it was, and not at an earlier or, for that matter, a later epoch. The answer is that the supreme and most excellent Light is infinite, exceeding comprehension and speculation, and that its hidden foundation is far beyond all understanding. Before anything was produced by emanation from it, there was no time or beginning within it. This is the solution to the difficulty offered by all standard theology, and it could have no appearance of novelty at the late period of Isaac Loria. It may be stated in a general way that when the Kabbalists touch the common ground of speculation, they seldom surpass their era in profundity or subtlety; I might also add they do not surpass it in the adequacy of their views, though philosophical completeness was not, of course, to be expected from any side.

But it is not often, nor is it for long, that works like the *Liber Drushim* stay limited to the common ground of speculation. In this instance, the Kabbalist moves quickly into the transcendental region of the *Sephiroth* and the manner of their emanation—another question, as he tells us, which has embroiled all Kabbalists in controversy. Do they proceed from one another in a simple successive series, or is their emanation arranged in columns? There is authority for both views and also for a third, which represents them as a series of homo-

These questions, says Rabbi Isaac, are hard and difficult to resolve, but he offers a solution on the authority of the Zohar: namely, that before the order of things was established, they were arranged one above the other, but after that time they were in three pillars—those of Mercy and Severity, with the central column of which *Kether* is the summit and *Malchuth* the base.

In subsequent chapters, the *Sephiroth* are considered under a dual aspect: namely, regarding the portion of the Divine Light contained in each and regarding the containing vessel. These are again distinguished into a surrounding and an inward Light, and an external and internal vessel. The existence of many worlds prior to the Sephirotic emanations is affirmed, following in this—as we have seen—both Talmudic and Zoharic tradition. Finally, several classifications of the *Sephiroth* are considered in the last chapter.

The study of the *Liber Drushim* may be especially recommended to those occultists who have been taught to regard the Kabalah as a doctrine of certainty, whereas it is largely empirical; its leading theorems give rise to as much dispute regarding their proper meaning as the principles of any other speculative philosophy.

The commentary on the “Book of Concealment,” as might be expected, is written somewhat on the *lucus a non lucendo* <note>A Latin idiom referring to an illogical explanation; literally, "a grove [is so named] from its not being light."</note> principle. It does not yield itself readily to an analysis of contents, as it takes various paragraphs of the text and explains their meaning consecutively, with the help of the *Idra Rabba* and the *Idra Zuta*. <meta>This page continues the analysis of Isaac Luria’s commentary on the "Book of Concealment" (*Siphra Dzeniutha*), specifically discussing the symbolism of the balance, the gendered pillars of the Sephiroth, and the nature of the Holy Ancient One.</meta>

# The Doctrine and Literature of the Kabalah

The treatise is explained by Proverbs 25:2: "It is the glory of God to conceal a thing," and *Ibid.* 11:2: "With the lowly is wisdom." The second reference explains why it is called both the Book of Concealment and the Book of Modesty. On the authority of the Zohar, section *Pekude*, the balance symbol—which has made this treatise so famous in Kabalism—is explained as representing the Male and Female principles; this follows from the developments in the "Lesser Holy Synod." The male denotes Mercy, the right-hand pillar of the *Sephiroth*, and the female denotes Severity, the pillar on the left hand. These principles are called the Father and the Mother, and in the Hebrew alphabet, they refer to *Jod* and *Nun*. The Father is perfect love, and the Mother is perfect severity. The latter had seven sons—namely, the Edomite kings—who had no foundation in the Holy Ancient One. These are empty lights dispelled by the source of lights concealed within the Mother. Male and female are conformations of the Holy Ancient One, corresponding to *Kether*, and are represented mystically by three heads signifying: *a*, the Unmanifested Wisdom, which is so withdrawn that it is as if it did not exist, in contrast to that which is manifested in the thirty-two paths; *b*, the Supreme Crown, which is the Holy Ancient One; and *c*, the Head which neither knows nor is known, namely, the *Ain Soph*. Thus, on one side of *Kether* is *Chokmah*, and on the other is the *latens Deitas* <note>Hidden Deity</note>, and *Chokmah*, or Wisdom, is the Father, while the Mother is the expansion of Understanding, *i.e.*, *Binah*.

He concludes that the summary of the entire mystery is that man in his prayers should focus his mind upon the foundation of all foundations, so that he may draw to himself a certain influence and blessing from the depths of that source. In this way, the obscure points of Kabalism are at times redeemed by the simplicity of the lesson extracted from them.

"The Book of the Revolutions of Souls" is undoubtedly a more fascinating treatise than an obscure explanation of such an obscure work as the *Sepher Dzenioutha*, but it is also difficult to explain in a small space because the system it develops is highly complex, even for a Kabalistic work. The greatest importance has been attached to it by occultists like Éliphas Lévi, who made no distinction between Zoharic and later doctrine. It will, therefore, be useful to see what is really involved in the famous treatise by Isaac de Loria.

The basis of its system is the doctrine of the "Book of Concealment" and its explanatory synods regarding the seven Edomite kings who came forth and passed away prior to the creation of the present universe. In these kings, there was good as well as evil, and a separation was therefore made; that which was good was used for the substance of the four Kabalistic worlds as they are now formed. Each of these worlds, according to Isaac de Loria, has its *Macroprosopus*, Supernal Father, Supernal Mother,

A similar origin is attributed to souls, and they are disposed similarly in the four worlds, some corresponding to the Bride, some to *Microprospus*, some to the Supernal Father, some to the Supernal Mother, and some again to the *Microprospus* in the world of *Assiah*. The totality of these souls constitutes the Psyche in *Assiah*, which, in reference to the supernal personalities of that world, therefore has five parts: the Psyche in the Psyche, or *Nephesh* of *Assiah*, the *mundus factivus* <note>The world of action or making.</note>; the medial spirit, or *Ruach* of the Psyche *factiva*; the *mens* <note>The mind.</note>, or *Neshamah*; the *vitalitas* <note>The vitality.</note>, or *Chaiah*; and the *singularitas* <note>The singularity.</note>, individuality, or *Jechidah*, all belonging to the Psyche *factiva*, or *Nephesh* of *Assiah*. There is a similar distribution through the three superior worlds, *Ruach* and its five-fold division being referred to *Yetzirah*, *Neshamah* to *Briah*, *Chaiah* to *Atziluth*, and *Jechida*, possibly to the world of unmanifest Deity which is beyond *Atziluth*. Loria’s system proceeds, however, upon a five-fold division of only four principles. Each of the five divisions is again attributed as follows in the Sephirotic scheme:

->I. *Nephesh* to *Malkuth*, the Kingdom, *i.e.*, the Bride.<-
->II. *Ruach* to the *Sephiroth* of *Microprospus*.<-
->III. *Neshamah* to the Mother, *i.e.*, *Binah*.<-
->IV. *Chaiah* to the Father, *i.e.*, *Chokmah*.<-
->V. *Jechidah* to *Kether*, *i.e.*, the Crown.<-

All these souls were contained in the original Adam at the time he was formed, some corresponding to the head, others to the eyes, and so with all the members. Now these souls are those of the Israelites, who are the *gens unica in terram*. We must look elsewhere for the origin of the nations of the world. The dross—the evil and rejected parts of the Edomite kings—are the *cortices* or shells which compose the adverse Adam Belial. But when the first parents partook of the forbidden fruit, their fall confounded the good with the evil of the *cortices*: that of Adam with the male shells of Samaël or Adam Belial, and that of Eve with the evil of his bride Lilith, the filth of the serpent; for the serpent had intercourse with Eve according to Isaac de Loria, which does not seem to be the consistent doctrine of the Zohar. It was after this fall that the nations of the world were produced from the shells. This is the doctrine which occultists accept by implication when they speak, as they do speak, of the connection between later Kabalism and the secret traditions on which their devotion is fixed. To put the position concisely, the souls of the Israelites are distributed in the members of the original Adam, regarded in his mystical extension through the four worlds, and the souls of the Gentiles in the members of Adam Belial. Liberation from the filth and venom of the serpent is by generation and death only, whereby the good is separated from the evil, until all the nations of the world shall have

been brought forth from evil, and the Israelites from the good kind.

Since the time when good and evil were thus mixed, two things have been necessary: (1) that the good should be separated from the evil; and (2) that the portion of good should be restored. The first is achieved by observing the prohibitive commandments of the law, and the second by observing the affirmative ones. Both classes must be fulfilled in their entirety—in thought, word, and deed—by every soul, whose revolutions must therefore continue until the whole law has been fulfilled. This law must also be studied in each of its four senses; failing this, the revolutions of the deficient soul will be further prolonged. This system seems to apply exclusively to the Israelites, as the nations of the world can only be destined to return to where they came from, and Adam Belial is obviously not subject to the law.

However, the system is subject to a certain moderation, as actual revolution is sometimes replaced by the *status embryonatus*. Revolution is the entry of a soul into the body of an infant at birth to experience the pain and trials prepared for that body. The alternative condition is the entry of a soul into the body of a grown man who must be at least thirty years old*—*that is*, when he is required to fulfill the commandments. The *status embryonatus* is entered either (a) because the soul in question has something to fulfill that was neglected in its previous revolution; or (b) for the benefit of the man who is

Revolution occurs: (1) for the cleansing of sin; (2) for the fulfillment of a neglected precept; (3) for leading others into the right way, in which case the returning soul is perfect in justice; (4) to receive the true spouse who was not deserved by the soul in the previous revolution. Four souls may revolve in one body, but no more; meanwhile, the *status embryonatus* may associate three alien souls with a single man, but again, no more. The object of all revolutions and all Kabbalistic embryology is the return of the Israelites to the stature of the first Adam, as everyone was involved in his fall since he encompassed all.

Such is the Kabbalistic doctrine of revolution according to Isaac de Loria. It is not pure Zoharic doctrine, nor is it any scheme of reincarnation peculiar to any school of occultism today. Insofar as it differs from the Zohar, it would be unreasonable to regard it as a clearer light of an old tradition; it is very interesting and very curious, yet it is aptly described as a reverie, written by Rabbi Chaim Vital from the mind of Isaac de Loria, and perhaps owing something to the scribe.

> "Before the emanations issued forth and existing things were created, the supreme light was infinitely extended. ### D.—ABRAHAM COHEN IRIRA

This Portuguese Jew was another follower of the school of de Loria, but influenced by the Platonic philosophy, which he sought to harmonize with Kabbalism in his “Gate of the Heavens,”<gloss>* Kabbala Denudata, Apparatus . . . part three, vol. 1.</gloss> as we have already seen. His other treatise is *Beth Elohim*, the “House of God,” containing three dissertations explaining the doctrines of Loria, but directly founded upon and citing at considerable length the pneumatological portions of the “Faithful Shepherd,” the *Pekude* section in the Zohar, and the “Ancient Supplements” of that work.<gloss>† Ibid. vol. 2, part three, treatise 1, p. 188 etc.</gloss> So much space has been given to Kabbalistic psychology that it will be permissible to conclude with this writer in a few words. The House of God has been inexplicably included by some occult writers* among the books that constitute the Zohar, but it is simply a commentary or expansion—of considerable importance in its own sphere—yet neither possessing nor claiming any pretense of antiquity.

### E.—Rabbi Issachar ben Napthali

This expositor of Kabbalism seems to have been a contemporary of Loria and, like him, was German. His chief work, the “Vision of the Priest,” was printed at Cracow in 1559.† It is a synopsis of the entire Zohar or, more properly, a methodical analysis of its contents, organized under a number of titles, each of which is subdivided according to the books of Moses. ### II. THE BOOK OF PURIFYING FIRE

When a given order of mystic symbolism, possessing distinct objects and a sphere of application more or less defined, is applied to the purposes of another order, we may expect to derive some curious results from the analogy thus established if we can come to understand the method. However, as I have already indicated, this interpenetration of typology is usually somewhat dazing in its results. The treatise entitled *Æsh Metzareph*, which means Purifying Fire, is an instance of the application of Kabbalistic apparatus to the purposes of alchemy and is, as far as I am aware, the sole instance of its kind. In this connection, we shall, however, do well to remember that Hermetic and Kabbalistic philosophy are ascribed by the majority of authorities in occultism to a common source,<note>* Thus Thomas Vaughan (Eugenius Philalethes), see Book 7, § 11, affirms in his *Magia Adamica* that the learning of the Jews, that is, their Kabbalah, was chemical, and that Flamel's "Book of Abraham the Jew" is the best proof of it. See A. E. Waite, "Magical Writings of Thomas Vaughan," London, 1888, p. 112.</note> while the rabbinical influence in alchemy is well illustrated by such legends as that

It is true that a work titled “The Philosophical Stone” is attributed to Saadiah by Moses Botrel, but we know it only from a single quotation, and we cannot say whether or not it concerns metallic transmutation. A few alchemical allusions can be found in the Zohar, which recognizes the existence of an archetypal gold and regards metals generally as composite substances. But these references are almost less than incidental, and it goes without saying that there is no serious occult chemistry in that great theosophical storehouse.

The treatise on *Purifying Fire* is written in Aramaic Chaldee, which is the language of the Talmud and the Zohar. It was used so extensively by Rosenroth in his *Lexicon* that nearly the whole work is found translated into Latin in the pages of the *Kabbala Denudata*.<note>I make this statement on Dr. Westcott’s authority, but no one has seen the original since Rosenroth.</note> It was reconstructed from this source in the early part of the eighteenth century by an occultist calling himself "A Lover of Philalethes," who translated it into an English version.<note>*Aesh Metzareph*, or Purifying Fire. A Chemico-Kabbalistic treatise collected from the *Kabbala Denudata* of Knorr von Rosenroth, London, 1714.</note> In the year 1894, this translation was included in a series of Hermetic reprints edited by Dr. Wynn Westcott.<note>*Collectanea Hermetica*, vol. iv. London, 1894.</note> The preface and notes that accompany this edition appear under the pseudonym *Sapere Aude* and are of considerable value. No information is, however, given as to the

<summary>This page discusses the sparse alchemical references in the Zohar and the history of the "Aesh Metzareph" (Purifying Fire), a Kabbalistic-alchemical text preserved by Rosenroth and later edited by Westcott.</summary>

Chaldean original, and the gaps occurring in the reconstruction have not been filled.

There is no evidence available by which we can determine with any degree of precision the period in which this treatise was composed.<note>It is mentioned by Claverus in a treatise titled *Observations on the Most Useful Things in the World*, 1706, p. 72 *et seq*. He provides an account of it intended to show that the Jews adapted the Kabbalistic *Sephiroth* to *Chrysopoeia*—that is, the art of alchemy. He also states that the Jews hold the *Aesh Metzareph* in such high regard that they consider no Christian worthy of reading it.</note> It is naturally later than the publication of the Zohar, which it quotes frequently. It is also later than the *Garden of Pomegranates* by Rabbi Moses of Cordova—a treatise possibly belonging to the middle of the sixteenth century—which it also quotes. It borrows processes from Rabbi Mordechai, a Kabbalistic alchemist whose dates I have been unable to discover,<note>A number of writers, mostly Kabbalistic, are categorized under this name in the bibliography of Bartolocci.</note> and it refers to the forged Latin treatises of Geber. We may therefore conclude that it does not precede Rosenroth by any significant length of time and may be tentatively placed at the beginning of the seventeenth century. Finally, it contains expressions which are common to most Latin alchemists and were derived by them from the Greeks, such as: "He who is wise may correct natures." It does not, therefore, possess the interest or importance that would belong to a chemico-Kabbalistic treatise from the Zohar period, and I have not been able to find any evidence regarding the authority attributed to it.

In the supplement to his *Key of the Great Mysteries*, Éliphas Lévi provides, first of all, what he calls

the fragments of the *Æsh Metzareph*, calling it one of the most important books of Hermetic science; secondly, the additions to its eight chapters, being further fragments which he claims to have discovered; thirdly, the hypothetical restoration of the original.<note>See *Information on the Great Mysteries of Hermetic Philosophy*, p. 405 and following.</note> The methods of the brilliant French occultist are well illustrated in each case. It should be observed that the fragments are designed to show the difficulties and the exhaustion which his research has spared his readers, and to illustrate the conscientious and serious nature of his studies. Upon examination, the first section proves not to be the fragments of the *Æsh Metzareph*, but a loose paraphrase that has very little correspondence with the original. The second section, which is also a paraphrase, is substantially found in Rosenroth and the English version. The hypothetical reconstruction serves only to show that Lévi, like everyone else, never saw the original (which some have said still exists), or he would not have so misapplied his ingenuity. Lastly, he attributes the work to Rabbi Abraham of the Flamel legend, thereby granting it an antiquity that is contradicted by its own references.<note>Firstly, in the title, which reads, *Fragments of the Ash Mézareph of Abraham the Jew*; secondly, in the hypothetical reconstruction of the treatise which connects it with the mystic book owned by Flamel. The *Æsh Metzareph* is entirely anonymous and is included as such in the bibliography of Wolf, volume 2, page 1265.</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the *Æsh Metzareph*, transitioning from a critique of modern interpretations to a description of the work's internal symbolic structure.</meta>

between two orders of mystic symbolism. It is described by its latest editor as "suggestive rather than explanatory," and he adds that its alchemical processes are not set out "in such a way that they could be carried out by a neophyte; any attempt to do so would reveal that something vital was missing at one stage or another." The statement is true of all alchemical literature, and the *Æsh Metzareph* has the common difficulties of purely Hermetic books, further complicated by the system of Gematria and the Sephirotic correspondences of the metals.

The treatise is mainly based on the correspondences indicated here, and it is in this sense that the mysteries of alchemical transmutation are said to "do not differ from the superior mysteries of the Kabalah." The *Sephiroth* of the material world are identical to those of the archetypal, and they are the same in the mineral kingdom. The alchemical root of the metals corresponds to *Kether*; all metals originate from there, just as the other *Sephiroth* are all emanations from the Crown. The Crown is concealed; so also is the metallic root. Lead is referred to *Chokmah*, which proceeds immediately from *Kether*, just as Saturn does from the metallic root. Tin has the place of *Binah*, Silver that of *Chesed*, and these three are the white metallic natures. Among the red, Gold is in correspondence with *Geburah*, Iron with *Tiphereth*, and the hermaphroditic Brass with *Netzach* and *Hod*. Quicksilver is referred to *Jesod*, and "the true Medicine of Metals" to *Malkuth*. The attribution will appear a little conventional in some cases, and it depends on a curious use of Scriptural

It emphasizes that these alchemical processes are symbolic and inseparable from Kabbalistic mysteries.</summary>

authority. However, the writer adds: "If anyone has placed these things in another order, I shall not argue with him, because all systems tend toward the one truth." To illustrate this, he gives another set of attributions as follows:

> "The three Supernals," namely, *Kether*, *Chokmah*, and *Binah*, are the three fountains of metallic things. "The thick water"—that is, Mercury—"is *Kether*, Salt is *Chokmah*, and Sulphur is *Binah*." These are the three principles of the alchemists. This attribution, the treatise says, is "for known reasons." *Chesed*, *Geburah*, and *Tiphereth* correspond, as before, to Silver, Gold, and Iron; *Netzach* is Tin, *Hod* is Copper, *Jesod* is Lead, while *Malkuth* is the "Metallic Woman," the "Luna of the Wise," and the "Field into which the seeds of secret minerals ought to be cast—that is, the Water of Gold." The attribution in either case has a hidden sense which "no tongue may be permitted to speak." The superficial explanations offered here and there should not, therefore, be taken seriously—for example, that Silver is referred to *Chesed* "on account of its whiteness, which denotes Mercy and Pity." The *Kamea* or Magical Squares of the planets are given in connection with each of the seven metals, but not always correctly.

The unique character of the work is well illustrated in the third chapter, where Daniel’s vision of the beast with ten horns is interpreted alchemically with the help of *gematria*.

## III. THE MYSTERIES OF LOVE

With the sole exception of Abraham Cohen Irira, the succession of Kabalistic writers whom we have reviewed so far never lowered themselves to use a common language. To that exception, we must now add the case of Rabbi Judah, son of Isaac Abravanel, better known by the name Leo the Hebrew. I must confess that there is no strictly necessary reason for including him in an account of the major documents of Kabalism; he lived very late, having been born in the kingdom of Castile shortly after the middle of the fifteenth century, and it is even stated that he broke away from all Jewish tradition by becoming a Christian. At the same time, there are two points by which he forces himself upon our attention; first, he is a favorite subject of reference for occult writers and therefore cannot be overlooked in a work that deals specifically with the occult interest in Kabalism; second, his dialogues on love have been more popular than any Kabalistic treatise. According to the most reliable opinion, they were originally written in Italian and first appeared in Rome in the year 1535. They were reprinted in Venice in 1541. Then they were translated into Latin by Sarrazin and published, according to Wolf, in 1564 in Vienna. This version, which has been praised for its elegance, was next included by Pistorius in his famous *Artis Cabalisticæ Scriptores* <note>Writers of the Kabbalistic Art</note>, Basel, 1587. They were translated twice into Spanish; the first version, and the only one I am familiar with,

<summary>This page introduces Leo the Hebrew (Rabbi Judah Abravanel) and his influential dialogues on love, noting his unique use of the vernacular and his popularity among occultists. It details the publishing history of his work across Italian, Latin, and Spanish editions.</summary>
<keywords>Leo the Hebrew, Judah Abravanel, Kabalism, Artis Cabalisticæ Scriptores, Pistorius, Abraham Cohen Irira, dialogues on love, Castile</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the bibliographical history of Leo the Hebrew (Judah Abravanel), detailing the various translations of his works and providing a biographical account of his life and the debate surrounding his religious conversion.</meta>

<page-num>315</page-num>

being that of Juan Costa in 1584. Lastly, there have been at least three French translations: specifically, by Pontus de Thiard in 1580; the Seigneur du Parc Champerrois; and Alexander Weill in 1875.

Although it is said that he wrote in Italian, Abravanel was Spanish by birth but was driven from his native country by the edict of Ferdinand and Isabella. This occurred in 1492. His first refuge was Naples, where he entered the king's service; however, the king died and his kingdom fell into the hands of Charles VIII, after which the Spanish Jew became a wanderer once more. Some say that he retired to Sicily, then to Corfu and Ponilles, and finally to Venice, where he died in 1508. Others report that he settled in Genoa and practiced medicine there with distinction for a long period. There is also significant confusion regarding the change, whether real or alleged, in his religious opinions. Basnage states that he was a man of a mild nature who socialized familiarly with Christians but denounced them in his writings, especially the priests and the Pope.<note>History of the Jews, book 7, vol. 5, pp. 1898, 1899.</note> Pistorius, on the other hand, portrays him as a converted Jew.<note>Drach says the same in his notice on the Kabbalism in *Harmony between the Church and the Synagogue*.</note> He is the subject of high praise, based on intimate knowledge, in the bibliography of Bartolocci.<note>*Bibliotheca Rabbinica*, iii. 86. There is no mention of his conversion in the entry, but the original edition of the "Dialogues" describes their author as "of the Hebrew nation and afterwards made a Christian."</note>

It must be admitted that the occult interest in Leo the Hebrew is not easily understood, and it is beyond speculation why he was included by

<meta>This page continues the critique of the "Dialogues of Love" by Leo the Hebrew, following the discussion of his life and religious identity. It examines why the work was included in Kabbalistic collections despite its lack of genuine Jewish theosophical content.</meta>

<page-num>316</page-num>

...Pistorius, in his ambitious and unfinished attempt to gather the notable treatises of Kabbalism. We look in vain for the essential doctrines of Jewish theosophy as we have learned them from the *Zohar*; we look in vain even for the meditations of the school of Luria. In their place, we find the elegant sentimentalism that characterized Italian literature of the period; we are reminded at times of Boccalini, and at others of the declamatory Latin exercises of Palingenius. The framework of the dialogues, if they can be said to possess a framework, belongs to classical mythology; the allusions, the illustrations, and the images are echoes of the Greek and Latin poets. When the philosophical authority is not Aristotle, it is Plato; there is only one direct reference to the Kabbalah in the entire three hundred folio pages which the dialogues occupy in Pistorius, and even then, it is a slight allusion to the successive renewals of the world, which suggests that the author had misunderstood the "restoration" mentioned in the "*Book of Concealment*." In contrast to this, there is not a single trace of Hebrew thought or influence; there is nothing which would lead us to suspect Jewish authorship except for such negative evidence as the equally complete absence of any Christian references. If the work can be said to recall anything outside the frivolous literature of the sixteenth century in Italy, it would be certain Sufi poets adapted for the understanding of Venetian ladies in the days of the Doges. And here, indeed, is the true secret of its popularity. It is not only so pleasing, so educated according to the standards of its period, so correct in its sentiment and expressing so little besides sentiment, and so refined in its

<summary>The author argues that Leo the Hebrew’s "Dialogues of Love" lacks genuine Kabbalistic or Hebrew influence, instead reflecting the classical mythology and sentimentalism of 16th-century Italian literature. Despite its inclusion in Pistorius's collection, the work's content is more akin to the style of Sufi poets or classical philosophers like Plato and Aristotle.</summary>
